Dharma › Theravāda › Visuddhimagga › Anussatikammaṭṭhānaniddesa
Chapter 8 · Anussatikammaṭṭhānaniddesa · Complete

Death, the Body, Breathing, and Peace

This page carries Ru-Yi's translation of the Visuddhimagga's chapter 8, Anussatikammaṭṭhānaniddesa ("The Description of the Meditation Subjects Consisting in Recollection") — recollection of death, mindfulness of the body (with the complete thirty-two-part anatomical contemplation), mindfulness of breathing in its full sixteen-step canonical form, and recollection of peace. By a wide margin the longest chapter in this translation so far, dominated by its exhaustive treatment of mindfulness of breathing.

Status: chapter 8 complete — all four recollections translated in full Translator: Ru-Yi Meditation Center Source: Vism VIII, Anussatikammaṭṭhānaniddesa (VRI/CST numbering) Author: Bhadantācariya Buddhaghosa, 5th c. CE

Translation Method

The Pāli source is the Chaṭṭha Saṅgāyana (Sixth Council) romanized edition maintained by the Vipassana Research Institute (VRI, tipitaka.org), cross-checked against the Pali Text Society's Rhys Davids edition (1920–21) as digitized on GRETIL. The VRI text is used under its stated terms — freely available for non-commercial use, with attribution to the Vipassana Research Institute — while the GRETIL/PTS digitization, which carries its own CC BY-SA licence, is used only to verify readings, never copied.

The English translation is Ru-Yi's own, made directly from the Pāli. No existing English translation of the Visuddhimagga — including Bhikkhu Ñāṇamoli's copyrighted The Path of Purification — is consulted, paraphrased, or used as a guide for wording or structure. Reading notes are grounded only in the text's own internal citations and in the general, uncontested conventions of the Pāli commentarial (aṭṭhakathā) genre — never in a modern scholar's specific interpretation. Each translated passage carries a short vocabulary list for readers learning Pāli alongside the text, continuing the same format established on this site's earlier Visuddhimagga chapters.

What Death Is, and the Right Way to Recollect It

The chapter opens by carefully excluding what recollection of death is not about — not the moment-to-moment "momentary death" of formations, not the figurative "death" of a felled tree, and not even the arahant's final cutting-off of the round of suffering — before defining its true object: the mere cutting-off of the life faculty within a single existence, whether by exhaustion of merit, exhaustion of lifespan, or an untimely accident. The chapter then warns, through four contrasting failure cases, that this recollection can misfire badly if approached without skill, producing grief, gladness, indifference, or terror rather than the clear, steady insight it is meant to cultivate.

Pali Source

Idāni ito anantarāya maraṇassatiyā bhāvanāniddeso anuppatto. Tattha maraṇanti ekabhavapariyāpannassa jīvitindriyassa upacchedo. Yaṃ panetaṃ arahantānaṃ vaṭṭadukkhasamucchedasaṅkhātaṃ samucchedamaraṇaṃ, saṅkhārānaṃ khaṇabhaṅgasaṅkhātaṃ khaṇikamaraṇaṃ, rukkho mato lohaṃ matantiādīsu sammutimaraṇañca, na taṃ idha adhippetaṃ.

Yampi cetaṃ adhippetaṃ, taṃ kālamaraṇaṃ akālamaraṇanti duvidhaṃ hoti. Tattha kālamaraṇaṃ puññakkhayena vā āyukkhayena vā ubhayakkhayena vā hoti. Akālamaraṇaṃ kammupacchedakakammavasena.

Tattha yaṃ vijjamānāyapi āyusantānajanakapaccayasampattiyā kevalaṃ paṭisandhijanakassa kammassa vipakkavipākattā maraṇaṃ hoti, idaṃ puññakkhayena maraṇaṃ nāma. Yaṃ gatikālāhārādisampattiyā abhāvena ajjatanakālapurisānaṃ viya vassasatamattaparimāṇassa āyuno khayavasena maraṇaṃ hoti, idaṃ āyukkhayena maraṇaṃ nāma. Yaṃ pana dūsīmārakalāburājādīnaṃ viya taṅkhaṇaññeva ṭhānācāvanasamatthena kammunā upacchinnasantānānaṃ, purimakammavasena vā satthaharaṇādīhi upakkamehi upacchijjamānasantānānaṃ maraṇaṃ hoti, idaṃ akālamaraṇaṃ nāma. Taṃ sabbampi vuttappakārena jīvitindriyupacchedena saṅgahitaṃ. Iti jīvitindriyupacchedasaṅkhātassa maraṇassa saraṇaṃ maraṇassati.

Taṃ bhāvetukāmena rahogatena paṭisallīnena "maraṇaṃ bhavissati, jīvitindriyaṃ upacchijjissatī"ti vā, "maraṇaṃ maraṇa"nti vā yoniso manasikāro pavattetabbo. Ayoniso pavattayato hi iṭṭhajanamaraṇānussaraṇe soko uppajjati vijātamātuyā piyaputtamaraṇānussaraṇe viya. Aniṭṭhajanamaraṇānussaraṇe pāmojjaṃ uppajjati verīnaṃ verimaraṇānussaraṇe viya. Majjhattajanamaraṇānussaraṇe saṃvego na uppajjati matakaḷevaradassane chavaḍāhakassa viya. Attano maraṇānussaraṇe santāso uppajjati ukkhittāsikaṃ vadhakaṃ disvā bhīrukajātikassa viya. Tadetaṃ sabbampi satisaṃvegañāṇavirahato hoti. Tasmā tattha tattha hatamatasatte oloketvā diṭṭhapubbasampattīnaṃ sattānaṃ matānaṃ maraṇaṃ āvajjetvā satiñca saṃvegañca ñāṇañca yojetvā "maraṇaṃ bhavissatī"tiādinā nayena manasikāro pavattetabbo. Evaṃ pavattento hi yoniso pavatteti, upāyena pavattetīti attho. Evaṃ pavattayatoyeva hi ekaccassa nīvaraṇāni vikkhambhanti, maraṇārammaṇā sati saṇṭhāti, upacārappattameva kammaṭṭhānaṃ hoti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, next after this, comes the exposition of the development of recollection of death. Herein, "death" means the cutting-off of the life faculty comprised within a single existence. As for the arahants' cutting-off, reckoned as the cutting-off of the suffering of the round, that is called "final death"; the momentary dissolution of formations is called "momentary death"; and such usages as "the tree has died" or "the metal has died" are called "conventional death" — this is not what is intended here.

What is intended here is twofold: timely death and untimely death. Herein, timely death occurs through the exhaustion of merit, or the exhaustion of the lifespan, or both together. Untimely death occurs by way of a kamma that cuts off other kamma.

Herein, that death which occurs, even though the conditions productive of a continuing lifespan are still present, simply because the kamma productive of rebirth-linking has finished ripening its result — this is called "death by exhaustion of merit." That death which occurs through the exhaustion of a lifespan measured, for the people of the present age, at a mere hundred years, through the absence of the fulfillment of destiny, era, food, and the rest — this is called "death by exhaustion of the lifespan." That death which occurs, as in the case of Dūsī Māra or King Kalābu, through a kamma capable of dislodging one from a state at that very moment, cutting off one's continuity, or through one's continuity being cut off by such assaults as the taking of a weapon, by force of a former kamma — this is called "untimely death." All of this, in the way described, is included under the cutting-off of the life faculty. Thus, recollection of death is the recollection of death reckoned as the cutting-off of the life faculty.

One desiring to develop this, alone in a secluded place, withdrawn, should make wise attention proceed thus: "death will occur, the life faculty will be cut off," or simply, "death, death." For one making it proceed unwisely, when recollecting the death of a dear person, grief arises, as in a mother who has just given birth recollecting the death of her beloved child; when recollecting the death of a disliked person, gladness arises, as in one recollecting the death of an enemy at the hands of enemies; when recollecting the death of a neutral person, no sense of urgency arises, as in a corpse-burner looking upon a dead body; and when recollecting one's own death, terror arises, as in one of a timid nature who has seen an executioner with drawn sword. All of this, in every case, is devoid of mindfulness, a sense of urgency, and knowledge. Therefore, having looked here and there upon beings slain and dead, and having brought to mind the death of beings, once seen to be possessed of good fortune, who have since died, and having conjoined mindfulness, a sense of urgency, and knowledge, attention should be made to proceed by the method beginning "death will occur." For one proceeding thus proceeds wisely — proceeds, that is, by the right method. For only in one proceeding thus are the hindrances suppressed, mindfulness with death as its object becomes established, and the meditation subject reaches access concentration.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The chapter's opening exclusions are a careful boundary-setting exercise: by first ruling out "final death" (the arahant's liberation from the round), "momentary death" (the constant micro-dissolution the Abhidhamma attributes to every mental and physical event), and merely figurative "death" (a felled tree, cast metal), the text isolates a single, specific, ordinary sense of the word before building an entire meditation practice on it — a reminder that even a word as seemingly unambiguous as "death" needs this kind of precise technical delimitation before it can serve as a meditation object.

The four failure-cases — grief for a loved one, gladness for an enemy, indifference for a stranger, terror for oneself — are a genuinely comprehensive taxonomy of how an untrained mind actually reacts to thoughts of death, sorted by the reactor's relationship to the person who died. What unites all four as failures, the text is explicit, is not the specific emotion produced but the shared absence of mindfulness, urgency, and knowledge — the same event (someone's death) recollected with those three qualities present, rather than absent, is what turns an emotionally reactive habit into an actual meditation subject capable of reaching access concentration.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
samucchedamaraṇa / khaṇikamaraṇa / sammutimaraṇa

final death / momentary death / conventional death — the three senses of "death" explicitly excluded from this recollection's actual object, leaving only the ordinary cutting-off of the life faculty within a single existence.

kālamaraṇa / akālamaraṇa

timely death / untimely death — the twofold classification of the death actually intended here, the first arising from exhausted merit or lifespan, the second from an intervening, kamma-cutting-kamma accident.

saṃvega

a sense of urgency, spiritual dismay — the specific quality, alongside mindfulness and knowledge, whose absence turns an ordinary emotional reaction to death (grief, gladness, indifference, terror) into a genuine meditative failure rather than a workable basis for practice.

The First Way: A Murderer Standing Ready

For meditators who need more structure than the bare method just given, the chapter offers eight specific approaches to recollecting death. The first compares death to an executioner who has been present since the very moment of birth, standing with sword already drawn at one's own neck — illustrated by the images of a mushroom cap forced up through soil by the very act of emerging, a sun that never once pauses in its westward course, and a swift mountain river that never flows backward.

Pali Source

Yassa pana ettāvatā na hoti, tena vadhakapaccupaṭṭhānato, sampattivipattito, upasaṃharaṇato, kāyabahusādhāraṇato, āyudubbalato, animittato, addhānaparicchedato, khaṇaparittatoti imehi aṭṭhahākārehi maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Tattha vadhakapaccupaṭṭhānatoti vadhakassa viya paccupaṭṭhānato. Yathā hi imassa sīsaṃ chindissāmīti asiṃ gahetvā gīvāya cārayamāno vadhako paccupaṭṭhitova hoti, evaṃ maraṇampi paccupaṭṭhitamevāti anussaritabbaṃ. Kasmā? Saha jātiyā āgatato, jīvitaharaṇato ca. Yathā hi ahicchattakamakuḷaṃ matthakena paṃsuṃ gahetvāva uggacchati, evaṃ sattā jarāmaraṇaṃ gahetvāva nibbattanti. Tathā hi nesaṃ paṭisandhicittaṃ uppādānantarameva jaraṃ patvā pabbatasikharato patitasilā viya bhijjati saddhiṃ sampayuttakhandhehi. Evaṃ khaṇikamaraṇaṃ tāva saha jātiyā āgataṃ. Jātassa pana avassaṃ maraṇato idhādhippetamaraṇampi saha jātiyā āgataṃ. Tasmā esa satto jātakālato paṭṭhāya yathā nāma uṭṭhito sūriyo atthābhimukho gacchateva, gatagataṭṭhānato īsakampi na nivattati. Yathā vā nadī pabbateyyā sīghasotā hārahārinī sandateva vattateva īsakampi na nivattati, evaṃ īsakampi anivattamāno maraṇābhimukhova yāti. Tena vuttaṃ —

"Yamekarattiṃ paṭhamaṃ, gabbhe vasati māṇavo;
Abbhuṭṭhitova so yāti, sa gacchaṃ na nivattatī"ti. (jā. 1.15.363)

Evaṃ gacchato cassa gimhābhitattānaṃ kunnadīnaṃ khayo viya, pāto āporasānugatabandhanānaṃ dumapphalānaṃ patanaṃ viya, muggarābhitāḷitānaṃ mattikabhājanānaṃ bhedo viya, sūriyarasmisamphuṭṭhānaṃ ussāvabindūnaṃ viddhaṃsanaṃ viya ca maraṇameva āsannaṃ hoti. Tenāha —

"Accayanti ahorattā, jīvitamuparujjhati;
Āyu khīyati maccānaṃ, kunnadīnaṃva odakaṃ. (saṃ. ni. 1.146)
"Phalānamiva pakkānaṃ, pāto papatato bhayaṃ;
Evaṃ jātāna maccānaṃ, niccaṃ maraṇato bhayaṃ.
"Yathāpi kumbhakārassa, kataṃ mattikabhājanaṃ;
Khuddakañca mahantañca, yaṃ pakkaṃ yañca āmakaṃ;
Sabbaṃ bhedanapariyantaṃ, evaṃ maccāna jīvitaṃ'. (su. ni. 581-582)
"Ussāvova tiṇaggamhi, sūriyuggamanaṃ pati;
Evamāyu manussānaṃ, mā maṃ amma nivārayā"ti. (jā. 1.11.79)

Evaṃ ukkhittāsiko vadhako viya saha jātiyā āgataṃ panetaṃ maraṇaṃ gīvāya asiṃ cārayamāno so vadhako viya jīvitaṃ haratiyeva, na aharitvā nivattati. Tasmā saha jātiyā āgatato, jīvitaharaṇato ca ukkhittāsiko vadhako viya maraṇampi paccupaṭṭhitamevāti evaṃ vadhakapaccupaṭṭhānato maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

But for one for whom this much is not enough, death should be recollected by these eight approaches: as an executioner standing ready, through the ruin of success, by comparison with others, as a body shared by many, through the fragility of life, as without any fixed sign, through the brevity of the span, and through the momentariness of the interval.

Herein, "as an executioner standing ready" means: standing ready like an executioner. For just as an executioner, having taken up a sword and holding it to a man's neck, thinking, "I will cut off this man's head," stands right there, ready — so too death should be recollected as standing ready in just this way. Why? Because it has come together with birth, and because it takes away life. For just as a mushroom cap, in the very act of pushing up, must carry the soil above it upward, so too beings, in the very act of being born, are born carrying aging-and-death along with them. Thus, immediately upon the arising of the rebirth-linking consciousness, that being, having reached aging, breaks apart together with its associated aggregates, like a rock fallen from a mountain peak. In this way, momentary death has come together with birth. But even this recollection's own intended death — the death that must inevitably come to one who is born — has likewise come together with birth. Therefore this being, from the moment of birth, is like the risen sun that goes only toward its setting, never turning back even slightly from wherever it has traveled; or like a swift-flowing mountain river that carries everything along, flowing and coursing on, never turning back even slightly — just so, never turning back even slightly, it goes only toward death. For this reason it was said —

"The young creature that dwells in the womb / for even a single first night — / he goes on, having already set out; / going on, he does not turn back." (Jā. 1.15.363)

And, as he thus goes on, death draws ever nearer to him, like the drying up of small streams scorched by the summer heat, like the falling, at dawn, of fruits attached by the merest thread of moisture, like the shattering of clay pots struck by a mallet, or like the vanishing of dewdrops touched by the sun's rays. For it was said —

"The days and nights go by, / life comes to its end; / the lifespan of mortals is used up, / like the water of small streams. (S.N. 1.146)
"As there is fear for ripened fruits / of falling at dawn, / so for mortals once born / there is always the fear of death.
"Just as, for a potter, / a clay vessel that has been made — / whether small or large, whether fired or unfired — / all alike end in breaking, / so too is the life of mortals. (Sn. 581–582)
"Like dew upon a blade of grass / at the rising of the sun, / such is the life of human beings — / do not hold me back, mother." (Jā. 1.11.79)

Thus, this death, having come together with birth like a murderer with drawn sword, holding that sword to the neck, simply takes life away just as that executioner would — it does not turn back without taking it. Therefore, because it has come together with birth, and because it takes away life, death, like an executioner with drawn sword, should be recollected as standing ready in just this way — thus death should be recollected as an executioner standing ready.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The mushroom simile makes a precise structural claim rather than a merely poetic one: it is not that death happens to follow birth in sequence, as one event following another in time, but that the very mechanics of being born already carry death within them, the way a mushroom cannot push upward without simultaneously displacing the earth above it. Aging-and-death, on this account, is not a separate event scheduled for later — it is built into the structural fact of having been reborn at all, present from the rebirth-linking consciousness's very first moment.

The four similes clustered together at the section's midpoint — drying streams, falling fruit, shattering pottery, vanishing dew — are chosen for a shared feature worth noticing: none of them depict a violent or external cause of ending, only the simple, unforced continuation of a process already underway from the very beginning. Death, in each image, is not something that interrupts life from outside, but the completion of a trajectory life was already following.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ukkhittāsika

one with a drawn/raised sword — ukkhitta (raised, lifted) + asi (sword); the recurring image for death throughout this section, an executioner already poised rather than merely approaching.

ahicchattakamakuḷa

a mushroom cap — ahi (snake) + chattaka (a small umbrella, a mushroom) + makuḷa (a bud, a cap); the image for how death is carried along inseparably with the very act of being born, exactly as soil is displaced upward in the act of the mushroom's own emergence.

gimhābhitatta kunnadī

a small stream scorched by summer heat — gimha (summer) + abhitatta (scorched) + kunnadī (a small river); one of four paired similes for death's steady approach, here evoking a water source visibly diminishing toward its own end.

The Ruin of Success, and Comparison with Others

The second approach observes that success shines only until ruin overtakes it — illustrated by a king who, having once enjoyed the whole earth, dies possessing barely half a small fruit's worth of sovereignty — and that every kind of health, youth, and life is itself simply a prelude to its own ending, sparing no one by caste or rank. The third approach turns to direct comparison: if death has overtaken people of vastly greater fame, merit, strength, psychic power, and wisdom than oneself — named individuals the tradition holds as historically real — how could it possibly spare someone as ordinary as the meditator himself?

Pali Source

Sampattivipattitoti idha sampatti nāma tāvadeva sobhati, yāva naṃ vipatti nābhibhavati, na ca sā sampatti nāma atthi, yā vipattiṃ atikkamma tiṭṭheyya. Tathā hi —

"Sakalaṃ mediniṃ bhutvā, datvā koṭisataṃ sukhī;
Aḍḍhāmalakamattassa, ante issarataṃ gato.
"Teneva dehabandhena, puññamhi khayamāgate;
Maraṇābhimukho sopi, asoko sokamāgato"ti.

Apica sabbaṃ ārogyaṃ byādhipariyosānaṃ, sabbaṃ yobbanaṃ jarāpariyosānaṃ, sabbaṃ jīvitaṃ maraṇapariyosānaṃ, sabboyeva lokasannivāso jātiyā anugato, jarāya anusaṭo, byādhinā abhibhūto, maraṇena abbhāhato. Tenāha —

"Yathāpi selā vipulā, nabhaṃ āhacca pabbatā;
Samantā anupariyeyyuṃ, nippothentā catuddisā.
"Evaṃ jarā ca maccu ca, adhivattanti pāṇine;
Khattiye brāhmaṇe vesse, sudde caṇḍālapukkuse;
Na kiñci parivajjeti, sabbamevābhimaddati.
"Na tattha hatthīnaṃ bhūmi, na rathānaṃ na pattiyā;
Na cāpi mantayuddhena, sakkā jetuṃ dhanena vā"ti. (saṃ. ni. 1.136)

Evaṃ jīvitasampattiyā maraṇavipattipariyosānataṃ vavatthapentena sampattivipattito maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Upasaṃharaṇatoti parehi saddhiṃ attano upasaṃharaṇato. Tattha sattahākārehi upasaṃharaṇato maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ, yasamahattato, puññamahattato, thāmamahattato, iddhimahattato, paññāmahattato, paccekabuddhato, sammāsambuddhatoti. Kathaṃ? Idaṃ maraṇaṃ nāma mahāyasānaṃ mahāparivārānaṃ sampannadhanavāhanānaṃ mahāsammatamandhātumahāsudassana daḷhanemi nimippabhutīnampi upari nirāsaṅkameva patitaṃ, kimaṅgaṃ pana mayhaṃ upari na patissati?

Mahāyasā rājavarā, mahāsammataādayo;
Tepi maccuvasaṃ pattā, mādisesu kathāva kāti.

Evaṃ tāva yasamahattato anussaritabbaṃ. Kathaṃ puññamahattato?

Jotiko jaṭilo uggo, meṇḍako atha puṇṇako;
Ete caññe ca ye loke, mahāpuññāti vissutā;
Sabbe maraṇamāpannā, mādisesu kathāva kāti.

Evaṃ puññamahattato anussaritabbaṃ. Kathaṃ thāmamahattato?

Vāsudevo baladevo, bhīmaseno yudhiṭṭhilo;
Cānuro yo mahāmallo, antakassa vasaṃ gatā.
Evaṃ thāmabalūpetā, iti lokamhi vissutā;
Etepi maraṇaṃ yātā, mādisesu kathāva kāti.

Evaṃ thāmamahattato anussaritabbaṃ. Kathaṃ iddhimahattato?

Pādaṅguṭṭhakamattena, vejayantamakampayi;
Yo nāmiddhimataṃ seṭṭho, dutiyo aggasāvako.
Sopi maccumukhaṃ ghoraṃ, migo sīhamukhaṃ viya;
Paviṭṭho saha iddhīhi, mādisesu kathāva kāti.

Evaṃ iddhimahattato anussaritabbaṃ. Kathaṃ paññāmahattato?

Lokanāthaṃ ṭhapetvāna, ye caññe atthi pāṇino;
Paññāya sāriputtassa, kalaṃ nāgghanti soḷasiṃ.
Evaṃ nāma mahāpañño, paṭhamo aggasāvako;
Maraṇassa vasaṃ patto, mādisesu kathāva kāti.

Evaṃ paññāmahattato anussaritabbaṃ. Kathaṃ paccekabuddhato? Yepi te attano ñāṇavīriyabalena sabbakilesasattunimmathanaṃ katvā paccekabodhiṃ pattā khaggavisāṇakappā sayambhuno, tepi maraṇato na muttā, kuto panāhaṃ muccissāmīti.

Taṃ taṃ nimittamāgamma, vīmaṃsantā mahesayo;
Sayambhuññāṇatejena, ye pattā āsavakkhayaṃ.
Ekacariyanivāsena, khaggasiṅgasamūpamā;
Tepi nātigatā maccuṃ, mādisesu kathāva kāti.

Evaṃ paccekabuddhato anussaritabbaṃ. Kathaṃ sammāsambuddhato? Yopi so bhagavā asītianubyañjanapaṭimaṇḍitadvattiṃsamahāpurisalakkhaṇavicitrarūpakāyo sabbākāraparisuddhasīlakkhandhādiguṇaratanasamiddhadhammakāyo yasamahattapuññamahattathāmamahattaiddhimahattapaññāmahattānaṃ pāraṃ gato asamo asamasamo appaṭipuggalo arahaṃ sammāsambuddho, sopi salilavuṭṭhinipātena mahāaggikkhandho viya maraṇavuṭṭhinipātena ṭhānaso vūpasanto.

Evaṃ mahānubhāvassa, yaṃ nāmetaṃ mahesino;
Na bhayena na lajjāya, maraṇaṃ vasamāgataṃ.
Nillajjaṃ vītasārajjaṃ, sabbasattābhimaddanaṃ;
Tayidaṃ mādisaṃ sattaṃ, kathaṃ nābhibhavissatīti.

Evaṃ sammāsambuddhato anussaritabbaṃ. Tassevaṃ yasamahattatādisampannehi parehi saddhiṃ maraṇasāmaññatāya attānaṃ upasaṃharitvā tesaṃ viya sattavisesānaṃ mayhampi maraṇaṃ bhavissatīti anussarato upacārappattaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ hotīti. Evaṃ upasaṃharaṇato maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

"Through the ruin of success": herein, success shines only for as long as ruin does not overtake it, and there is no such thing as a success that stands beyond the reach of ruin. Thus indeed —

"Having enjoyed the whole earth, happy, having given away a hundred koṭis, / in the end he attained a sovereignty worth barely half a small fruit. / With that same bodily frame, once his merit had come to exhaustion, / facing death, that formerly griefless one came to grief."

Moreover, all health ends in sickness, all youth ends in old age, all life ends in death; the entire abode of the world is followed by birth, pursued by aging, overcome by sickness, struck down by death. Hence it was said —

"Just as massive rocky mountains, / reaching to the sky, / might roll in from every side, / crushing all four quarters,
So too aging and death / overwhelm living beings — / nobles, brahmins, merchants, / commoners, outcastes, and scavengers;
It spares nothing at all, / it crushes everything alike.
There is no ground there for elephants, / nor for chariots, nor for foot-soldiers; / nor can one conquer it by strategy, / nor even by wealth." (S.N. 1.136)

Thus, determining that the success of life ends in the ruin of death, death should be recollected through the ruin of success.

"By comparison with others": comparing oneself together with others. Herein, death should be recollected through comparison by seven approaches: by way of great fame, great merit, great strength, great psychic power, great wisdom, Paccekabuddhas, and Fully Enlightened Buddhas. How? This death falls, without the slightest hesitation, even upon those of great fame, great retinues, and abundant wealth and vehicles — Mahāsammata, Mandhātā, Mahāsudassana, Daḷhanemi, Nimi, and the rest — so how much less will it fail to fall upon me?

Kings of great fame, / Mahāsammata and the rest — / even they came under the sway of death; / what then is to be said of one such as me?

Thus, by way of great fame, it should be recollected. How, by way of great merit?

Jotika, Jaṭila, Ugga, Meṇḍaka, and Puṇṇaka — / these and other such, renowned in the world as of great merit — / all of them met with death; / what then is to be said of one such as me?

Thus, by way of great merit, it should be recollected. How, by way of great strength?

Vāsudeva, Baladeva, Bhīmasena, Yudhiṭṭhila, / and Cānura, the great wrestler — all came under the sway of the Ender.
These, thus endowed with strength and might, renowned in the world — / even they went to death; what then is to be said of one such as me?

Thus, by way of great strength, it should be recollected. How, by way of great psychic power?

With merely his big toe, he once shook the Vejayanta palace — / he who was named foremost among those possessing psychic power, the second chief disciple.
Even he entered the dreadful jaws of death, as a deer into a lion's jaws, / together with all his psychic powers; what then is to be said of one such as me?

Thus, by way of great psychic power, it should be recollected. How, by way of great wisdom?

Setting aside the Protector of the World, whatever other living beings there are, / they are not worth a sixteenth part of Sāriputta's wisdom.
Even he, thus named of great wisdom, the first chief disciple, / came under the power of death — what then is to be said of one such as me?

Thus, by way of great wisdom, it should be recollected. How, by way of Paccekabuddhas? Even those self-become ones who, by the strength of their own knowledge and energy, having crushed all the enemies of the defilements, attained Paccekabodhi, comparable to the horn of a rhinoceros — even they are not freed from death; how then shall I be freed?

Those great sages who, examining this or that sign, / by the power of self-arisen knowledge, attained the destruction of the taints —
Living the solitary life, comparable to a rhinoceros's horn — / even they did not go beyond death; what then is to be said of one such as me?

Thus, by way of Paccekabuddhas, it should be recollected. How, by way of Fully Enlightened Buddhas? Even that Blessed One, whose form-body was adorned with the eighty minor marks and the thirty-two marks of a great man, whose Dhamma-body was made resplendent with the treasure of purified virtue and other such qualities in every respect, who had gone beyond the far shore of great fame, great merit, great strength, great psychic power, and great wisdom, unequaled, equal only to the unequaled, without a peer, an arahant, a perfectly self-enlightened one — even he, like a great mass of fire struck by a downpour of rain, was extinguished on the spot by the downpour of death.

Thus, for one of such great power, this great sage — / neither through fear nor through shame did death come to overpower him.
This shameless thing, devoid of any diffidence, that crushes all beings — / how should it fail to overpower a being such as me?

Thus, by way of Fully Enlightened Buddhas, it should be recollected. For one who, thus comparing himself with others endowed with great fame and the rest, by way of their shared subjection to death, recollects, "death will come to me too, just as it did to those distinguished beings" — for him, the meditation subject reaches access concentration. Thus, death should be recollected by comparison with others.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The comparison method's rhetorical structure is deliberately escalating: it moves through fame, merit, strength, psychic power, and wisdom — each category topped out by a maximally impressive named example (legendary universal monarchs, the wealthiest patrons, legendary strongmen, Mahāmoggallāna's toe shaking a celestial palace, Sāriputta's wisdom outweighing all other beings sixteen-to-one) — before climbing past even these to Paccekabuddhas and finally to the Fully Enlightened Buddha himself. Each step's refrain, "what then is to be said of one such as me," gains force precisely because the examples keep getting more extreme, making the final comparison to the Buddha's own death the rhetorical climax the whole sequence has been building toward.

The closing verse on the Buddha's death is carefully worded to avoid any suggestion that death overcame him through weakness: it explicitly denies that fear or shame played any role, framing his passing instead as a natural extinguishing, like fire meeting rain — a process, not a defeat. The point of citing it here is not to diminish the Buddha's attainment but to establish, with maximal force, that literally nothing — not even complete enlightenment — exempts anyone from this basic fact of existence.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
mādisesu kathāva kā

what then is to be said of one such as me? — the refrain closing each of the seven comparison-verses, driving home the argument's logical force after each named example.

khaggavisāṇakappa

comparable to a rhinoceros's horn — a standard epithet for Paccekabuddhas, whose solitary path of realization (like a rhinoceros's single horn) contrasts with a fully enlightened Buddha's establishment of a teaching lineage.

Mahāsammata / Mandhātā / Mahāsudassana

legendary universal monarchs (cakkavattin) from the distant past, named here as examples of the greatest possible worldly fame and dominion, all nonetheless subject to death.

A Body Shared by Many, and the Fragility of Life

The fourth approach observes that this body is not truly one's own private possession at all: eighty families of worms already inhabit it, being born, aging, and dying within it as though it were their maternity ward, sickroom, and cemetery combined, while hundreds of internal diseases and external dangers converge on it from every direction like weapons flying in toward a target set at a crossroads. The fifth approach exposes just how little actually sustains a single moment of continued life — breath, posture, temperature, the elements, and food, each one a fragile, momentary condition rather than a solid guarantee.

Pali Source

Kāyabahusādhāraṇatoti ayaṃ kāyo bahusādhāraṇo. Asītiyā tāva kimikulānaṃ sādhāraṇo, tattha chavinissitā pāṇā chaviṃ khādanti, cammanissitā cammaṃ khādanti, maṃsanissitā maṃsaṃ khādanti, nhārunissitā nhāruṃ khādanti, aṭṭhinissitā aṭṭhiṃ khādanti, miñjanissitā miñjaṃ khādanti. Tattheva jāyanti jīyanti mīyanti, uccārapassāvaṃ karonti. Kāyova nesaṃ sūtigharañceva gilānasālā ca susānañca vaccakuṭi ca passāvadoṇikā ca. Svāyaṃ tesampi kimikulānaṃ pakopena maraṇaṃ nigacchatiyeva. Yathā ca asītiyā kimikulānaṃ, evaṃ ajjhattikānaṃyeva anekasatānaṃ rogānaṃ bāhirānañca ahivicchikādīnaṃ maraṇassa paccayānaṃ sādhāraṇo.

Yathā hi catumahāpathe ṭhapite lakkhamhi sabbadisāhi āgatā sarasattitomarapāsāṇādayo nipatanti, evaṃ kāyepi sabbupaddavā nipatanti. Svāyaṃ tesampi upaddavānaṃ nipātena maraṇaṃ nigacchatiyeva. Tenāha bhagavā — "idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu divase nikkhante rattiyā paṭihitāya iti paṭisañcikkhati, bahukā kho me paccayā maraṇassa, ahi vā maṃ ḍaṃseyya, vicchiko vā maṃ ḍaṃseyya, satapadī vā maṃ ḍaṃseyya, tena me assa kālaṅkiriyā, so mamassa antarāyo, upakkhalitvā vā papateyyaṃ, bhattaṃ vā me bhuttaṃ byāpajjeyya, pittaṃ vā me kuppeyya, semhaṃ vā me kuppeyya, satthakā vā me vātā kuppeyyuṃ, tena me assa kālaṅkiriyā, so mamassa antarāyo"ti. Evaṃ (a. ni. 6.20) kāyabahusādhāraṇato maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Āyudubbalatoti āyu nāmetaṃ abalaṃ dubbalaṃ. Tathā hi sattānaṃ jīvitaṃ assāsapassāsūpanibaddhañceva iriyāpathūpanibaddhañca sītuṇhūpanibaddhañca mahābhūtūpanibaddhañca āhārūpanibaddhañca. Tadetaṃ assāsapassāsānaṃ samavuttitaṃ labhamānameva pavattati. Bahi nikkhantanāsikavāte pana anto apavisante, paviṭṭhe vā anikkhamante mato nāma hoti. Catunnaṃ iriyāpathānampi samavuttitaṃ labhamānameva pavattati. Aññataraññatarassa pana adhimattatāya āyusaṅkhārā upacchijjanti. Sītuṇhānampi samavuttitaṃ labhamānameva pavattati. Atisītena pana atiuṇhena vā abhibhūtassa vipajjati. Mahābhūtānampi samavuttitaṃ labhamānameva pavattati. Pathavīdhātuyā pana āpodhātuādīnaṃ vā aññataraññatarassa pakopena balasampannopi puggalo patthaddhakāyo vā atisārādivasena kilinnapūtikāyo vā mahāḍāhapareto vā sambhijjamānasandhibandhano vā hutvā jīvitakkhayaṃ pāpuṇāti. Kabaḷīkārāhārampi yuttakāle labhantasseva jīvitaṃ pavattati, āhāraṃ alabhamānassa pana parikkhayaṃ gacchatīti. Evaṃ āyudubbalato maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

"As a body shared by many": this body is shared by many. First, it is shared by eighty families of worms: those dwelling on the skin eat the skin, those on the hide eat the hide, those on the flesh eat the flesh, those on the tendons eat the tendons, those on the bones eat the bones, those on the marrow eat the marrow. There they are born, there they age, there they die, there they void excrement and urine — the body itself is their maternity house, their sick-ward, their charnel ground, their latrine, their urinal. And this body, through the disturbance of even these worm-families, does come to death. And just as it is shared by eighty families of worms, so too it is shared by the conditions for death consisting of hundreds of internal diseases, and external dangers such as snakes and scorpions.

For just as, when a target is set up at a crossroads, arrows, spears, javelins, stones, and the like, coming from every direction, fall upon it, so too every calamity falls upon the body. And this body, through the falling of even these calamities, does come to death. Hence the Blessed One said — "here, monks, a monk, when the day has departed and the night has set in, reflects thus: 'many indeed are the conditions for my death — a snake might bite me, a scorpion might bite me, a centipede might bite me, and by that I might die; that would be an obstacle for me. Or I might stumble and fall; or the food I have eaten might disagree with me; or my bile might become disturbed, or my phlegm might become disturbed, or piercing winds within me might become disturbed, and by that I might die; that would be an obstacle for me'" (A.N. 6.20). Thus, death should be recollected as a body shared by many.

"Through the fragility of life": this lifespan is powerless, fragile. For thus the life of beings is bound up with the in-breath and out-breath, bound up with the postures, bound up with heat and cold, bound up with the great elements, and bound up with food. This continues only as long as the in-breaths and out-breaths obtain an even functioning. But when the wind that has gone out through the nostrils does not enter again, or when what has entered does not go out again, then one is said to be dead. Likewise, it continues only as long as the four postures obtain an even functioning; but through the excess of one or another of them, the vital formations are cut off. Likewise, it continues only as long as cold and heat obtain an even functioning; but for one overcome by excessive cold or excessive heat, it perishes. Likewise, it continues only as long as the great elements obtain an even functioning; but through a disturbance in the earth element, or in one or another of the water element and the rest, even a person of full strength may become rigid of body, or may become one whose body is soaked and putrid through dysentery and the like, or may be overcome by a great burning fever, or may have his joints breaking apart, and so reach the end of his life. And even the physical food that nourishes continues to sustain life only as long as one obtains it at the proper time; but for one not obtaining food, it comes to complete exhaustion. Thus, death should be recollected through the fragility of life.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The description of the body as the worm-families' "maternity house, sick-ward, charnel ground, latrine, and urinal" is a deliberately unglamorous piece of imagery, undermining the ordinary sense of exclusive bodily ownership from an unexpected angle: the point is not that the body is repulsive in the abstract (that argument belongs to the foulness meditations of an earlier chapter) but that it is not even exclusively one's own to begin with — an entire ecosystem of other beings already lives, is born, and dies inside it, continuously, right now, quite apart from any question of its eventual decay.

The fivefold list of what sustains a single moment of life — breath, posture, temperature, the elements, food — is notable for treating each as an independent point of failure rather than redundant safeguards: the text does not suggest that if breath fails, posture or temperature could somehow compensate. Each condition, on its own, is fully sufficient to end life the moment it fails, meaning that continued existence at any given instant depends on the simultaneous, uninterrupted cooperation of five entirely separate systems, none of which is under one's ultimate control.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
asīti kimikula

the eighty families of worms — asīti (eighty) + kimi (worm) + kula (family, lineage); the traditional number of distinct worm-species said to inhabit the human body, each attached to a specific bodily tissue.

catumahāpatha

a crossroads of four main roads — catu (four) + mahāpatha (a main road); the setting for the target-and-arrows simile, chosen because a crossroads exposes a fixed point to danger converging from every direction at once.

āyusaṅkhāra

the vital formations, life-formations — āyu (life, vitality) + saṅkhāra (formation); the specific continuity that is said to be "cut off" the moment any one of the five sustaining conditions (breath, posture, temperature, elements, food) fails decisively.

Without Any Fixed Sign, a Brief Span, and a Moment's Duration

The sixth approach observes that nothing about death is ever fixed or predictable — not the moment in the womb it might strike, not the specific illness that will cause it, not the time of day, not even the place the body will finally fall, since one born inside a village might die outside it and vice versa. The seventh recalls that even the longest human life today runs to barely a hundred years, urging a monk to treat even the time it takes to swallow a few mouthfuls, or a single breath, as a real unit of time in which death might arrive. The eighth and most rigorous approach pushes past even this: in the strictest sense, a being's life lasts only a single moment of consciousness, no longer than a cartwheel's momentary contact with the ground.

Pali Source

Animittatoti avavatthānato, paricchedābhāvatoti attho. Sattānaṃ hi —

Jīvitaṃ byādhi kālo ca, dehanikkhepanaṃ gati;
Pañcete jīvalokasmiṃ, animittā na nāyare.

Tattha jīvitaṃ tāva "ettakameva jīvitabbaṃ, na ito para"nti vavatthānābhāvato animittaṃ. Kalalakālepi hi sattā maranti, abbudapesighanamāsikadvemāsatemāsacatumāsapañcamāsadasamāsakālepi. Kucchito nikkhantasamayepi. Tato paraṃ vassasatassa antopi bahipi marantiyeva. Byādhipi "imināva byādhinā sattā maranti, nāññenā"ti vavatthānābhāvato animitto. Cakkhurogenāpi hi sattā maranti, sotarogādīnaṃ aññatarenāpi. Kālopi "imasmiṃyeva kāle maritabbaṃ, nāññasmi"nti evaṃ vavatthānābhāvato animitto. Pubbaṇhepi hi sattā maranti, majjhanhikādīnaṃ aññatarasmimpi. Dehanikkhepanampi "idheva mīyamānānaṃ dehena patitabbaṃ, nāññatrā"ti evaṃ vavatthānābhāvato animittaṃ. Antogāme jātānaṃ hi bahigāmepi attabhāvo patati. Bahigāme jātānampi antogāme. Tathā thalajānaṃ vā jale, jalajānaṃ vā thaleti anekappakārato vitthāretabbaṃ. Gatipi "ito cutena idha nibbattitabba"nti evaṃ vavatthānābhāvato animittā. Devalokato hi cutā manussesupi nibbattanti, manussalokato cutā devalokādīnampi yattha katthaci nibbattantīti evaṃ yantayuttagoṇo viya gatipañcake loko samparivattatīti evaṃ animittato maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Addhānaparicchedatoti manussānaṃ jīvitassa nāma etarahi paritto addhā. Yo ciraṃ jīvati, so vassasataṃ, appaṃ vā bhiyyo. Tenāha bhagavā — "appamidaṃ, bhikkhave, manussānaṃ āyu, gamanīyo samparāyo, kattabbaṃ kusalaṃ, caritabbaṃ brahmacariyaṃ, natthi jātassa amaraṇaṃ. Yo, bhikkhave, ciraṃ jīvati, so vassasataṃ, appaṃ vā bhiyyoti.

Appamāyumanussānaṃ, hīḷeyya naṃ suporiso;
Careyyādittasīsova, natthi maccussa nāgamoti. (saṃ. ni. 1.145)

Aparampi āha — "bhūtapubbaṃ, bhikkhave, arako nāma satthā ahosī"ti sabbampi sattahi upamāhi alaṅkataṃ suttaṃ vitthāretabbaṃ.

Aparampi āha — "yocāyaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu evaṃ maraṇassatiṃ bhāveti, aho vatāhaṃ rattindivaṃ jīveyyaṃ, bhagavato sāsanaṃ manasikareyyaṃ, bahuṃ vata me kataṃ assāti. ... Ime vuccanti, bhikkhave, bhikkhū pamattā viharanti, dandhaṃ maraṇassatiṃ bhāventi āsavānaṃ khayāya. Yo ca khvāyaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu evaṃ maraṇassatiṃ bhāveti, aho vatāhaṃ tadantaraṃ jīveyyaṃ, yadantaraṃ ekaṃ ālopaṃ saṅkhāditvā ajjhoharāmi, bhagavato sāsanaṃ manasikareyyaṃ, bahuṃ vata me kataṃ assāti. Yo cāyaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu evaṃ maraṇassatiṃ bhāveti, aho vatāhaṃ tadantaraṃ jīveyyaṃ, yadantaraṃ assasitvā vā passasāmi, passasitvā vā assasāmi, bhagavato sāsanaṃ manasikareyyaṃ, bahuṃ vata me kataṃ assāti. Ime vuccanti, bhikkhave, bhikkhū appamattā viharanti, tikkhaṃ maraṇassatiṃ bhāventi āsavānaṃ khayāyā"ti (a. ni. 6.19). Evaṃ catupañcālopasaṅkhādanamattaṃ avissāsiyo paritto jīvitassa addhāti evaṃ addhānaparicchedato maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Khaṇaparittatoti paramatthato hi atiparitto sattānaṃ jīvitakkhaṇo ekacittappavattimattoyeva. Yathā nāma rathacakkaṃ pavattamānampi ekeneva nemippadesena pavattati, tiṭṭhamānampi ekeneva tiṭṭhati, evameva ekacittakkhaṇikaṃ sattānaṃ jīvitaṃ. Tasmiṃ citte niruddhamatte satto niruddhoti vuccati. Yathāha — "atīte cittakkhaṇe jīvittha, na jīvati, na jīvissati. Anāgate cittakkhaṇe na jīvittha, na jīvati, jīvissati. Paccuppanne cittakkhaṇe na jīvittha, jīvati, na jīvissati.

"Jīvitaṃ attabhāvo ca, sukhadukkhā ca kevalā;
Ekacittasamāyuttā, lahu so vattate khaṇo.
"Ye niruddhā marantassa, tiṭṭhamānassa vā idha;
Sabbepi sadisā khandhā, gatā appaṭisandhikā.
"Anibbattena na jāto, paccuppannena jīvati;
Cittabhaṅgā mato loko, paññatti paramatthiyā"ti. (mahāni. 39)

Evaṃ khaṇaparittato maraṇaṃ anussaritabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

"As without any fixed sign": meaning, without any determination, without any delimitation. For, in the world of the living —

Life, illness, the time, / where the body will be laid down, and the destiny — / these five, in the world of the living, / are known to be without any fixed sign.

Herein, first, "life" is without any fixed sign because there is no determination that "one must live only this much, and no further." For beings die even at the time they are a mere embryonic clot, at the stages of a swelling, a lump of flesh, a solid mass, and at one month, two months, three months, four months, five months, ten months; at the moment of emerging from the womb; and thereafter they die both within and beyond a hundred years. "Illness" too is without any fixed sign, because there is no determination that "beings die by this illness only, and no other." For beings die of an eye disease, and equally of an ear disease or some other disease. "The time" too is without any fixed sign, because there is no determination that "one must die at this time only, and no other." For beings die in the morning, and equally at midday or some other time. "Where the body will be laid down" too is without any fixed sign, because there is no determination that "those dying must fall with their body here only, and nowhere else." For the body of one born inside a village may fall outside the village, and that of one born outside may fall inside — and likewise, in many ways, that of a land-creature may fall in water, and that of a water-creature on land. "Destiny" too is without any fixed sign, because there is no determination that "one passing away from here must be reborn there." For beings passing away from the deva world are reborn even among humans, and beings passing away from the human world are reborn in the deva world and elsewhere, in any of a variety of places — thus the world revolves through the five destinies like an ox yoked to a machine. Thus, death should be recollected as without any fixed sign.

"Through the brevity of the span": the span of human life at present is indeed brief. One who lives long lives a hundred years, or a little more. Hence the Blessed One said: "brief, monks, is this human lifespan; a future state must be gone to; what is wholesome should be done; the holy life should be lived; there is no non-dying for one who is born. One, monks, who lives long, lives a hundred years, or a little more."

Let the good man scorn this brief human life; / let him live as though his head were ablaze — / there is no escaping the Ender of life. (S.N. 1.145)

He said further: "once, monks, there was a teacher named Araka" — the entire discourse, adorned with seven similes, should be given in full.

He said further: "that monk, monks, who develops recollection of death thus — 'oh, that I might live a day and a night, that I might attend to the Blessed One's teaching — much indeed would then be done by me' ... these, monks, are called monks who dwell heedlessly, who develop recollection of death sluggishly, for the destruction of the taints. But that monk, monks, who develops recollection of death thus — 'oh, that I might live for just that interval within which I chew and swallow a single mouthful, that I might attend to the Blessed One's teaching — much indeed would then be done by me,' or, 'oh, that I might live for just that interval within which I breathe out after breathing in, or breathe in after breathing out, that I might attend to the Blessed One's teaching — much indeed would then be done by me' — these, monks, are called monks who dwell heedfully, who develop recollection of death keenly, for the destruction of the taints" (A.N. 6.19). Thus, the span of life is untrustworthy and brief, amounting to no more than the time it takes to chew four or five mouthfuls — thus death should be recollected through the brevity of the span.

"Through the momentariness of the interval": in the ultimate sense, indeed, the life-moment of beings is extremely brief — amounting to no more than the occurrence of a single moment of consciousness. Just as a cart-wheel, whether rolling or standing still, touches the ground at only a single point of its rim, so too the life of beings lasts for only a single moment of consciousness. As soon as that consciousness has ceased, the being is said to have ceased. As it is said: "in a past moment of consciousness one lived, one does not live, one will not live. In a future moment of consciousness one did not live, one does not live, one will live. In the present moment of consciousness one did not live, one lives, one will not live.

"Life, personal existence, pleasure and pain, all together, / are bound up with a single moment of consciousness; that moment passes swiftly.
Whether for one dying or one still standing here, / the aggregates that have ceased are all alike — gone, without renewal of the link.
Not yet arisen, one is not born; one lives through the present; / the world dies with the breaking-up of consciousness — such is the designation in the ultimate sense." (Mahāniddesa 39)

Thus, death should be recollected through the momentariness of the interval.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The "without any fixed sign" approach systematically dismantles five separate expectations people quietly hold about their own deaths — that it will happen at some predictable stage of life, from some particular cause, at some particular time of day, in some particular place, leading to some particular destination — showing, item by item, that no such regularity actually exists. What makes this approach distinct from the earlier "brevity of the span" argument is that it targets not the length of life but the total unpredictability of every specific circumstance surrounding its end.

The final approach's insistence that a being's true lifespan is a single moment of consciousness represents this recollection's most philosophically radical move: it is not merely saying that conventional life is short, but that even the conventional notion of a continuous, day-spanning "life" is itself only a convenient designation (paññatti) — in the strictest analysis, what actually exists from moment to moment is a rapid, unbroken succession of individually complete, individually ceasing units of experience, with no single persisting thing that lives through all of them. This is the recollection's own bridge into the treatise's broader Abhidhamma account of momentariness.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
gati

a destiny, a realm of rebirth — one of the five things declared "without any fixed sign," since passing from any one realm of existence carries no guarantee of where the next rebirth will occur.

ālopa

a mouthful — the concrete unit of time (the duration of chewing and swallowing four or five mouthfuls) the Buddha uses to make "the brevity of the span" viscerally immediate rather than abstract.

ekacittakkhaṇika

lasting a single moment of consciousness — eka (one) + citta (mind, consciousness) + khaṇika (momentary); the technical claim underlying the "momentariness of the interval" approach, treating a lifetime, in the strictest sense, as an unbroken chain of individually ceasing conscious moments rather than one continuous thing.

The Benefits of Recollecting Death

Whichever of the eight approaches a meditator uses, sustained recollection of death suppresses the hindrances and produces access concentration — never absorption, since death, unlike the more purely constructed objects of other meditation subjects, is itself an actual, urgency-inducing fact rather than a neutral concept the mind can simply rest upon. The chapter closes by describing the practical transformation this recollection produces: constant heedfulness, disenchantment with every possible mode of existence, freedom from clinging to life, and — most strikingly — the ability to face one's own actual moment of death without the terror, confusion, and panic that overtakes those who never prepared for it.

Pali Source

Iti imesaṃ aṭṭhannaṃ ākārānaṃ aññataraññatarena anussaratopi punappunaṃ manasikāravasena cittaṃ āsevanaṃ labhati, maraṇārammaṇā sati santiṭṭhati, nīvaraṇāni vikkhambhanti, jhānaṅgāni pātubhavanti. Sabhāvadhammattā pana saṃvejanīyattā ca ārammaṇassa appanaṃ appatvā upacārappattameva jhānaṃ hoti. Lokuttarajjhānaṃ pana dutiyacatutthāni ca āruppajjhānāni sabhāvadhammepi bhāvanāvisesena appanaṃ pāpuṇanti. Visuddhibhāvanānukkamavasena hi lokuttaraṃ appanaṃ pāpuṇāti. Ārammaṇātikkamabhāvanāvasena āruppaṃ. Appanāpattasseva hi jhānassa ārammaṇasamatikkamanamattaṃ tattha hoti. Idha pana tadubhayampi natthi. Tasmā upacārappattameva jhānaṃ hoti. Tadetaṃ maraṇassatibalena uppannattā maraṇassaticceva saṅkhaṃ gacchati.

Imañca pana maraṇassatiṃ anuyutto bhikkhu satataṃ appamatto hoti, sabbabhavesu anabhiratisaññaṃ paṭilabhati, jīvitanikantiṃ jahāti, pāpagarahī hoti, asannidhibahulo parikkhāresu vigatamalamacchero, aniccasaññā cassa paricayaṃ gacchati, tadanusāreneva dukkhasaññā anattasaññā ca upaṭṭhāti. Yathā abhāvitamaraṇā sattā sahasā vāḷamigayakkhasappacoravadhakābhibhūtā viya maraṇasamaye bhayaṃ santāsaṃ sammohaṃ āpajjanti, evaṃ anāpajjitvā abhayo asammūḷho kālaṃ karoti. Sace diṭṭheva dhamme amataṃ nārādheti, kāyassa bhedā sugatiparāyano hoti.

Tasmā have appamādaṃ, kayirātha sumedhaso;
Evaṃ mahānubhāvāya, maraṇassatiyā sadāti.

Idaṃ maraṇassatiyaṃ vitthārakathāmukhaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

Thus, for one recollecting death by any one or another of these eight approaches, the mind gains repeated practice through this constant attention; mindfulness with death as its object becomes established; the hindrances are suppressed; the jhāna factors become manifest. But, because the object is an actual, real state, and because it induces a sense of urgency, the jhāna that arises does not reach absorption, but only reaches access. The supramundane jhāna, however, as well as the second and fourth immaterial jhānas, do reach absorption even with respect to actual, real states, through the distinctive power of their development — for the supramundane reaches absorption by way of the successive order of purification, and the immaterial by way of the development that surpasses its object. For, in the case of a jhāna that has already reached absorption, only the mere surpassing of its object occurs there. But here, neither of these two conditions is present. Therefore, the jhāna that results here reaches only access. And this, because it has arisen by the strength of recollecting death, is reckoned simply as recollection of death.

And a monk devoted to this recollection of death becomes constantly heedful; he acquires the perception of non-delight in all forms of existence; he abandons attachment to life; he becomes one who condemns evil; he becomes one who does not accumulate an excess of requisites, free from the stain of stinginess regarding them; and the perception of impermanence becomes familiar to him, and, following upon that, the perception of suffering and the perception of non-self become established as well. Just as beings who have not developed this recollection of death fall, at the time of death, into fear, terror, and confusion, as though suddenly overcome by fierce wild animals, non-human beings, snakes, robbers, or an executioner — so he, without falling into any such state, dies unafraid and unconfused. And if he does not attain the deathless in this very life, then, at the breaking-up of the body, he becomes one bound for a happy destination.

Therefore let the wise one always practice / diligence toward this recollection of death, / so greatly powerful.

This is the opening of the detailed discussion on recollection of death.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The explanation for why recollection of death caps out at access concentration draws a precise distinction between two different kinds of "real state" objects: ordinary recollection of death cannot reach absorption because death is genuinely urgency-inducing and cannot be neutralized into a purely restful object, but the text is careful to note two exceptions — the supramundane jhāna (which reaches absorption through the accumulated momentum of the entire purification sequence) and certain immaterial jhānas (which reach it by a development that has already surpassed needing a calming object at all). This shows the chapter's characteristic precision: rather than stating a flat rule, it identifies exactly which two cases require separate explanation and supplies it.

The closing claim — that a practitioner of this recollection dies "unafraid and unconfused," in explicit contrast to those who face death "as though suddenly overcome by fierce wild animals... or an executioner" — brings the chapter full circle to its very first section, where an executioner with a drawn sword was the opening image for death's own constant proximity. The entire eightfold structure of this recollection turns out to be preparation for exactly one moment: meeting, without panic, the same fact the meditation has spent so much effort making vividly, unavoidably present all along.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
saṃvejanīya

inducing a sense of urgency — the specific quality of death as an object that keeps this recollection at access concentration rather than full absorption, since a truly urgency-inducing object resists the kind of settling absorption requires.

asannidhibahula

not given to excessive accumulation — a (not) + sannidhi (storing up, accumulation) + bahula (much, abundant); one of the practical character traits this recollection is said to cultivate, a natural consequence of no longer clinging tightly to continued possession.

jīvitanikanti

attachment to life, clinging to continued existence — jīvita (life) + nikanti (relish, clinging); the specific attachment this recollection is designed to loosen, distinct from ordinary sense-desire.

Mindfulness of the Body: Origins and the Thirty-Two-Part Formula

The chapter turns to a meditation subject the Buddha praised as unknown before his own appearance and beyond the reach of any other spiritual teacher — a single practice said to lead to profound urgency, great benefit, security from bondage, mindfulness and full awareness, knowledge and vision, a happy dwelling here and now, and the very fruit of liberating knowledge. Of the fourteen sections that make up the full canonical exposition of mindfulness of the body, this chapter identifies only one — attention to the thirty-two repulsive parts of the body — as the meditation subject properly belonging here, since the others are treated either elsewhere (the breathing section, as its own separate meditation subject) or as insight practice rather than concentration.

Pali Source

Idāni yaṃ taṃ aññatra buddhuppādā appavattapubbaṃ sabbatitthiyānaṃ avisayabhūtaṃ tesu tesu suttantesu "ekadhammo, bhikkhave, bhāvito bahulīkato mahato saṃvegāya saṃvattati. Mahato atthāya saṃvattati. Mahato yogakkhemāya saṃvattati. Mahato satisampajaññāya saṃvattati. Ñāṇadassanapaṭilābhāya saṃvattati. Diṭṭhadhammasukhavihārāya saṃvattati. Vijjāvimuttiphalasacchikiriyāya saṃvattati. Katamo ekadhammo? Kāyagatā sati…" (a. ni. 1.563 ādayo). Amataṃ te, bhikkhave, paribhuñjanti, ye kāyagatāsatiṃ paribhuñjanti. Amataṃ te, bhikkhave, na paribhuñjanti, ye kāyagatāsatiṃ na paribhuñjanti. Amataṃ tesaṃ, bhikkhave, paribhuttaṃ… aparibhuttaṃ… parihīnaṃ… aparihīnaṃ… viraddhaṃ… aviraddhaṃ, yesaṃ kāyagatāsati āraddhāti (a. ni. 1.603) evaṃ bhagavatā anekehi ākārehi pasaṃsitvā "kathaṃ bhāvitā, bhikkhave, kāyagatāsati kathaṃ bahulīkatā mahapphalā hoti mahānisaṃsā? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu araññagato vā"tiādinā (ma. ni. 3.154) nayena ānāpānapabbaṃ, iriyāpathapabbaṃ, catusampajaññapabbaṃ, paṭikkūlamanasikārapabbaṃ, dhātumanasikārapabbaṃ, navasivathikapabbānīti imesaṃ cuddasannaṃ pabbānaṃ vasena kāyagatāsatikammaṭṭhānaṃ niddiṭṭhaṃ, tassa bhāvanāniddeso anuppatto.

Tattha yasmā iriyāpathapabbaṃ catusampajaññapabbaṃ dhātumanasikārapabbanti imāni tīṇi vipassanāvasena vuttāni. Nava sivathikapabbāni vipassanāñāṇesuyeva ādīnavānupassanāvasena vuttāni. Yāpi cettha uddhumātakādīsu samādhibhāvanā ijjheyya, sā asubhaniddese pakāsitāyeva. Ānāpānapabbaṃ pana paṭikkūlamanasikārapabbañca imānevettha dve samādhivasena vuttāni. Tesu ānāpānapabbaṃ ānāpānassativasena visuṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃyeva. Yaṃ panetaṃ "puna caparaṃ, bhikkhave, bhikkhu imameva kāyaṃ uddhaṃ pādatalā adho kesamatthakā tacapariyantaṃ pūraṃ nānappakārassa asucino paccavekkhati. Atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesā lomā…pe… mutta"nti (ma. ni. 3.154) evaṃ matthaluṅgaṃ aṭṭhimiñjena saṅgahetvā paṭikkūlamanasikāravasena desitaṃ dvattiṃsākārakammaṭṭhānaṃ, idamidha kāyagatāsatīti adhippetaṃ.

Tatthāyaṃ pāḷivaṇṇanāpubbaṅgamo bhāvanāniddeso. Imameva kāyanti imaṃ catumahābhūtikaṃ pūtikāyaṃ. Uddhaṃ pādatalāti pādatalato upari. Adho kesamatthakāti kesaggato heṭṭhā. Tacapariyantanti tiriyaṃ tacaparicchinnaṃ. Pūraṃ nānappakārassa asucino paccavekkhatīti nānappakārakesādiasucibharito ayaṃ kāyoti passati. Kathaṃ? Atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesā…pe… muttanti.

Tattha atthīti saṃvijjanti. Imasminti yvāyaṃ uddhaṃ pādatalā adho kesamatthakā tacapariyanto pūro nānappakārassa asucinoti vuccati, tasmiṃ. Kāyeti sarīre. Sarīraṃ hi asucisañcayato kucchitānaṃ kesādīnañceva cakkhurogādīnañca rogasatānaṃ āyabhūtato kāyoti vuccati. Kesā lomāti ete kesādayo dvattiṃsākārā. Tattha atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesā, atthi imasmiṃ kāye lomāti evaṃ sambandho veditabbo.

Imasmiṃ hi pādatalā paṭṭhāya upari, kesamatthakā paṭṭhāya heṭṭhā, tacato paṭṭhāya paritoti ettake byāmamatte kaḷevare sabbākārenapi vicinanto na koci kiñci muttaṃ vā maṇiṃ vā veḷuriyaṃ vā agaruṃ vā kuṅkumaṃ vā kappūraṃ vā vāsacuṇṇādiṃ vā aṇumattampi sucibhāvaṃ passati, atha kho paramaduggandhajegucchaṃ asirikadassanaṃ nānappakāraṃ kesalomādibhedaṃ asuciṃyeva passati. Tena vuttaṃ "atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesā lomā…pe… mutta"nti. Ayamettha padasambandhato vaṇṇanā.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now comes the exposition of the development of that meditation subject which, apart from a Buddha's appearance, had never before existed, and lies beyond the domain of all sectarian teachers — the subject which, in this and that discourse, the Blessed One praised in many ways: "one thing, monks, developed and made much of, conduces to great urgency, conduces to great benefit, conduces to great security from bondage, conduces to great mindfulness and full awareness, conduces to the attainment of knowledge and vision, conduces to a happy dwelling here and now, conduces to the realization of the fruit of true knowledge and deliverance. What is that one thing? Mindfulness of the body..." (A.N. 1.563 and following). "They partake of the deathless, monks, who partake of mindfulness of the body. They do not partake of the deathless, monks, who do not partake of mindfulness of the body. The deathless has been partaken of by them... has not been partaken of... has been neglected... has not been neglected... has been missed... has not been missed, by those for whom mindfulness of the body has been undertaken" (A.N. 1.603) — having thus praised it by the Blessed One in many ways, the meditation subject of mindfulness of the body was set forth by way of these fourteen sections: "and how, monks, is mindfulness of the body developed, how, made much of, is it of great fruit and great benefit? Here, monks, a monk gone to the forest..." (M.N. 3.154) and so on — the breathing section, the postures section, the four-kinds-of-full-awareness section, the section on attention to repulsiveness, the section on attention to the elements, and the nine charnel-ground sections. The exposition of its development has now been reached.

Herein, since the postures section, the four-kinds-of-full-awareness section, and the elements section are stated by way of insight; and the nine charnel-ground sections are stated, within the very knowledges of insight, by way of contemplating danger; and whatever development of concentration might succeed in regard to the bloated and the rest has already been made known in the description of the foul — therefore, of these, it is only the breathing section and the section on attention to repulsiveness that are stated here by way of concentration. Of these two, the breathing section, by way of mindfulness of breathing, is itself already a separate meditation subject in its own right. As for what remains — "furthermore, monks, a monk reviews this very body, from the soles of the feet upward, from the tips of the hairs downward, bounded by the skin, as full of manifold impurity: 'there are, in this body, head-hairs, body-hairs...urine'" (M.N. 3.154) — this, taught by way of attention to repulsiveness, gathering the brain together with the bone-marrow, is what is intended here as mindfulness of the body.

Herein, this is the exposition of development that proceeds first by way of commenting on the canonical text. "This very body" means this foul body composed of the four great elements. "From the soles of the feet upward" means: above from the soles of the feet. "From the tips of the hairs downward" means: below from the tips of the hairs. "Bounded by the skin" means: delimited crosswise by the skin. "Reviews as full of manifold impurity" means: he sees, "this body is filled with manifold impurity, hair and the rest." How? "There are, in this body, head-hairs...urine."

Herein, "there are" means: they are found to exist. "In this" means: in that body which is spoken of as "from the soles of the feet upward, from the tips of the hairs downward, bounded by the skin, full of manifold impurity." "The body" means the physical frame — for the body is called "kāya" because it is the accumulation-point of impurity, and the origin of hundreds of diseases such as those of the hairs and the rest, and of the eyes and the rest. "Head-hairs, body-hairs" refers to these thirty-two aspects, hair and the rest. Herein, the connection should be understood thus: "there are, in this body, head-hairs; there are, in this body, body-hairs."

For, in this body-frame measuring a mere fathom, bounded above from the soles of the feet, below from the tips of the hairs, and around from the skin, a meditator investigating in every possible way finds not the slightest speck of anything pure — not a pearl, nor a gem, nor beryl, nor aloeswood, nor saffron, nor camphor, nor scented powder, or the like — but sees only, in the manifold divisions of hair and the rest, an impurity that is extremely foul-smelling, disgusting, and unlovely to behold. For this it was said: "there are, in this body, head-hairs, body-hairs... urine." This, herein, is the commentary by way of the connection of the words.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The chapter's careful sorting of the fourteen canonical sections of mindfulness of the body — three assigned to insight, nine to contemplating danger within insight, one already covered by the earlier foulness chapter, one made into its own separate meditation subject, and only one (the thirty-two parts) actually belonging to this chapter as concentration practice — is a small but significant piece of internal cross-referencing: it shows the treatise actively managing where each piece of a single canonical discourse's rich, fourteen-part teaching gets its full treatment, rather than repeating material or leaving gaps.

The catalogue of precious substances explicitly searched for and not found — pearl, gem, beryl, aloeswood, saffron, camphor — functions as a deliberate provocation to the reader's own imagination of bodily beauty and value: these are exactly the materials used to adorn and perfume a body in the ordinary, admiring sense, and the passage's rhetorical move is to search the body itself, at this same fine scale, for even a trace of such value, and find none.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
cuddasa pabba

the fourteen sections — cuddasa (fourteen) + pabba (a joint, section); the complete traditional division of the canonical mindfulness-of-the-body discourse, of which only the thirty-two-parts section is treated here as a distinct concentration-meditation subject.

yogakkhema

security from bondage — yoga (a bond, a yoke) + khema (safety, security); one of the seven benefits the Buddha is quoted praising this practice for, referring to freedom from the four bonds that tie beings to the round of existence.

byāmamatta kaḷevara

a body-frame measuring a mere fathom — the same phrase used in the previous chapter's cosmology section for the physical body, here applied to emphasize how modest a container this "impurity-accumulation" actually is.

Learning the Sign: Sevenfold Skill in Grasping

Before any of the thirty-two parts can be contemplated for their repulsiveness, the meditator must first learn the meditation subject properly — reciting it aloud repeatedly, in forward and reverse order, exactly as two elders once became stream-enterers simply through four months of reciting the thirty-two-part formula under an accomplished teacher — and grasp each part by seven distinct approaches: verbally, mentally, by color, by shape, by direction, by location, and by delimitation.

Pali Source

Imaṃ pana kammaṭṭhānaṃ bhāvetukāmena ādikammikena kulaputtena vuttappakāraṃ kalyāṇamittaṃ upasaṅkamitvā idaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ gahetabbaṃ. Tenāpissa kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathentena sattadhā uggahakosallaṃ dasadhā ca manasikārakosallaṃ ācikkhitabbaṃ. Tattha vacasā manasā vaṇṇato saṇṭhānato disato okāsato paricchedatoti evaṃ sattadhā uggahakosallaṃ ācikkhitabbaṃ.

Imasmiṃ hi paṭikkūlamanasikārakammaṭṭhāne yopi tipiṭako hoti, tenāpi manasikārakāle paṭhamaṃ vācāya sajjhāyo kātabbo. Ekaccassa hi sajjhāyaṃ karontasseva kammaṭṭhānaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti malayavāsī mahādevattherassa santike uggahitakammaṭṭhānānaṃ dvinnaṃ therānaṃ viya. Thero kira tehi kammaṭṭhānaṃ yācito cattāro māse imaṃyeva sajjhāyaṃ karothāti dvattiṃsākārapāḷiṃ adāsi. Te kiñcāpi nesaṃ dve tayo nikāyā paguṇā, padakkhiṇaggāhitāya pana cattāro māse dvattiṃsākāraṃ sajjhāyantāva sotāpannā ahesuṃ. Tasmā kammaṭṭhānaṃ kathentena ācariyena antevāsiko vattabbo "paṭhamaṃ tāva vācāya sajjhāyaṃ karohī"ti.

Karontena ca tacapañcakādīni paricchinditvā anulomapaṭilomavasena sajjhāyo kātabbo. Kesā lomā nakhā dantā tacoti hi vatvā puna paṭilomato taco dantā nakhā lomā kesāti vattabbaṃ.

Tadanantaraṃ vakkapañcake maṃsaṃ nhāru aṭṭhi aṭṭhimiñjaṃ vakkanti vatvā puna paṭilomato vakkaṃ aṭṭhimiñjaṃ aṭṭhi nhāru maṃsaṃ, taco dantā nakhā lomā kesāti vattabbaṃ.

Tato papphāsapañcake hadayaṃ yakanaṃ kilomakaṃ pihakaṃ papphāsanti vatvā puna paṭilomato papphāsaṃ pihakaṃ kilomakaṃ yakanaṃ hadayaṃ, vakkaṃ aṭṭhimiñjaṃ aṭṭhi nhāru maṃsaṃ, taco dantā nakhā lomā kesāti vattabbaṃ.

Tato matthaluṅgapañcake antaṃ antaguṇaṃ udariyaṃ karīsaṃ matthaluṅganti vatvā puna paṭilomato matthaluṅgaṃ karīsaṃ udariyaṃ antaguṇaṃ antaṃ, papphāsaṃ pihakaṃ kilomakaṃ yakanaṃ hadayaṃ, vakkaṃ aṭṭhimiñjaṃ aṭṭhi nhāru maṃsaṃ, taco dantā nakhā lomā kesāti vattabbaṃ.

Tato medachakke pittaṃ semhaṃ pubbo lohitaṃ sedo medoti vatvā puna paṭilomato medo sedo lohitaṃ pubbo semhaṃ pittaṃ, matthaluṅgaṃ karīsaṃ udariyaṃ antaguṇaṃ antaṃ, papphāsaṃ pihakaṃ kilomakaṃ yakanaṃ hadayaṃ, vakkaṃ aṭṭhimiñjaṃ aṭṭhi nhāru maṃsaṃ, taco dantā nakhā lomā kesāti vattabbaṃ.

Tato muttachakke assu vasā kheḷo siṅghāṇikā lasikā muttanti vatvā puna paṭilomato muttaṃ lasikā siṅghāṇikā kheḷo vasā assu, medo sedo lohitaṃ pubbo semhaṃ pittaṃ, matthaluṅgaṃ karīsaṃ udariyaṃ antaguṇaṃ antaṃ, papphāsaṃ pihakaṃ kilomakaṃ yakanaṃ hadayaṃ, vakkaṃ aṭṭhimiñjaṃ aṭṭhi nhāru maṃsaṃ, taco dantā nakhā lomā kesāti vattabbaṃ.

Evaṃ kālasataṃ kālasahassaṃ kālasatasahassampi vācāya sajjhāyo kātabbo. Vacasā sajjhāyena hi kammaṭṭhānatanti paguṇā hoti, na ito cito ca cittaṃ vidhāvati. Koṭṭhāsā pākaṭā honti, hatthasaṅkhalikā viya vatipādapanti viya ca khāyanti.

Yathā pana vacasā, tatheva manasāpi sajjhāyo kātabbo. Vacasā sajjhāyo hi manasā sajjhāyassa paccayo hoti. Manasā sajjhāyo lakkhaṇapaṭivedhassa paccayo hoti.

Vaṇṇatoti kesādīnaṃ vaṇṇo vavatthapetabbo. Saṇṭhānatoti tesaññeva saṇṭhānaṃ vavatthapetabbaṃ. Disatoti imasmiṃ hi sarīre nābhito uddhaṃ uparimadisā, adho heṭṭhimadisā, tasmā ayaṃ koṭṭhāso imissā nāma disāyāti disā vavatthapetabbā. Okāsatoti ayaṃ koṭṭhāso imasmiṃ nāma okāse patiṭṭhitoti evaṃ tassa tassa okāso vavatthapetabbo. Paricchedatoti sabhāgaparicchedo visabhāgaparicchedoti dve paricchedā. Tattha ayaṃ koṭṭhāso heṭṭhā ca upari ca tiriyañca iminā nāma paricchinnoti evaṃ sabhāgaparicchedo veditabbo. Kesā na lomā, lomāpi na kesāti evaṃ amissakatāvasena visabhāgaparicchedo veditabbo.

Evaṃ sattadhā uggahakosallaṃ ācikkhantena pana idaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ asukasmiṃ sutte paṭikkūlavasena kathitaṃ, asukasmiṃ dhātuvasenāti ñatvā ācikkhitabbaṃ. Idañhi mahāsatipaṭṭhāne (dī. ni. 2.377) paṭikkūlavaseneva kathitaṃ. Mahāhatthipadopama(ma. ni. 1.300 ādayo) mahārāhulovāda(ma. ni. 2.113 ādayo) dhātuvibhaṅgesu(ma. ni. 3.342 ādayo) dhātuvasena kathitaṃ. Kāyagatāsatisutte (ma. ni. 3.153) pana yassa vaṇṇato upaṭṭhāti, taṃ sandhāya cattāri jhānāni vibhattāni. Tattha dhātuvasena kathitaṃ vipassanākammaṭṭhānaṃ hoti. Paṭikkūlavasena kathitaṃ samathakammaṭṭhānaṃ. Tadetaṃ idha samathakammaṭṭhānamevāti.

Ru-Yi Translation

A clansman just beginning, desiring to develop this meditation subject, should approach a good friend of the kind already described and take up this meditation subject from him. And that teacher too, in explaining the meditation subject, should explain the sevenfold skill in learning and the tenfold skill in attention. Herein, the sevenfold skill in learning should be explained as: verbally, mentally, by color, by shape, by direction, by location, and by delimitation.

For in this meditation subject of attention to repulsiveness, even one who has mastered the entire Tipiṭaka should, at the time of attending to it, first recite it aloud. For some, the meditation subject becomes manifest simply through the very act of reciting, as happened for the two elders who took up their meditation subject in the presence of the Elder Mahādeva, a dweller in the hill country. It is told that this elder, when asked by them for a meditation subject, gave them the thirty-two-part canonical text, saying, "recite just this for four months." Although they had already mastered two or three of the Nikāyas, nevertheless, through their thoroughgoing manner of learning, they became stream-enterers simply by reciting the thirty-two parts for four months. Therefore, the teacher explaining a meditation subject should tell his pupil, "first, recite it aloud."

And, in reciting it, it should be recited forward and backward, delimited into groups of five beginning with the skin-pentad. Having said "head-hairs, body-hairs, nails, teeth, skin," it should then be said in reverse: "skin, teeth, nails, body-hairs, head-hairs."

Next, in the kidney-pentad: having said "flesh, sinews, bones, bone-marrow, kidneys," it should then be said in reverse: "kidneys, bone-marrow, bones, sinews, flesh; skin, teeth, nails, body-hairs, head-hairs."

Next, in the lung-pentad: having said "heart, liver, diaphragm, spleen, lungs," it should then be said in reverse: "lungs, spleen, diaphragm, liver, heart; kidneys, bone-marrow, bones, sinews, flesh; skin, teeth, nails, body-hairs, head-hairs."

Next, in the brain-pentad: having said "intestines, mesentery, stomach-contents, feces, brain," it should then be said in reverse: "brain, feces, stomach-contents, mesentery, intestines; lungs, spleen, diaphragm, liver, heart; kidneys, bone-marrow, bones, sinews, flesh; skin, teeth, nails, body-hairs, head-hairs."

Next, in the fat-hexad: having said "bile, phlegm, pus, blood, sweat, fat," it should then be said in reverse: "fat, sweat, blood, pus, phlegm, bile; brain, feces, stomach-contents, mesentery, intestines; lungs, spleen, diaphragm, liver, heart; kidneys, bone-marrow, bones, sinews, flesh; skin, teeth, nails, body-hairs, head-hairs."

Next, in the urine-hexad: having said "tears, oil-of-the-skin, saliva, nasal mucus, synovial fluid, urine," it should then be said in reverse: "urine, synovial fluid, nasal mucus, saliva, oil-of-the-skin, tears; fat, sweat, blood, pus, phlegm, bile; brain, feces, stomach-contents, mesentery, intestines; lungs, spleen, diaphragm, liver, heart; kidneys, bone-marrow, bones, sinews, flesh; skin, teeth, nails, body-hairs, head-hairs."

Thus, oral recitation should be performed a hundred times, a thousand times, even a hundred thousand times. For through oral recitation, the meditation subject's own text becomes thoroughly mastered, and the mind does not run about here and there; the parts become manifest, and appear as though linked together like the joints of the fingers, or like the boundary-markers of a fence.

Just as by voice, so too by mind, recitation should be performed. For oral recitation is a condition for mental recitation, and mental recitation is a condition for the penetration of the characteristic.

"By color" means: the color of head-hairs and the rest should be determined. "By shape" means: their shape too should be determined. "By direction" means: in this body, there is an upper direction above the navel and a lower direction below it; therefore it should be determined that "this part belongs to such-and-such a direction." "By location" means: it should be determined, "this part is situated in such-and-such a location." "By delimitation" means: there are two delimitations, delimitation by similar parts and delimitation by dissimilar parts. Herein, it should be understood as delimitation by similar parts that "this part is delimited below, above, and across by this much"; and it should be understood as delimitation by dissimilar parts, by way of non-admixture, that "head-hairs are not body-hairs, and body-hairs are not head-hairs."

In explaining the sevenfold skill in learning thus, the teacher should also explain, having ascertained it, that this meditation subject is taught by way of repulsiveness in one discourse and by way of the elements in another. For it is taught by way of repulsiveness alone in the Great Discourse on the Establishing of Mindfulness (D.N. 2.377); by way of the elements in the Simile of the Elephant's Footprint, the Greater Discourse of Advice to Rāhula, and the Discourse on the Exposition of the Elements. But in the Discourse on Mindfulness of the Body, with reference to one for whom it becomes manifest by way of color, the four jhānas are analyzed. Herein, what is taught by way of the elements is an insight meditation subject; what is taught by way of repulsiveness is a serenity meditation subject. This one, here, is precisely the serenity meditation subject.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The two elders' story is a striking testimony to the power of sheer memorized recitation independent of any other insight: despite already having mastered several complete divisions of the canon — a scholarly achievement most monks would never approach — they attained stream-entry through nothing more sophisticated than four months of repeating the same thirty-two-part list forward and backward. The chapter offers this not as an argument that scholarship is worthless, but as evidence that the sheer thoroughness and repetition of "thoroughgoing" (padakkhiṇaggāhitā) learning can itself become the decisive factor, regardless of how much prior textual knowledge one already possesses.

The forward-and-reverse recitation pattern, grouping the thirty-two parts into pentads and a hexad, is a genuinely effective memorization technique still recognizable to any student of mnemonics: reciting a list backward as well as forward forces a level of active engagement with each item's position that mere forward repetition does not, which is presumably why the chapter insists on it before any actual contemplation of repulsiveness can even begin.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
tacapañcaka

the skin-pentad — taca (skin) + pañcaka (a group of five); the first of six groups of the thirty-two body parts (hair, body-hair, nails, teeth, skin), each pentad or hexad recited as its own memorized unit before combining with the rest.

sabhāgaparicchedo / visabhāgaparicchedo

delimitation by similar parts / delimitation by dissimilar parts — the two-way analysis (already familiar from the earlier foulness-meditation chapter) applied here to each of the thirty-two body parts in turn: its own spatial boundary, and its distinctness from every other part.

padakkhiṇaggāhitā

thoroughgoing, complete mastery in learning — padakkhiṇa (circumambulation, going all the way around) + gāhitā (having grasped); the quality credited for the two elders' swift attainment despite the meditation subject's apparent simplicity.

The Tenfold Skill in Attention: A Monkey Among Palm Trees
