Beyond the sevenfold skill in learning, the chapter details ten further refinements needed to actually develop the meditation subject: proceeding in order without skipping, not too fast and not too slow, warding off outward distraction, going beyond the mere concept "hair, body-hair" to the repulsiveness itself, releasing whichever parts fail to appear clearly, and finally achieving absorption — illustrated by two vivid similes of increasingly narrowed focus: a hunter's monkey, chased back and forth across thirty-two palm trees until it finally settles on just one, and an almsround monk who receives progressively less food each day until, on the third day, his bowl is already full at the very first house.

Pali Source

Evaṃ sattadhā uggahakosallaṃ ācikkhitvā anupubbato, nātisīghato, nātisaṇikato, vikkhepapaṭibāhanato, paṇṇattisamatikkamanato, anupubbamuñcanato, appanāto, tayo ca suttantāti evaṃ dasadhā manasikārakosallaṃ ācikkhitabbaṃ. Tattha anupubbatoti idañhi sajjhāyakaraṇato paṭṭhāya anupaṭipāṭiyā manasikātabbaṃ, na ekantarikāya. Ekantarikāya hi manasikaronto yathā nāma akusalo puriso dvattiṃsapadaṃ nisseṇiṃ ekantarikāya ārohanto kilantakāyo patati, na ārohanaṃ sampādeti, evameva bhāvanāsampattivasena adhigantabbassa assādassa anadhigamā kilantacitto patati, na bhāvanaṃ sampādeti.

Anupubbato manasikarontenāpi ca nātisīghato manasikātabbaṃ. Atisīghato manasikaroto hi yathā nāma tiyojanamaggaṃ paṭipajjitvā okkamanavissajjanaṃ asallakkhetvā sīghena javena satakkhattumpi gamanāgamanaṃ karoto purisassa kiñcāpi addhānaṃ parikkhayaṃ gacchati, atha kho pucchitvāva gantabbaṃ hoti, evameva kevalaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ pariyosānaṃ pāpuṇāti, avibhūtaṃ pana hoti, na visesaṃ āvahati, tasmā nātisīghato manasikātabbaṃ.

Yathā ca nātisīghato, evaṃ nātisaṇikatopi. Atisaṇikato manasikaroto hi yathā nāma tadaheva tiyojanamaggaṃ gantukāmassa purisassa antarāmagge rukkhapabbatataḷākādīsu vilambamānassa maggo parikkhayaṃ na gacchati, dvīhatīhena pariyosāpetabbo hoti, evameva kammaṭṭhānaṃ pariyosānaṃ na gacchati, visesādhigamassa paccayo na hoti.

Vikkhepapaṭibāhanatoti kammaṭṭhānaṃ vissajjetvā bahiddhā puthuttārammaṇe cetaso vikkhepo paṭibāhitabbo. Appaṭibāhato hi yathā nāma ekapadikaṃ papātamaggaṃ paṭipannassa purisassa akkamanapadaṃ asallakkhetvā ito cito ca vilokayato padavāro virajjhati, tato sataporise papāte patitabbaṃ hoti, evameva bahiddhā vikkhepe sati kammaṭṭhānaṃ parihāyati paridhaṃsati. Tasmā vikkhepapaṭibāhanato manasikātabbaṃ.

Paṇṇattisamatikkamanatoti yāyaṃ kesā lomātiādikā paṇṇatti, taṃ atikkamitvā paṭikkūlanti cittaṃ ṭhapetabbaṃ. Yathā hi udakadullabhakāle manussā araññe udapānaṃ disvā tattha tālapaṇṇādikaṃ kiñcideva saññāṇaṃ bandhitvā tena saññāṇena āgantvā nhāyanti ceva pivanti ca. Yadā pana nesaṃ abhiṇhasañcārena āgatāgatapadaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti, tadā saññāṇena kiccaṃ na hoti, icchiticchitakkhaṇe gantvā nhāyanti ceva pivanti ca, evameva pubbabhāge kesā lomātipaṇṇattivasena manasikaroto paṭikkūlabhāvo pākaṭo hoti. Atha kesā lomātipaṇṇattiṃ samatikkamitvā paṭikkūlabhāveyeva cittaṃ ṭhapetabbaṃ.

Anupubbamuñcanatoti yo yo koṭṭhāso na upaṭṭhāti, taṃ taṃ muñcantena anupubbamuñcanato manasikātabbaṃ. Ādikammikassa hi kesāti manasikaroto manasikāro gantvā muttanti imaṃ pariyosānakoṭṭhāsameva āhacca tiṭṭhati. Muttanti ca manasikaroto manasikāro gantvā kesāti imaṃ ādikoṭṭhāsameva āhacca tiṭṭhati. Athassa manasikaroto manasikaroto keci koṭṭhāsā upaṭṭhahanti, keci na upaṭṭhahanti. Tena ye ye upaṭṭhahanti, tesu tesu tāva kammaṃ kātabbaṃ. Yāva dvīsu upaṭṭhitesu tesampi eko suṭṭhutaraṃ upaṭṭhahati, evaṃ upaṭṭhitaṃ pana tameva punappunaṃ manasikarontena appanā uppādetabbā.

Tatrāyaṃ upamā — yathā hi dvattiṃsatālake tālavane vasantaṃ makkaṭaṃ gahetukāmo luddo ādimhi ṭhitatālassa paṇṇaṃ sarena vijjhitvā ukkuṭṭhiṃ kareyya, atha kho so makkaṭo paṭipāṭiyā tasmiṃ tasmiṃ tāle patitvā pariyantatālameva gaccheyya, tatthapi gantvā luddena tatheva kate puna teneva nayena āditālaṃ āgaccheyya, so evaṃ punappunaṃ paripātiyamāno ukkuṭṭhukkuṭṭhiṭṭhāneyeva uṭṭhahitvā anukkamena ekasmiṃ tāle nipatitvā tassa vemajjhe makuḷatālapaṇṇasūciṃ daḷhaṃ gahetvā vijjhiyamānopi na uṭṭhaheyya, evaṃsampadamidaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ.

Tatridaṃ opammasaṃsandanaṃ — yathā hi tālavane dvattiṃsatālā, evaṃ imasmiṃ kāye dvattiṃsakoṭṭhāsā. Makkaṭo viya cittaṃ. Luddo viya yogāvacaro. Makkaṭassa dvattiṃsatālake tālavane nivāso viya yogino cittassa dvattiṃsakoṭṭhāsake kāye ārammaṇavasena anusañcaraṇaṃ. Luddena ādimhi ṭhitatālassa paṇṇaṃ sarena vijjhitvā ukkuṭṭhiyā katāya makkaṭassa tasmiṃ tasmiṃ tāle patitvā pariyantatālagamanaṃ viya yogino kesāti manasikāre āraddhe paṭipāṭiyā gantvā pariyosānakoṭṭhāseyeva cittassa saṇṭhānaṃ. Puna paccāgamanepi eseva nayo. Punappunaṃ paripātiyamānassa makkaṭassa ukkuṭṭhukkuṭṭhiṭṭhāne uṭṭhānaṃ viya punappunaṃ manasikaroto kesuci kesuci upaṭṭhitesu anupaṭṭhahante vissajjetvā upaṭṭhitesu parikammakaraṇaṃ. Anukkamena ekasmiṃ tāle nipatitvā tassa majjhe makuḷatālapaṇṇasūciṃ daḷhaṃ gahetvā vijjhiyamānassapi anuṭṭhānaṃ viya avasāne dvīsu upaṭṭhitesu yo suṭṭhutaraṃ upaṭṭhāti, tameva punappunaṃ manasikaritvā appanāya uppādanaṃ.

Aparāpi upamā — yathā nāma piṇḍapātiko bhikkhu dvattiṃsakulaṃ gāmaṃ upanissāya vasanto paṭhamageheyeva dve bhikkhā labhitvā parato ekaṃ vissajjeyya. Punadivase tisso labhitvā parato dve vissajjeyya. Tatiyadivase ādimhiyeva pattapūraṃ labhitvā āsanasālaṃ gantvā paribhuñjeyya. Evaṃsampadamidaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ. Dvattiṃsakulagāmo viya hi dvattiṃsākāro. Piṇḍapātiko viya yogāvacaro. Tassa taṃ gāmaṃ upanissāya vāso viya yogino dvattiṃsākāre parikammakaraṇaṃ. Paṭhamagehe dve bhikkhā labhitvā parato ekissā vissajjanaṃ viya dutiyadivase tisso labhitvā parato dvinnaṃ vissajjanaṃ viya ca manasikaroto manasikaroto anupaṭṭhahante vissajjetvā upaṭṭhitesu yāva koṭṭhāsadvaye parikammakaraṇaṃ. Tatiyadivase ādimhiyeva pattapūraṃ labhitvā āsanasālāyaṃ nisīditvā paribhogo viya dvīsu yo suṭṭhutaraṃ upaṭṭhāti, tameva punappunaṃ manasikaritvā appanāya uppādanaṃ.

Appanātoti appanākoṭṭhāsato kesādīsu ekekasmiṃ koṭṭhāse appanā hotīti veditabbāti ayamevettha adhippāyo.

Tayo ca suttantāti adhicittaṃ, sītibhāvo, bojjhaṅgakosallanti ime tayo suttantā vīriyasamādhiyojanatthaṃ veditabbāti ayamettha adhippāyo.

Ru-Yi Translation

Having thus explained the sevenfold skill in learning, the tenfold skill in attention should also be explained: proceeding in order, not too quickly, not too slowly, warding off distraction, going beyond the mere concept, releasing them in succession, by way of absorption, and the three discourses. Herein, "in order" means: it should be attended to in due sequence, beginning from the recitation, not by skipping alternately. For one attending to it by skipping alternately is like an unskilled man climbing a thirty-two-runged ladder by skipping every other rung, who falls exhausted before completing the climb — just so, without reaching the enjoyment to be attained through the accomplishment of development, one's mind grows weary and falls away, failing to accomplish the development.

And even one attending in due order should not attend too quickly. For one attending too quickly is like a man setting out on a three-league road, who, without noting the side-paths and turnings, travels back and forth swiftly even a hundred times — though the distance itself is thereby used up, he must still go and ask the way. Just so, the meditation subject merely reaches its conclusion, but remains unclear and brings no distinction. Therefore it should not be attended to too quickly.

And just as not too quickly, so too not too slowly. For one attending too slowly is like a man wishing to travel a three-league road that same day, who, lingering along the way at trees, mountains, and lakes, does not use up the distance, and must complete the journey in two or three days instead. Just so, the meditation subject does not reach its conclusion, and does not become a condition for the attainment of distinction.

"Warding off distraction" means: having let go of the meditation subject, the mind's distraction toward a variety of external objects should be warded off. For, without warding it off, one is like a man walking a single-track path along a precipice who, without noting where to place his footsteps, looks this way and that, misses his footing, and must fall down a hundred-man-deep precipice. Just so, when there is external distraction, the meditation subject deteriorates and collapses. Therefore it should be attended to by way of warding off distraction.

"Going beyond the mere concept" means: the mind should be fixed on repulsiveness, going beyond the concept "hair, body-hair," and so on. For just as, at a time when water is scarce, people, having seen a well in the forest, tie some marker, such as a palm-leaf, there, and, by means of that marker, come and bathe and drink — but when, through their constant coming and going, the path there and back becomes evident, the marker is no longer needed, and they go and bathe and drink at whatever moment they wish — just so, in the earlier stage, for one attending by way of the concept "hair, body-hair," the state of repulsiveness becomes evident; then, having gone beyond the concept "hair, body-hair," the mind should be fixed on the state of repulsiveness itself.

"Releasing them in succession" means: whichever part fails to appear should be released, attending by way of releasing them one after another. For a beginner, attending "hair," his attention, proceeding onward, comes to rest precisely on that final part, "urine." And, attending "urine," his attention, proceeding onward, comes to rest precisely on that first part, "hair." Then, as he attends again and again, some parts become manifest and some do not. Of these, work should be done, first, on whichever ones do become manifest. Once two of these have become manifest, one of even these two becomes more clearly manifest than the other; and, attending again and again to that very one thus manifest, absorption should be produced.

Here is a simile: just as a hunter, wishing to catch a monkey living in a grove of thirty-two palm trees, might pierce the leaf of the tree standing first with an arrow and give a shout — whereupon that monkey, leaping in succession from tree to tree, would go all the way to the last tree, and, the hunter having done the same there too, would by that same method come back to the first tree — and, being thus made to leap back and forth again and again, would eventually, right at the very spot where the shout is given, leap up, and, alighting by degrees upon one tree, would firmly seize the sharp tip of a young palm-leaf in its middle and, even when pierced there, would no longer leap up — just so should this be understood.

Here is the correspondence of the simile: just as there are thirty-two palm trees in the grove, so are there thirty-two parts in this body. The mind is like the monkey; the meditator is like the hunter. The monkey's dwelling in the grove of thirty-two palm trees is like the meditator's mind ranging, by way of its object, throughout the body of thirty-two parts. The monkey's leaping from tree to tree, all the way to the last tree, after the hunter has pierced the leaf of the first tree with an arrow and given a shout, is like the mind's settling precisely on the last part, once the meditator, attending "hair," has proceeded in due order. The same applies to the return journey. The monkey's leaping up again and again, right at the very spot where the shout is given, is like the meditator's doing preparatory work on whichever parts do become manifest, having released those that do not, as he attends again and again. The monkey's eventually alighting by degrees upon one tree, firmly seizing the sharp tip of a young palm-leaf, and no longer leaping up even when pierced there, is like the production of absorption through attending again and again to whichever of two manifest parts becomes the more clearly manifest, in the end.

Here is a further simile: just as an almsround monk, dwelling in dependence on a village of thirty-two families, might, having received two portions of alms-food at the very first house, give up asking at one further house; and, on the next day, having received three portions, give up asking at two further houses; and, on the third day, having received a bowlful right at the very first house, go to the sitting-hall and eat there — just so should this be understood. The village of thirty-two families is like the thirty-two aspects; the almsround monk is like the meditator; his dwelling in dependence on that village is like the meditator's preparatory work on the thirty-two aspects. Receiving two portions at the first house and giving up one further house, and, the next day, receiving three and giving up two, is like doing preparatory work, as he attends again and again, on up to two parts, having released those that do not become manifest, until two do. Receiving a bowlful right at the first house on the third day, and going to the sitting-hall to eat, is like the production of absorption through attending again and again to whichever of the two becomes the more clearly manifest, in the end.

"By way of absorption" means precisely this: it should be understood that absorption occurs in one or another single part among hair and the rest, reckoned as the part of absorption — this alone is the intent here.

"The three discourses" means: the discourses on higher mind, on coolness, and on skill in the enlightenment factors — these three discourses should be understood as intended for the purpose of applying energy and concentration in the right measure.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The two similes, though pointed at the identical underlying process — narrowing attention from thirty-two objects down to one — are chosen to illustrate two genuinely different phases of that narrowing. The hunted monkey depicts the frantic, exhausting back-and-forth movement of a mind still ranging over the entire object before settling; the almsround monk depicts something calmer and more incremental — a steady, day-by-day reduction achieved simply by giving up the search once enough has already been found. Together they cover both the turbulent and the gradual routes by which the same convergence on a single part can occur.

The well-marker simile for "going beyond the mere concept" makes a precise point about how a conceptual label functions only as scaffolding, not as the destination: the palm-leaf marker is genuinely useful for a stranger still learning where the well is, but becomes actively unnecessary — even slightly in the way — once the path is already known by heart. "Hair, body-hair" functions the same way for the meditator: indispensable at first for locating the object, but eventually something the mind should move past entirely, resting directly on the perceived repulsiveness itself rather than continuing to rehearse the naming words.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
paṇṇattisamatikkamana

going beyond the mere concept — paṇṇatti (a concept, a designation) + samatikkamana (surmounting, going beyond); the specific skill of eventually letting go of the naming-words themselves once they have served their purpose of locating the repulsiveness they point to.

anupubbamuñcana

releasing in succession — anupubba (successive) + muñcana (releasing, letting go); the practical triage method of abandoning attention to parts that fail to appear clearly, in favor of concentrating effort on whichever ones do.

ukkuṭṭhi

a shout, a war-cry — the hunter's signal in the monkey simile, marking the specific spot the monkey is startled from at each stage of the chase, corresponding to the meditator's attention beginning again at "hair."

Hair: The Complete Fivefold Analysis of Repulsiveness

Before working through all thirty-two parts, the chapter establishes its full method on the very first one — head-hair — determining it by color, shape, direction, location, and delimitation, then showing it to be repulsive in five distinct ways: by color, shape, smell, origin, and location, each demonstrated through a vivid comparison (a strand of hair found in one's food, a young child's excrement compared to an old dog's bloated corpse). Every one of the remaining thirty-one parts will be treated by this identical method, so this section serves as the template for the entire chapter that follows.

Pali Source

Karontena pana kesesu tāva nimittaṃ gahetabbaṃ. Kathaṃ? Ekaṃ vā dve vā kese luñcitvā hatthatale ṭhapetvā vaṇṇo tāva vavatthapetabbo. Chinnaṭṭhānepi kese oloketuṃ vaṭṭati. Udakapatte vā yāgupatte vā oloketumpi vaṭṭatiyeva. Kāḷakakāle disvā kāḷakāti manasikātabbā. Setakāle setāti. Missakakāle pana ussadavasena manasikātabbā honti. Yathā ca kesesu, evaṃ sakalepi tacapañcake disvāva nimittaṃ gahetabbaṃ.

Evaṃ nimittaṃ gahetvā sabbakoṭṭhāse vaṇṇasaṇṭhānadisokāsaparicchedavasena vavatthapetvā vaṇṇasaṇṭhānagandhaāsayokāsavasena pañcadhā paṭikkūlato vavatthapetabbā.

Tatrāyaṃ sabbakoṭṭhāsesu anupubbakathā. Kesā tāva pakativaṇṇena kāḷakā addāriṭṭhakavaṇṇā. Saṇṭhānato dīghavaṭṭalikā tulādaṇḍasaṇṭhānā. Disato uparimadisāya jātā. Okāsato ubhosu passesu kaṇṇacūḷikāhi, purato nalāṭantena, pacchato galavāṭakena paricchinnā. Sīsakaṭāhaveṭhanaṃ allacammaṃ kesānaṃ okāso. Paricchedato kesā sīsaveṭhanacamme vīhaggamattaṃ pavisitvā patiṭṭhitena heṭṭhā attano mūlatalena, upari ākāsena, tiriyaṃ aññamaññena paricchinnā, dve kesā ekato natthīti ayaṃ sabhāgaparicchedo. Kesā na lomā, lomā na kesāti evaṃ avasesaekatiṃsakoṭṭhāsehi amissīkatā kesā nāma pāṭiyekko ekakoṭṭhāsoti ayaṃ visabhāgaparicchedo. Idaṃ kesānaṃ vaṇṇādito vavatthāpanaṃ.

Idaṃ pana nesaṃ vaṇṇādivasena pañcadhā paṭikkūlato vavatthāpanaṃ. Kesā nāmete vaṇṇatopi paṭikkūlā. Saṇṭhānatopi gandhatopi āsayatopi okāsatopi paṭikkūlā.

Manuññepi hi yāgupatte vā bhattapatte vā kesavaṇṇaṃ kiñci disvā kesamissakamidaṃ haratha nanti jigucchanti. Evaṃ kesā vaṇṇato paṭikkūlā. Rattiṃ bhuñjantāpi kesasaṇṭhānaṃ akkavākaṃ vā makacivākaṃ vā chupitvāpi tatheva jigucchanti. Evaṃ saṇṭhānato paṭikkūlā.

Telamakkhanapupphadhūpādi saṅkhāravirahitānañca kesānaṃ gandho paramajeguccho hoti. Tato jegucchataro aggimhi pakkhittānaṃ. Kesā hi vaṇṇasaṇṭhānato appaṭikkūlāpi siyuṃ, gandhena pana paṭikkūlāyeva. Yathā hi daharassa kumārassa vaccaṃ vaṇṇato haliddivaṇṇaṃ, saṇṭhānatopi haliddipiṇḍasaṇṭhānaṃ. Saṅkāraṭṭhāne chaḍḍitañca uddhumātakakāḷasunakhasarīraṃ vaṇṇato tālapakkavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato vaṭṭetvā vissaṭṭhamudiṅgasaṇṭhānaṃ. Dāṭhāpissa sumanamakuḷasadisāti ubhayampi vaṇṇasaṇṭhānato siyā appaṭikkūlaṃ gandhena pana paṭikkūlameva. Evaṃ kesāpi siyuṃ vaṇṇasaṇṭhānato appaṭikkūlā gandhena pana paṭikkūlāyevāti.

Yathā pana asuciṭṭhāne gāmanissandena jātāni sūpeyyapaṇṇāni nāgarikamanussānaṃ jegucchāni honti aparibhogāni, evaṃ kesāpi pubbalohitamuttakarīsapittasemhādinissandena jātattā jegucchāti idaṃ nesaṃ āsayato pāṭikkulyaṃ.

Ime ca kesā nāma gūtharāsimhi uṭṭhitakaṇṇikaṃ viya ekatiṃsakoṭṭhāsarāsimhi jātā. Te susānasaṅkāraṭṭhānādīsu jātasākaṃ viya parikkhādīsu jātakamalakuvalayādipupphaṃ viya ca asuciṭṭhāne jātattā paramajegucchāti idaṃ nesaṃ okāsato pāṭikkulyaṃ.

Yathā ca kesānaṃ, evaṃ sabbakoṭṭhāsānaṃ vaṇṇasaṇṭhānagandhāsayokāsavasena pañcadhā paṭikkūlatā veditabbā. Vaṇṇasaṇṭhānadisokāsaparicchedavasena pana sabbepi visuṃ visuṃ vavatthapetabbā.

Ru-Yi Translation

In doing this, the sign should first be grasped in the hair. How? Having plucked out one or two hairs and placed them on the palm of the hand, the color should first be determined. It is also proper to look at the hair on the spot where it has been cut, or to look at it in a bowl of water or of rice-gruel. Seeing it black in color, it should be attended to as "black"; white, as "white"; and when mixed, it should be attended to by way of its predominant color. And just as with the hair, so too the sign should be grasped by seeing it throughout the whole of the skin-pentad.

Having grasped the sign in this way, having determined all the parts by way of color, shape, direction, location, and delimitation, they should then be determined as repulsive in five ways: by color, shape, smell, origin, and location.

Here, this is the discussion proceeding in order through all the parts. First, head-hairs: by their natural color, they are black, the color of a fresh myrobalan seed. By shape, they are long and round, in the shape of a measuring-rod. By direction, they arise in the upper direction. By location, they are bounded, on both sides, by the ear-lobes, in front by the edge of the forehead, and behind by the nape of the neck; the moist skin wrapping the skull is the location of the hairs. By delimitation: the hairs, having entered a rice-grain's depth into the skin wrapping the skull and become fixed there, are delimited below by their own root-surface, above by space, and across by one another — no two hairs occupy the same spot; this is their delimitation by similar parts. That head-hairs are hair alone, unmixed with the remaining thirty-one parts — "hair is not body-hair, body-hair is not hair" — is their delimitation by dissimilar parts, by way of non-admixture. This is the determination of the hairs by way of color and the rest.

This, now, is their determination as repulsive in five ways, by way of color and the rest. Hairs, indeed, are repulsive by color; they are also repulsive by shape, by smell, by origin, and by location.

For even in agreeable rice-gruel or rice, on seeing some hair-colored thing, people are disgusted, saying, "take away this thing mixed with hair" — thus hairs are repulsive by color. Even those eating at night, having touched something of a hair-like shape, such as an akka-fiber or a hemp-fiber, are disgusted in just the same way — thus they are repulsive by shape.

And the smell of hairs devoid of such preparation as oiling, flower-scenting, or perfuming with incense, is extremely disgusting; more disgusting still is the smell of hairs thrown into a fire. For hairs might well be unrepulsive by color and shape, yet are repulsive by smell nonetheless. For just as a young child's excrement is, in color, the color of turmeric, and, in shape, the shape of a lump of turmeric; and just as a bloated black dog's corpse, thrown away in a rubbish-heap, is, in color, the color of a ripe palm-fruit, and, in shape, rounded like a drum laid on its side, with its fangs resembling jasmine buds — both of these might well be unrepulsive by color and shape, yet are repulsive by smell nonetheless; just so, hairs too might be unrepulsive by color and shape, yet are repulsive by smell nonetheless.

And just as vegetables suitable for curry, grown from the outflow of a village at an impure spot, are disgusting and unusable to city-dwelling people, so too hairs, having grown from the outflow of pus, blood, urine, feces, bile, phlegm, and the like, are disgusting — this is their repulsiveness by origin.

And these hairs, growing in the mass of the thirty-one remaining parts as though a tuft sprouting up from a heap of dung, are extremely disgusting through having grown at an impure location, like a vegetable grown in a charnel ground or a rubbish-heap, or like a lotus or blue water-lily growing amid refuse — this is their repulsiveness by location.

And just as with the hairs, so too the fivefold repulsiveness of all the parts should be understood, by way of color, shape, smell, origin, and location. And all of them, likewise, should be individually determined by way of color, shape, direction, location, and delimitation.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The five-way repulsiveness analysis established here for hair is deliberately exhaustive precisely so it need not be repeated in full for the remaining thirty-one parts: color, shape, smell, origin, and location are shown to be genuinely independent axes — hair could in principle be neutral or even attractive by color and shape alone, and the argument specifically shows that smell by itself is sufficient to establish repulsiveness even when the other criteria fail, using the child's-excrement and dead-dog comparisons to prove the point vividly.

The distinction between repulsiveness "by origin" and "by location" is a fine one worth noting: origin concerns what a thing grew out of (hair growing from an outflow of bodily impurities, like a vegetable irrigated by sewage), while location concerns where it currently sits (hair growing amid the other thirty-one impure parts, like a lotus growing in a rubbish-heap). The two similes are chosen to be structurally parallel — both involve something that might be beautiful in itself (a vegetable, a lotus flower) rendered repulsive purely by the impurity of its growing conditions — making explicit that the parts' own individual appearance is never really the issue; their context is.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ussadavasena

by way of the predominant [color] — the instruction for handling hair of mixed color (some black, some white), attending to whichever color predominates rather than trying to register every strand individually.

vaṇṇato saṇṭhānato gandhato āsayato okāsato

by color, by shape, by smell, by origin, by location — the standing fivefold template for demonstrating each of the thirty-two body parts' repulsiveness, established here in full on hair and then applied more briefly to every subsequent part.

āsaya

origin, source, outflow — distinguished here from okāsa (location): a part's āsaya is what it grew out of (bodily fluids), while its okāsa is where it currently sits (amid the other thirty-one parts).

Body-Hair, Nails, Teeth, and Skin

The chapter completes the "skin-pentad" — the first group of five parts recited together — determining body-hair, the twenty nail-plates, the thirty-two teeth-bones, and the skin each by color, shape, direction, location, and delimitation, closing with the observation that skin's own famous whiteness is normally hidden beneath a thin, darker outer layer, becoming visible only where that outer layer has been destroyed by fire or a wound.

Pali Source

Tattha lomā tāva pakativaṇṇato na kesā viya asambhinnakāḷakā, kāḷapiṅgalā pana honti. Saṇṭhānato onataggā tālamūlasaṇṭhānā. Disato dvīsu disāsu jātā. Okāsato ṭhapetvā kesānaṃ patiṭṭhitokāsañca hatthapādatalāni ca yebhuyyena avasesasarīraveṭhanacamme jātā. Paricchedato sarīraveṭhanacamme likhāmattaṃ pavisitvā patiṭṭhitena heṭṭhā attano mūlatalena, upari ākāsena, tiriyaṃ aññamaññena paricchinnā, dve lomā ekato natthi, ayaṃ nesaṃ sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Nakhāti vīsatiyā nakhapattānaṃ nāmaṃ. Te sabbepi vaṇṇato setā. Saṇṭhānato macchasakalikasaṇṭhānā. Disato pādanakhā heṭṭhimadisāya, hatthanakhā uparimadisāyāti dvīsu disāsu jātā. Okāsato aṅgulīnaṃ aggapiṭṭhesu patiṭṭhitā. Paricchedato dvīsu disāsu aṅgulikoṭimaṃsehi, anto aṅgulipiṭṭhimaṃsena, bahi ceva agge ca ākāsena, tiriyaṃ aññamaññena paricchinnā, dve nakhā ekato natthi, ayaṃ nesaṃ sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Dantāti paripuṇṇadantassa dvattiṃsa dantaṭṭhikāni. Tepi vaṇṇato setā. Saṇṭhānato anekasaṇṭhānā. Tesaṃ hi heṭṭhimāya tāva dantapāḷiyā majjhe cattāro dantā mattikāpiṇḍe paṭipāṭiyā ṭhapitaalābubījasaṇṭhānā. Tesaṃ ubhosu passesu ekeko ekamūlako ekakoṭiko mallikamakuḷasaṇṭhāno. Tato ekeko dvimūlako dvikoṭiko yānakaupatthambhinisaṇṭhāno. Tato dve dve timūlā tikoṭikā. Tato dve dve catumūlā catukoṭikāti. Uparimapāḷiyāpi eseva nayo. Disato uparimadisāya jātā. Okāsato dvīsu hanukaṭṭhikesu patiṭṭhitā. Paricchedato heṭṭhā hanukaṭṭhike patiṭṭhitena attano mūlatalena, upari ākāsena, tiriyaṃ aññamaññena paricchinnā, dve dantā ekato natthi, ayaṃ nesaṃ sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Tacoti sakalasarīraṃ veṭhetvā ṭhitacammaṃ. Tassa upari kāḷasāmapītādivaṇṇā chavi nāma yā sakalasarīratopi saṅkaḍḍhiyamānā badaraṭṭhimattā hoti. Taco pana vaṇṇato setoyeva. So cassa setabhāvo aggijālābhighātapaharaṇappahārādīhi viddhaṃsitāya chaviyā pākaṭo hoti. Saṇṭhānato sarīrasaṇṭhānova hoti. Ayamettha saṅkhepo.

Vitthārato pana pādaṅgulittaco kosakārakakosasaṇṭhāno. Piṭṭhipādattaco puṭabandhaupāhanasaṇṭhāno. Jaṅghattaco bhattapuṭakatālapaṇṇasaṇṭhāno. Ūruttaco taṇḍulabharitadīghatthavikasaṇṭhāno. Ānisadattaco udakapūritapaṭaparissāvanasaṇṭhāno. Piṭṭhittaco phalakonaddhacammasaṇṭhāno. Kucchittaco vīṇādoṇikonaddhacammasaṇṭhāno. Urattaco yebhuyyena caturassasaṇṭhāno. Ubhayabāhuttaco tūṇironaddhacammasaṇṭhāno. Piṭṭhihatthattaco khurakosasaṇṭhāno, phaṇakatthavikasaṇṭhāno vā. Hatthaṅgulittaco kuñcikākosakasaṇṭhāno. Gīvattaco galakañcukasaṇṭhāno. Mukhattaco chiddāvachiddo kīṭakulāvakasaṇṭhāno. Sīsattaco pattatthavikasaṇṭhānoti.

Disato dvīsu disāsu jāto. Okāsato sakalasarīraṃ pariyonandhitvā ṭhito. Paricchedato heṭṭhā patiṭṭhitatalena, upari ākāsena paricchinno, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Ru-Yi Translation

Herein, first, body-hairs: by natural color, unlike hairs, they are not uniformly black, but rather dark brown. By shape, they curve at the tip, in the shape of a palm-tree root. By direction, they arise in both directions. By location, apart from the location occupied by the hairs and the palms of the hands and soles of the feet, they grow for the most part on the remaining skin covering the body. By delimitation: having entered the skin covering the body to the depth of a mere scratch and become fixed there, they are delimited below by their own root-surface, above by space, and across by one another — no two body-hairs occupy the same spot; this is their delimitation by similar parts. Their delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Nails is the name for the twenty nail-plates. All of them, by color, are white. By shape, they are in the shape of fish-scales. By direction, the toenails arise in the lower direction, the fingernails in the upper direction — thus in both directions. By location, they are fixed on the upper surfaces of the fingers and toes. By delimitation: they are delimited, on both sides, by the flesh at the tips of the fingers and toes, within by the flesh on the back of the fingers and toes, without and at the tip by space, and across by one another — no two nails occupy the same spot; this is their delimitation by similar parts. Their delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Teeth: for one of complete dentition, thirty-two tooth-bones. These too, by color, are white. By shape, of various shapes. For, first, in the lower row of teeth, the middle four teeth are in the shape of gourd-seeds set in succession in a lump of clay. On both sides of these, one tooth each has a single root and a single point, in the shape of a jasmine bud. Next to that, one tooth each has two roots and two points, in the shape of a cart's supporting prop. Next, two teeth each have three roots and three points. Next, two teeth each have four roots and four points. The same method applies to the upper row as well. By direction, they arise in the upper direction. By location, they are fixed in the two jawbones. By delimitation: fixed below in the jawbone, they are delimited by their own root-surface, above by space, and across by one another — no two teeth occupy the same spot; this is their delimitation by similar parts. Their delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Skin is the hide that stands wrapping the whole body. On its surface is what is called the "cuticle," black, tawny, or yellow in color and the like, which, gathered together from the entire body, amounts to no more than the size of a jujube-stone. Skin itself, however, is white in color; and this whiteness of it becomes evident when the cuticle has been destroyed by the blast of a flame or by the blow of a weapon. By shape, it is simply the shape of the body. This, herein, is the summary.

In detail, however: the skin of the toes is in the shape of a silk-worm's cocoon. The skin of the top of the foot is in the shape of a shoe stitched with a covered toe-piece. The skin of the calf is in the shape of a rice-wrapped palm-leaf packet. The skin of the thigh is in the shape of a long sack filled with rice. The skin of the buttocks is in the shape of a water-filled straining-cloth. The skin of the back is in the shape of a hide stretched over a plank. The skin of the flank is in the shape of a hide stretched over the resonating-body of a lute. The skin of the chest is, for the most part, in the shape of a square. The skin of both arms is in the shape of a hide-covered quiver. The skin of the back of the hand is in the shape of a razor-case, or the shape of a comb-case. The skin of the fingers is in the shape of a key-case. The skin of the neck is in the shape of a bodice covering the throat. The skin of the face is riddled with holes, like the nest of a certain insect. The skin of the head is in the shape of a bowl-cover.

By direction, it arises in both directions. By location, it stands wrapping the entire body. By delimitation: it is delimited below by its own fixed surface, and above by space; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The extensive catalogue of shapes for skin at different parts of the body — a cocoon for the toes, a rice-sack for the thigh, a quiver for the arms, a bowl-cover for the head — is an unusually granular exercise even by this treatise's standards, moving well past the fivefold analytical template into pure, itemized observation. Its purpose is the same throughout: replacing an intuitive, unified sense of "my skin" with a decomposed inventory of separate, oddly-shaped coverings, none of which individually resembles anything a person would ordinarily find attractive.

The clarification that skin's own famous whiteness is ordinarily hidden beneath a thin, darker "cuticle" — visible only once that outer layer has been burned or wounded away — quietly undercuts the very cliché the meditation is working against: the poetic praise of "fair skin" in ordinary speech is, on this analysis, praise not of the skin itself but of a superficial film sitting on top of it, with the true skin beneath only becoming visible under injury.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
chavi

the cuticle, the outer skin-film — distinguished here from taco (skin proper): a thin, colored surface layer that conceals skin's actual whiteness, visible only where destroyed by burning or wounding.

nakhapatta

a nail-plate — nakha (nail) + patta (a plate, a leaf); the term for each of the twenty fingernails and toenails, described by the same fivefold color/shape/direction/location/delimitation method as every other part.

badaraṭṭhi

a jujube-stone — badara (jujube fruit) + aṭṭhi (stone, pit); the unit of comparison for the total volume of cuticle gathered from the entire body, emphasizing how little of it there actually is despite its visual prominence.

Flesh, Sinews, Bones, Bone-Marrow, and Kidneys

The chapter turns to the body's structural substance: nine hundred pieces of flesh described part by part through comparisons to flame-of-the-forest flowers and lumps of clay, sixty major tendons running through the body like woven cords of every thickness, more than three hundred bones catalogued individually — down to the precise shape of each finger joint and rib — through comparisons to jackfruit seeds, drumsticks, and old snake-spines, the marrow packed inside them, and finally the paired kidneys joined by a single stalk like twin mangoes on one stem.

Pali Source

Maṃsanti nava maṃsapesisatāni. Taṃ sabbampi vaṇṇato rattaṃ kiṃsukapupphasadisaṃ. Saṇṭhānato jaṅghapiṇḍikamaṃsaṃ tālapaṇṇapuṭabhattasaṇṭhānaṃ. Ūrumaṃsaṃ nisadapotasaṇṭhānaṃ. Ānisadamaṃsaṃ uddhanakoṭisaṇṭhānaṃ. Piṭṭhimaṃsaṃ tālaguḷapaṭalasaṇṭhānaṃ. Phāsukadvayamaṃsaṃ koṭṭhalikāya kucchiyaṃ tanumattikālepasaṇṭhānaṃ. Thanamaṃsaṃ vaṭṭetvā avakkhittamattikāpiṇḍasaṇṭhānaṃ. Bāhudvayamaṃsaṃ dviguṇaṃ katvā ṭhapitaniccammamahāmūsikasaṇṭhānaṃ. Evaṃ oḷārikoḷārikaṃ pariggaṇhantassa sukhumampi pākaṭaṃ hoti. Disato dvīsu disāsu jātaṃ. Okāsato vīsādhikāni tīṇi aṭṭhisatāni anulimpitvā ṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato heṭṭhā aṭṭhisaṅghāte patiṭṭhitatalena, upari tacena, tiriyaṃ aññamaññena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Nhārūti nava nhārusatāni. Vaṇṇato sabbepi nhārū setā. Saṇṭhānato nānāsaṇṭhānā. Etesu hi gīvāya uparimabhāgato paṭṭhāya pañca mahānhārū sarīraṃ vinandhamānā purimapassena otiṇṇā. Pañca pacchimapassena. Pañca dakkhiṇapassena. Pañca vāmapassena. Dakkhiṇahatthaṃ vinandhamānāpi hatthassa purimapassena pañca. Pacchimapassena pañca. Tathā vāmahatthaṃ vinandhamānā. Dakkhiṇapādaṃ vinandhamānāpi pādassa purimapassena pañca. Pacchimapassena pañca. Tathā vāmapādaṃ vinandhamānāpīti evaṃ sarīradhārakā nāma saṭṭhimahānhārū kāyaṃ vinandhamānā otiṇṇā. Ye kaṇḍarātipi vuccanti. Te sabbepi kandalamakuḷasaṇṭhānā. Aññe pana taṃ taṃ padesaṃ ajjhottharitvā ṭhitā. Tato sukhumatarā suttarajjukasaṇṭhānā. Aññe tato sukhumatarā pūtilatāsaṇṭhānā, aññe tato sukhumatarā mahāvīṇātantisaṇṭhānā. Aññe thūlasuttakasaṇṭhānā. Hatthapādapiṭṭhīsu nhārū sakuṇapādasaṇṭhānā. Sīse nhārū dārakānaṃ sīsajālakasaṇṭhānā. Piṭṭhiyaṃ nhārū ātape pasāritaallajālasaṇṭhānā. Avasesā taṃtaṃaṅgapaccaṅgānugatā nhārū sarīre paṭimukkajālakañcukasaṇṭhānā. Disato dvīsu disāsu jātā. Okāsato sakalasarīre aṭṭhīni ābandhitvā ṭhitā. Paricchedato heṭṭhā tiṇṇaṃ aṭṭhisatānaṃ upari patiṭṭhitatalehi, upari maṃsacammāni āhacca ṭhitappadesehi, tiriyaṃ aññamaññena paricchinnā, ayaṃ nesaṃ sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Aṭṭhīti ṭhapetvā dvattiṃsa dantaṭṭhīni avasesāni catusaṭṭhi hatthaṭṭhīni, catusaṭṭhi pādaṭṭhīni, catusaṭṭhi maṃsanissitāni muduaṭṭhīni, dve paṇhikaṭṭhīni, ekekasmiṃ pāde dve dve gopphakaṭṭhīni, dve jaṅghaṭṭhīni, ekaṃ jaṇṇukaṭṭhi, ekaṃ ūruṭṭhi, dve kaṭiṭṭhīni, aṭṭhārasa piṭṭhikaṇṭakaṭṭhīni, catuvīsati phāsukaṭṭhīni, cuddasa uraṭṭhīni, ekaṃ hadayaṭṭhi, dve akkhakaṭṭhīni, dve koṭṭaṭṭhīni, dve bāhuṭṭhīni, dve dve aggabāhuṭṭhīni, satta gīvaṭṭhīni, dve hanukaṭṭhīni, ekaṃ nāsikaṭṭhi, dve akkhiṭṭhīni, dve kaṇṇaṭṭhīni, ekaṃ nalāṭaṭṭhi. Ekaṃ muddhaṭṭhi, nava sīsakapālaṭṭhīnīti evaṃ timattāni aṭṭhisatāni, tāni sabbānipi vaṇṇato setāni. Saṇṭhānato nānāsaṇṭhānāni.

Tattha hi aggapādaṅguliaṭṭhīni katakabījasaṇṭhānāni. Tadanantarāni majjhapabbaṭṭhīni panasaṭṭhisaṇṭhānāni. Mūlapabbaṭṭhīni paṇavasaṇṭhānāni. Piṭṭhipādaṭṭhīni koṭṭitakandalakandarāsisaṇṭhānāni. Paṇhikaṭṭhi ekaṭṭhitālaphalabījasaṇṭhānaṃ. Gopphakaṭṭhīni baddhakīḷāgoḷakasaṇṭhānāni. Jaṅghaṭṭhīnaṃ gopphakaṭṭhīsu patiṭṭhitaṭṭhānaṃ apanītatacasindikaḷīrasaṇṭhānaṃ. Khuddakajaṅghaṭṭhikaṃ dhanukadaṇḍasaṇṭhānaṃ. Mahantaṃ milātasappapiṭṭhisaṇṭhānaṃ. Jaṇṇukaṭṭhi ekato parikkhīṇapheṇakasaṇṭhānaṃ. Tattha jaṅghaṭṭhikassa patiṭṭhitaṭṭhānaṃ atikhiṇaggagosiṅgasaṇṭhānaṃ. Ūruṭṭhi duttacchitavāsiparasudaṇḍasaṇṭhānaṃ. Tassa kaṭiṭṭhimhi patiṭṭhitaṭṭhānaṃ kīḷāgoḷakasaṇṭhānaṃ. Tena kaṭiṭṭhino patiṭṭhitaṭṭhānaṃ aggacchinnamahāpunnāgaphalasaṇṭhānaṃ. Kaṭiṭṭhīni dvepi ekābaddhāni hutvā kumbhakārikauddhanasaṇṭhānāni. Pāṭiyekkaṃ kammārakūṭayottakasaṇṭhānāni. Koṭiyaṃ ṭhitaṃ ānisadaṭṭhi adhomukhaṃ katvā gahitasappaphaṇasaṇṭhānaṃ, sattaṭṭhaṭṭhānesu chiddāvachiddaṃ. Piṭṭhikaṇṭakaṭṭhīni abbhantarato uparūpari ṭhapitasīsapaṭṭaveṭhakasaṇṭhānāni. Bāhirato vaṭṭanāvaḷisaṇṭhānāni. Tesaṃ antarantarā kakacadantasadisā dve tayo kaṇṭakā honti. Catuvīsatiyā phāsukaṭṭhīsu aparipuṇṇāni aparipuṇṇaasisaṇṭhānāni. Paripuṇṇāni paripuṇṇaasisaṇṭhānāni. Sabbānipi odātakukkuṭassa pasāritapakkhasaṇṭhānāni. Cuddasa uraṭṭhīni jiṇṇasandamānikapañjarasaṇṭhānāni. Hadayaṭṭhi dabbiphaṇasaṇṭhānaṃ. Akkhakaṭṭhīni khuddakalohavāsidaṇḍasaṇṭhānāni. Koṭṭaṭṭhīni ekato parikkhīṇasīhaḷakuddālasaṇṭhānāni. Bāhuṭṭhīni ādāsadaṇḍakasaṇṭhānāni. Aggabāhuṭṭhīni yamakatālakandasaṇṭhānāni. Maṇibandhaṭṭhīni ekato alliyāpetvā ṭhapitasīsakapaṭṭaveṭhakasaṇṭhānāni. Piṭṭhihatthaṭṭhīni koṭṭitakandalakandarāsisaṇṭhānāni. Hatthaṅgulīsu mūlapabbaṭṭhīni paṇavasaṇṭhānāni. Majjhapabbaṭṭhīni aparipuṇṇapanasaṭṭhisaṇṭhānāni. Aggapabbaṭṭhīni katakabījasaṇṭhānāni. Satta gīvaṭṭhīni daṇḍena vijjhitvā paṭipāṭiyā ṭhapitavaṃsakaḷīracakkalakasaṇṭhānāni. Heṭṭhimahanukaṭṭhi kammārānaṃ ayokūṭayottakasaṇṭhānaṃ. Uparimaṃ avalekhanasatthakasaṇṭhānaṃ. Akkhikūpanāsakūpaṭṭhīni apanītamiñjataruṇatālaṭṭhisaṇṭhānāni. Nalāṭaṭṭhi adhomukhaṭṭhapitasaṅkhathālakakapālasaṇṭhānaṃ. Kaṇṇacūḷikaṭṭhīni nhāpitakhurakosasaṇṭhānāni. Nalāṭakaṇṇacūḷikānaṃ upari paṭṭabandhanokāse aṭṭhisaṅkuṭitaghaṭapuṇṇapaṭalakhaṇḍasaṇṭhānaṃ. Muddhaṭṭhi mukhacchinnavaṅkanāḷikerasaṇṭhānaṃ. Sīsaṭṭhīni sibbetvā ṭhapitajajjaralābukaṭāhasaṇṭhānāni.

Disato dvīsu disāsu jātāni. Okāsato avisesena sakalasarīre ṭhitāni. Visesena panettha sīsaṭṭhīni givaṭṭhīsu patiṭṭhitāni. Gīvaṭṭhīni piṭṭhikaṇṭakaṭṭhīsu. Piṭṭhikaṇṭakaṭṭhīni kaṭiṭṭhīsu. Kaṭiṭṭhīni ūruṭṭhīsu. Ūruṭṭhīni jaṇṇukaṭṭhīsu. Jaṇṇukaṭṭhīni jaṅghaṭṭhīsu. Jaṅghaṭṭhīni gopphakaṭṭhīsu. Gopphakaṭṭhīni piṭṭhipādaṭṭhīsu patiṭṭhitāni. Paricchedato anto aṭṭhimiñjena, uparito maṃsena, agge mūle ca aññamaññena paricchinnāni, ayaṃ nesaṃ sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Aṭṭhimiñjanti tesaṃ tesaṃ aṭṭhīnaṃ abbhantaragataṃ miñjaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato setaṃ. Saṇṭhānato mahantamahantānaṃ aṭṭhīnaṃ abbhantaragataṃ veḷunāḷiyaṃ pakkhittaseditamahāvettaggasaṇṭhānaṃ. Khuddānukhuddakānaṃ abbhantaragataṃ veḷuyaṭṭhipabbesu pakkhittaseditatanuvettaggasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato dvīsu disāsu jātaṃ. Okāsato aṭṭhīnaṃ abbhantare patiṭṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato aṭṭhīnaṃ abbhantaratalehi paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Vakkanti ekabandhanā dve maṃsapiṇḍikā. Taṃ vaṇṇato mandarattaṃ pāḷibhaddakaṭṭhivaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato dārakānaṃ yamakakīḷāgoḷakasaṇṭhānaṃ, ekavaṇṭapaṭibaddhaambaphaladvayasaṇṭhānaṃ vā. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato galavāṭakā nikkhantena ekamūlena thokaṃ gantvā dvidhā bhinnena thūlanhārunā vinibaddhaṃ hutvā hadayamaṃsaṃ parikkhipitvā ṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato vakkaṃ vakkabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Ru-Yi Translation

Flesh: nine hundred pieces of flesh. All of it, by color, is red, like the flame-of-the-forest flower. By shape: the flesh of the calf is in the shape of cooked rice wrapped in a palm-leaf packet. The flesh of the thigh is in the shape of a pounding-stone's roller. The flesh of the buttocks is in the shape of an oven's ridge. The flesh of the back is in the shape of a slab of palm-sugar. The flesh over the two rows of ribs is in the shape of a thin coating of clay on the inner wall of a large jar. The flesh of the breast is in the shape of a rounded lump of clay dropped down. The flesh of the two upper arms is in the shape of a large skinned rat, doubled over and set down. Thus, for one grasping it from the grossest downward, even the finest becomes evident. By direction, it arises in both directions. By location, it stands smeared over three hundred and twenty bones. By delimitation: it is delimited below by the fixed surface resting on the mass of bones, above by the skin, and across by one another; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Sinews: nine hundred sinews. By color, all sinews are white. By shape, of various shapes. Among these, five great sinews, binding the body, descend on the front side beginning from the upper part of the neck; five on the back side; five on the right side; five on the left side. Binding the right hand too, five descend on the front of the hand, five on the back; likewise binding the left hand. Binding the right foot too, five descend on the front of the foot, five on the back; likewise binding the left foot. Thus, sixty great body-sustaining sinews, binding the body, descend through it. These are also called "cords." All of these are in the shape of banana-shoots. Others lie spread over this or that region. Finer than these are ones in the shape of a thread-cord. Others, finer still, are in the shape of a putilatā-creeper. Others, finer still, are in the shape of a great lute's strings. Others are in the shape of a thick thread. The sinews on the backs of the hands and feet are in the shape of a bird's foot. The sinews on the head are in the shape of a child's hair-net. The sinews on the back are in the shape of a wet net spread out in the sun. The remaining sinews, following each limb and member, are in the shape of a net-garment fitted over the body. By direction, they arise in both directions. By location, they stand binding the bones throughout the entire body. By delimitation: they are delimited below by the surfaces resting upon the three hundred bones, above by the places where they meet the flesh and skin, and across by one another; this is their delimitation by similar parts. Their delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Bones: setting aside the thirty-two tooth-bones, the remaining are sixty-four hand-bones, sixty-four foot-bones, sixty-four soft bones resting on flesh, two heel-bones, two ankle-bones in each foot, two shin-bones, one knee-bone, one thigh-bone, two hip-bones, eighteen spine-bones, twenty-four rib-bones, fourteen breast-bones, one heart-bone, two collar-bones, two shoulder-blade-bones, two upper-arm-bones, two forearm-bones each, seven neck-bones, two jaw-bones, one nose-bone, two eye-sockets, two ear-bones, one forehead-bone, one crown-bone, and nine skull-bones — thus, in all, three hundred bones. All of these, by color, are white. By shape, of various shapes.

Herein, the bones of the toe-tips are in the shape of kataka-seeds. The next, the middle joint-bones, are in the shape of jackfruit seeds. The base joint-bones are in the shape of small drums. The bones of the tops of the feet are in the shape of a heap of pounded banana-stem fibers. The heel-bone is in the shape of a single palm-fruit seed. The ankle-bones are in the shape of tied playing-balls. The place where the shin-bone rests on the ankle-bones is in the shape of an unhusked, young tāla-shoot. The smaller shin-bone is in the shape of a bow-stick; the larger, in the shape of an old snake's spine. The knee-bone is in the shape of a swelling worn down on one side. The place there where the shin-bone rests is in the shape of the sharpened tip of a cow's horn. The thigh-bone is in the shape of a poorly carved axe-handle. The place where it rests on the hip-bone is in the shape of a playing-ball. The place where the hip-bone rests on that is in the shape of a great punnāga fruit with its tip cut off. The two hip-bones, joined together as one, are in the shape of a potter's kiln; separately, in the shape of a smith's tongs-cord. The tail-bone, standing at the end, turned downward, is in the shape of a cobra's hood grasped, riddled with seven or eight holes. The spine-bones, from within, are in the shape of a row of head-cloth coils placed one above another; from without, in the shape of a strand of rounded beads. Between them, here and there, are two or three spurs resembling saw-teeth. Of the twenty-four rib-bones, the incomplete ones are in the shape of incomplete swords; the complete ones, in the shape of complete swords. All of them together are in the shape of the outspread wings of a white cock. The fourteen breast-bones are in the shape of an old chariot's worn-out frame. The heart-bone is in the shape of a spoon's hood. The collar-bones are in the shape of small iron knife-handles. The shoulder-blade-bones are in the shape of a worn Sinhalese hoe on one side. The upper-arm-bones are in the shape of a mirror-handle. The forearm-bones are in the shape of a pair of tāla-shoots joined together. The wrist-bones are in the shape of head-cloth coils placed close together on one side. The bones of the backs of the hands are in the shape of a heap of pounded banana-stem fibers. In the fingers, the base joint-bones are in the shape of small drums; the middle joint-bones, in the shape of incomplete jackfruit seeds; the tip joint-bones, in the shape of kataka-seeds. The seven neck-bones are in the shape of bamboo-shoot segments pierced with a stick and set in a row. The lower jaw-bone is in the shape of a smith's iron-hammer's cord. The upper jaw-bone is in the shape of a scraping-knife. The eye-socket and nose-bones are in the shape of a young palm-nut with its kernel removed. The forehead-bone is in the shape of a conch-shell bowl set face-down. The temple-bones are in the shape of a barber's razor-case. Above the forehead and temple-bones, at the place of the head-band, it is in the shape of a piece of cracked pottery bent to fit a full water-pot. The crown-bone is in the shape of a curved coconut with its mouth cut off. The skull-bones are in the shape of a cracked, stitched-together gourd-shell.

By direction, they arise in both directions. By location, they stand, without distinction, throughout the entire body; but more specifically, the skull-bones rest on the neck-bones, the neck-bones on the spine-bones, the spine-bones on the hip-bones, the hip-bones on the thigh-bones, the thigh-bones on the knee-bones, the knee-bones on the shin-bones, the shin-bones on the ankle-bones, and the ankle-bones rest on the bones of the tops of the feet. By delimitation: they are delimited within by the bone-marrow, above by the flesh, and at the tips and bases by one another; this is their delimitation by similar parts. Their delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Bone-marrow: the marrow found within each of these bones. It is white in color. By shape: within the larger bones, it is in the shape of the softened tip of a large split cane placed inside a bamboo tube; within the smaller ones, it is in the shape of the softened tip of a thin cane placed inside the joints of a bamboo stick. By direction, it arises in both directions. By location, it is fixed within the bones. By delimitation: it is delimited by the inner surfaces of the bones; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Kidneys: two lumps of flesh joined by a single bond. By color, it is slightly red, the color of a pālibhaddaka-tree seed. By shape, it is in the shape of a pair of children's twin playing-balls, or of a pair of mango fruits joined by a single stalk. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands, having gone a short distance in a single root emerging from the throat-hollow and split into two, bound by a thick sinew and encircling the heart-flesh. By delimitation: it is delimited by its own portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The bone catalogue's sheer specificity — down to the precise shape of each individual finger-joint, distinguishing a base joint from a middle joint from a tip joint by three separate comparisons — reflects a level of anatomical attentiveness that goes well beyond what devotional repulsion alone would seem to require. This granularity serves the meditation's actual technique directly: a meditator working through the thirty-two parts by the "releasing in succession" method needs genuinely distinct mental images to hold onto for each sub-part, or the whole catalogue collapses into a single undifferentiated blur.

The similes chosen throughout — kitchen tools, cart parts, agricultural implements, children's toys, common seeds and fruits — are drawn almost entirely from ordinary domestic and village life rather than anything exotic or specially disturbing. This is a deliberate stylistic choice consistent with the whole thirty-two-parts method: the repulsiveness being cultivated is meant to arise from clear-eyed recognition of what the body actually is, not from manufactured horror, so the comparisons stay resolutely mundane even while describing bone and marrow in exhaustive anatomical detail.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
saṭṭhi mahānhārū

the sixty great sinews — saṭṭhi (sixty) + mahā (great) + nhāru (sinew, tendon); the body-sustaining tendon network described as running through the neck, torso, and all four limbs in symmetrical groups of five.

timattāni aṭṭhisatāni

three hundred bones (in round measure) — the traditional total count of the skeletal bones (excluding the thirty-two teeth, counted separately), catalogued here individually by shape from the toe-tips to the skull.

ekavaṇṭapaṭibaddha

joined by a single stalk — eka (one) + vaṇṭa (a stalk, stem) + paṭibaddha (bound, connected); the image for the paired kidneys, compared to twin mango fruits sharing one stem, capturing both their duality and their physical connection.

Heart, Liver, Diaphragm, Spleen, and Lungs

The chapter turns to the chest's internal organs: the heart, shaped like an inverted lotus bud with a small hollow at its center holding the blood on which mind itself is said to depend — its very color reportedly varying by a meditator's own temperament — the twin-lobed liver, the membranous diaphragm, the tongue-shaped spleen whose rupture from a blow ends life, and the ragged, flavorless lungs, likened to a lump of chewed straw for their utter blandness once stripped of anything actually eaten or drunk.

Pali Source

Hadayanti hadayamaṃsaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato rattapadumapattapiṭṭhivaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato bāhirapattāni apanetvā adhomukhaṃ ṭhapitapadumamakuḷasaṇṭhānaṃ. Bahi maṭṭhaṃ, anto kosātakīphalassa abbhantarasadisaṃ. Paññavantānaṃ thokaṃ vikasitaṃ, mandapaññānaṃ makuḷitameva. Anto cassa punnāgaṭṭhipatiṭṭhānamatto āvāṭako hoti, yattha addhapasatamattaṃ lohitaṃ saṇṭhāti, yaṃ nissāya manodhātu ca manoviññāṇadhātu ca vattanti. Taṃ panetaṃ rāgacaritassa rattaṃ hoti, dosacaritassa kāḷakaṃ, mohacaritassa maṃsadhovanaudakasadisaṃ, vitakkacaritassa kulatthayūsavaṇṇaṃ, saddhācaritassa kaṇikārapupphavaṇṇaṃ, paññācaritassa acchaṃ vippasannaṃ anāvilaṃ paṇḍaraṃ parisuddhaṃ niddhotajātimaṇi viya jutimantaṃ khāyati. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato sarīrabbhantare dvinnaṃ thanānaṃ majjhe patiṭṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato hadayaṃ hadayabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Yakananti yamakamaṃsapaṭalaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato rattaṃ paṇḍukadhātukaṃ nātirattakumudassa pattapiṭṭhivaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato mūle ekaṃ agge yamakaṃ koviḷārapattasaṇṭhānaṃ. Tañca dandhānaṃ ekameva hoti mahantaṃ, paññavantānaṃ dve vā tīṇi vā khuddakāni. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ, okāsato dvinnaṃ thanānaṃ abbhantare dakkhiṇapassaṃ nissāya ṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato yakanaṃ yakanabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Kilomakanti paṭicchannāpaṭicchannabhedato duvidhaṃ pariyonahanamaṃsaṃ. Taṃ duvidhampi vaṇṇato setaṃ dukūlapilotikavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato attano okāsasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato paṭicchannakilomakaṃ uparimāya disāya. Itaraṃ dvīsu disāsu jātaṃ. Okāsato paṭicchannakilomakaṃ hadayañca vakkañca paṭicchādetvā, appaṭicchannakilomakaṃ sakalasarīre cammassa heṭṭhato maṃsaṃ pariyonandhitvā ṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato heṭṭhā maṃsena, upari cammena, tiriyaṃ kilomakabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Pihakanti udarajivhāmaṃsaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato nīlaṃ nigguṇḍipupphavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato sattaṅgulappamāṇaṃ abandhanaṃ kāḷavacchakajivhāsaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato hadayassa vāmapasse udarapaṭalassa matthakapassaṃ nissāya ṭhitaṃ, yasmiṃ paharaṇappahārena bahinikkhante sattānaṃ jīvitakkhayo hoti. Paricchedato pihakabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Papphāsanti dvattiṃsamaṃsakhaṇḍappabhedaṃ papphāsamaṃsaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato rattaṃ nātipakkaudumbaraphalavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato visamacchinnabahalapūvakhaṇḍasaṇṭhānaṃ. Abbhantare asitapītānaṃ abhāve uggatena kammajatejusmānā abbhāhatattā saṃkhāditapalālapiṇḍamiva nirasaṃ nirojaṃ. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato sarīrabbhantare dvinnaṃ thanānaṃ antare hadayañca yakanañca upari chādetvā olambantaṃ ṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato papphāsabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Ru-Yi Translation

Heart: the flesh of the heart. By color, it is the color of the back of a red lotus petal. By shape, it is in the shape of a lotus bud, with its outer petals removed, set facing downward. Outside it is smooth; within it is like the interior of a loofah fruit. In the wise, it is opened up a little; in those of dull wisdom, it remains merely a bud. Within it there is a small hollow, the size of a punnāga seed's socket, in which about half a measure of blood collects, in dependence on which the mind-element and mind-consciousness-element function. This blood is red for one of a lustful temperament; black for one of a hating temperament; like meat-washing water for one of a deluded temperament; the color of horse-gram broth for one of a speculative temperament; the color of a kaṇikāra flower for one of a faithful temperament; and, for one of a wise temperament, it appears clear, thoroughly transparent, unclouded, pale, and pure, radiant like a well-polished, genuine gem. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it is fixed within the body, between the two breasts. By delimitation: the heart is delimited by its own portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Liver: a twin fleshy membrane. By color, it is red, of a pale hue, the color of the back of a not-too-red water-lily's petal. By shape, single at its base and twinned at its tip, in the shape of a koviḷāra leaf. In those of slow understanding, it is one single large piece; in those of wisdom, it is two or three small pieces. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands within the two breasts, resting against the right side. By delimitation: the liver is delimited by its own portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Diaphragm: a covering flesh, twofold by division into the covered and the uncovered kind. Both kinds, by color, are white, the color of a coarse cotton cloth. By shape, they take the shape of their own location. By direction, the covered diaphragm arises in the upper direction; the other arises in both directions. By location, the covered diaphragm stands covering the heart and the kidneys; the uncovered diaphragm stands enveloping the flesh beneath the skin throughout the entire body. By delimitation: it is delimited below by the flesh, above by the skin, and across by its own portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Spleen: the flesh that is the "tongue" of the stomach. By color, it is dark blue, the color of a nigguṇḍi flower. By shape, it is about seven fingers in measure, unattached, in the shape of a black calf's tongue. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands on the left side of the heart, resting against the upper side of the stomach's membrane; and when it comes out from a wound caused by a blow, the life of beings comes to its end. By delimitation: it is delimited by its own portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Lungs: the fleshy tissue divided into thirty-two pieces of flesh. By color, it is red, the color of a not-quite-ripe fig. By shape, it is in the shape of a thick cake, unevenly cut. Inside, in the absence of anything eaten or drunk, being struck by the heat born of kamma rising up within it, it is flavorless and without nourishment, like a lump of chewed straw. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands within the body, between the two breasts, hanging down and covering the heart and liver from above. By delimitation: it is delimited by its own portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The claim that the small pool of blood inside the heart varies in color according to the meditator's own temperament is a striking piece of physiological-psychological correspondence: rather than treating the six temperaments (already established in the earlier chapter on finding a meditation subject) as purely mental or behavioral dispositions, this passage locates a visible, physical marker of them inside the body's own anatomy — a lustful temperament literally shows up as red blood, a wise temperament as clear, gem-like blood, making the temperament theory something with a claimed bodily basis rather than a purely psychological classification.

The lung's description as "flavorless and without nourishment, like a lump of chewed straw" once anything actually swallowed has been extracted from it is a small but pointed observation: an organ constantly working, expanding and contracting with every breath, is nonetheless itself utterly inert and valueless in its own substance — an image of ceaseless, purposeful function coexisting with total blandness at the level of the material doing the functioning.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
manodhātu / manoviññāṇadhātu

the mind-element / the mind-consciousness-element — two of the eighteen elements in the standard Abhidhamma classification of consciousness, here said to function in specific dependence on the small quantity of blood collected within the heart's hollow.

rāgacarita / dosacarita / mohacarita / vitakkacarita / saddhācarita / paññācarita

the lustful / hating / deluded / speculative / faithful / wise temperaments — the standard sixfold temperament classification, here each assigned a specific, distinct color for the blood pooled within the heart.

kammajatejo

heat born of kamma — kammaja (produced by kamma) + tejo (heat, fire); the internal metabolic heat said to strike the lungs and render them flavorless from within, independent of anything externally eaten or drunk.

Intestines, Mesentery, Stomach-Contents, and Feces

The chapter turns to the digestive tract itself: the coiled intestines, as long as a person's own height many times over, compared to a headless snake curled in a trough of blood; the mesentery binding its coils together like the cords threading a treadle-machine's parts; and, in its most vivid and unflinching passage yet, the stomach as a teeming home for thirty-two kinds of worms that batter the heart when hungry and race to strip the first mouthfuls of any meal — all building to the observation that whatever is eaten separates, in the end, into only five possible fates.

Pali Source

Antanti purisassa dvattiṃsahatthā itthiyā aṭṭhavīsatihatthā ekavīsatiyā ṭhānesu obhaggā antavaṭṭi. Tadetaṃ vaṇṇato setaṃ sakkharasudhāvaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato lohitadoṇiyaṃ ābhujitvā ṭhapitasīsacchinnasappasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato dvīsu disāsu jātaṃ. Okāsato upari galavāṭake heṭṭhā ca karīsamagge vinibandhattā galavāṭakakarīsamaggapariyante sarīrabbhantare ṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato antabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Antaguṇanti antabhogaṭṭhānesu bandhanaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato setaṃ dakasītalikamūlavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato dakasītalikamūlasaṇṭhānameva. Disato dvīsu disāsu jātaṃ. Okāsato kuddālapharasukammādīni karontānaṃ yantākaḍḍhanakāle yantasuttakamiva yantaphalakāni antabhoge ekato agaḷante ābandhitvā pādapuñchanarajjumaṇḍalakassa antarā saṃsibbitvā ṭhitarajjukā viya ekavīsatiyā antabhogānaṃ antarā ṭhitaṃ. Paricchedato antaguṇabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Udariyanti udare bhavaṃ asitapītakhāyitasāyitaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato ajjhohaṭāhāravaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato parissāvane sithilabaddhataṇḍulasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato udare ṭhitaṃ.

Udaraṃ nāma ubhato nippīḷiyamānassa allasāṭakassa majjhe sañjātaphoṭakasadisaṃ antapaṭalaṃ, bahi maṭṭhaṃ, anto maṃsakasambupaliveṭhanakiliṭṭhapāvārakapupphakasadisaṃ, kuthitapanasatacassa abbhantarasadisantipi vattuṃ vaṭṭati, yattha takkoṭakā gaṇḍuppādakā tālahīrakā sūcimukhakā paṭatantasuttakā iccevamādidvattiṃsakulappabhedā kimayo ākulabyākulā saṇḍasaṇḍacārino hutvā nivasanti, ye pānabhojanādimhi avijjamāne ullaṅghitvā viravantā hadayamaṃsaṃ abhihananti, pānabhojanādiajjhoharaṇavelāyañca uddhaṃmukhā hutvā paṭhamajjhohaṭe dve tayo ālope turitaturitā viluppanti, yaṃ tesaṃ kimīnaṃ sūtigharaṃ vaccakuṭi gilānasālā susānañca hoti. Yattha seyyathāpi nāma caṇḍālagāmadvāre candanikāya nidāghasamaye thūlaphusitake deve vassante udakena vuyhamānaṃ muttakarīsacammaaṭṭhinhārukhaṇḍakheḷasiṅghāṇikālohitappabhutinānākuṇapajātaṃ nipatitvā kaddamodakāluḷitaṃ dvīhatīhaccayena sañjātakimikulaṃ sūriyātapasantāpavegakuthitaṃ upari pheṇapupphuḷake muñcantaṃ abhinīlavaṇṇaṃ paramaduggandhajegucchaṃ neva upagantuṃ, na daṭṭhuṃ araharūpataṃ āpajjitvā tiṭṭhati, pageva ghāyituṃ vā sāyituṃ vā, evameva nānappakāraṃ pānabhojanādidantamusalasañcuṇṇitaṃ jivhāhatthaparivattitakheḷalālāpalibuddhaṃ taṅkhaṇavigatavaṇṇagandharasādisampadaṃ tantavāyakhalisuvānavamathusadisaṃ nipatitvā pittasemhavātapaliveṭhitaṃ hutvā udaraggisantāpavegakuthitaṃ kimikulākulaṃ uparūpari pheṇapupphuḷakāni muñcantaṃ paramakasambuduggandhajegucchabhāvaṃ āpajjitvā tiṭṭhati. Yaṃ sutvāpi pānabhojanādīsu amanuññatā saṇṭhāti, pageva paññācakkhunā avaloketvā. Yattha ca patitaṃ pānabhojanādi pañcadhā vivekaṃ gacchati, ekaṃ bhāgaṃ pāṇakā khādanti, ekaṃ bhāgaṃ udaraggi jhāpeti, eko bhāgo muttaṃ hoti, eko bhāgo karīsaṃ, eko bhāgo rasabhāvaṃ āpajjitvā soṇitamaṃsādīni upabrūhayati.

Paricchedato udarapaṭalena ceva udariyabhāgena ca paricchinnaṃ. Ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Karīsanti vaccaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato yebhuyyena ajjhohaṭāhāravaṇṇameva hoti. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato heṭṭhimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato pakkāsaye ṭhitaṃ. Pakkāsayo nāma heṭṭhānābhi-piṭṭhikaṇṭakamūlānaṃ antare antāvasāne ubbedhena aṭṭhaṅgulamatto veḷunāḷikasadiso, yattha seyyathāpi nāma upari bhūmibhāge patitaṃ vassodakaṃ ogaḷitvā heṭṭhā bhūmibhāgaṃ pūretvā tiṭṭhati, evameva yaṃkiñci āmāsaye patitaṃ pānabhojanādikaṃ udaragginā pheṇuddehakaṃ pakkaṃ pakkaṃ nisadāya pisitamiva saṇhabhāvaṃ āpajjitvā antabilena ogaḷitvā ogaḷitvā omadditvā veḷupabbe pakkhipamānapaṇḍumattikā viya sannicitaṃ hutvā tiṭṭhati. Paricchedato pakkāsayapaṭalena ceva karīsabhāgena ca paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Matthaluṅganti sīsakaṭāhabbhantare ṭhitamiñjarāsi. Taṃ vaṇṇato setaṃ ahicchattakapiṇḍavaṇṇaṃ. Dadhibhāvaṃ asampattaṃ duṭṭhakhīravaṇṇantipi vattuṃ vaṭṭati. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato sīsakaṭāhabbhantare cattāro sibbinimagge nissāya samodhānetvā ṭhapitā cattāro piṭṭhapiṇḍā viya samohitaṃ tiṭṭhati. Paricchedato sīsakaṭāhassa abbhantaratalehi ceva matthaluṅgabhāgena ca paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Ru-Yi Translation

Intestines: a coiled intestinal cord, thirty-two hand-spans in a man, twenty-eight in a woman, folded at twenty-one places. By color, it is white, the color of lime made from shells. By shape, it is in the shape of a headless snake curled up and placed in a trough of blood. By direction, it arises in both directions. By location, it is bound above at the throat-hollow and below at the excretory passage, and stands, within the body, between the throat-hollow and the excretory passage. By delimitation: it is delimited by its own intestinal portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Mesentery: the binding at the places where the intestine coils. By color, it is white, the color of the root of a water-lily. By shape, exactly in the shape of a water-lily root. By direction, it arises in both directions. By location, just as, when people doing work with a spade, an axe, or the like use a machine's own draw-cord to bind the machine's parts together where the coil would otherwise slip loose, and, sewn through the middle of a foot-wiping rope-circle, a cord stands there — just so, it stands between the twenty-one coils of the intestine. By delimitation: it is delimited by its own mesenteric portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Stomach-contents: whatever, in the stomach, has been eaten, drunk, chewed, and tasted. By color, it is the color of swallowed food. By shape, it is in the shape of loosely packed rice in a straining-cloth. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands in the stomach.

The "stomach" is the intestinal membrane, resembling a swelling that arises in the middle of a wet cloth being wrung from both ends — smooth on the outside, and within resembling a soiled cloak-wrapping smeared with flesh-refuse, or, one might say, resembling the inside of a boiled jackfruit's rind. There dwell, moving about in confused disorder in dense clusters, worms of thirty-two distinct families — takkoṭaka, gaṇḍuppādaka, tālahīraka, sūcimukhaka, paṭatantasuttaka, and the like — who, in the absence of drink or food, leap up crying out and batter the heart's flesh; and, at the time of swallowing drink, food, and the like, turn their faces upward and race, in great haste, to strip away the first two or three mouthfuls swallowed. For these worms, this same place is their maternity house, their latrine, their sick-ward, and their charnel ground. Just as, at the gate of an outcaste village, in a cesspool, in the hot season, when thick drops of rain fall, there collects, carried along by the water, a mixture of various carcasses — urine, feces, skin, bone, bits of sinew, spittle, mucus, blood, and the like — churned together with mud and water, and, within two or three days, a mass of worms arises in it, seething with the force of the sun's heat, releasing bubbles of foam above, deep blue-black in color, extremely foul-smelling and disgusting, reaching a state fit neither to be approached nor even looked at, let alone smelled or tasted — just so, this manifold food and drink and the rest, pounded by the pestle of the teeth, seized by the tongue and hand, entangled in spittle and saliva, its color, smell, taste, and other qualities vanished at that very moment, resembling the vomit of a starving dog belonging to a weaver, falls in and, wrapped about by bile, phlegm, and wind, seethes with the force of the stomach-fire's heat, teeming with worm-families, releasing bubbles of foam one upon another, reaching a state of the utmost, refuse-like, foul-smelling disgust, and there it stands. Merely hearing of this is enough to establish distaste for drink, food, and the like — how much more so, looking upon it with the eye of wisdom. And whatever drink, food, and the like fall there separate into five portions: one portion is eaten by the worm-creatures; one portion is burned up by the stomach-fire; one portion becomes urine; one portion becomes feces; and one portion, having turned into nutriment, nourishes blood, flesh, and the rest.

By delimitation, it is delimited by the stomach's own membrane and by its own stomach-content portion. This is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Feces: excrement. By color, for the most part, it is simply the color of the food swallowed. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location. By direction, it arises in the lower direction. By location, it stands in the rectum. The "rectum" is a tube resembling a length of bamboo, about eight fingers in extent, situated between the lower part of the navel and the base of the spine, at the very end of the intestines — where, just as rainwater falling on an upper level of ground trickles down and stands filling the lower level of ground, so too whatever drink, food, and the like fall into the stomach, cooked again and again by the stomach-fire into a froth-boiling state, made fine as though ground on a grindstone, trickles down bit by bit through the interior of the intestine, is pressed together, and stands accumulated there, like pale clay packed into a bamboo joint. By delimitation, it is delimited by the rectum's own membrane and by its own fecal portion. This is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Brain: the mass of marrow-substance found within the skull. By color, it is white, the color of a mass of mushroom-caps; one might also say the color of spoiled milk not yet reaching the state of curd. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands gathered together within the skull, resting against the four seams, like four lumps of dough joined together. By delimitation, it is delimited by the skull's own inner surfaces and by its own brain-portion. This is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The extended cesspool simile for the stomach's contents is the most sustained single image in the entire thirty-two-parts contemplation, and its structure is worth noticing: it does not simply compare the stomach to something disgusting in general, but builds a point-by-point correspondence — food's color, smell, and taste vanishing "at that very moment" of swallowing exactly parallels how the various carcasses in the cesspool lose their original identity once mixed together, and the worm-infestation arising within two or three days in both cases drives home that digestion, mechanically speaking, is not different in kind from decomposition, only faster and hidden from view.

The closing fivefold division of what happens to any food eaten — consumed by intestinal worms, burned by digestive heat, becoming urine, becoming feces, or becoming nutriment for blood and flesh — is a striking piece of accounting: only one of the five outcomes actually does what eating is ordinarily thought to accomplish (nourishing the body), while the other four are framed as loss, waste, or feeding creatures other than oneself. The passage quietly reframes the entire, usually unexamined act of eating as mostly maintenance and disposal, with genuine nourishment as only a minority outcome.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
antaguṇa

the mesentery — anta (intestine) + guṇa (a cord, a string); the connective tissue binding the intestine's twenty-one coils together, compared here to the draw-cord of a mechanical device.

dvattiṃsakulappabheda kimi

worms of thirty-two distinct families — the specific population said to inhabit the stomach, named individually (takkoṭaka, gaṇḍuppādaka, and others) and described as actively battering the heart when food is absent and racing to consume the first mouthfuls of any meal.

pakkāsaya

the rectum, the receptacle of cooked matter — pakka (cooked, ripened) + āsaya (receptacle, abode); the final segment of the digestive tract where feces accumulate, its name reflecting the "cooking" process food has already undergone by the time it arrives there.

Bile, Phlegm, Pus, Blood, Sweat, and Fat

The chapter turns to the body's fluids proper: bile in its two forms, contained and uncontained, the latter spreading through the body and, when disturbed, causing the eyes to yellow and the limbs to tremble; phlegm, described through the vivid image of pond-scum parting and reclosing around whatever falls into a pool of water; pus, which has no fixed dwelling place at all, forming only wherever a wound or injury happens to occur; blood in its own two forms, one drop constantly moistening the vital organs and another far larger accumulation pervading the whole body; the sweat that only becomes apparent when the body grows heated; and the fat that people find too repugnant to use even for its practical properties, despite its abundance.

Pali Source

Pittanti dve pittāni baddhapittañca abaddhapittañca. Tattha baddhapittaṃ vaṇṇato bahalamadhukatelavaṇṇaṃ. Abaddhapittaṃ milātaākulipupphavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato ubhayampi okāsasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato baddhapittaṃ uparimāya disāya jātaṃ, itaraṃ dvīsu disāsu jātaṃ. Okāsato abaddhapittaṃ ṭhapetvā kesalomadantanakhānaṃ maṃsavinimuttaṭṭhānañceva thaddhasukkhacammañca udakamiva telabindu avasesasarīraṃ byāpetvā ṭhitaṃ, yamhi kupite akkhīni pītakāni honti, bhamanti, gattaṃ kampati, kaṇḍūyati. Baddhapittaṃ hadayapapphāsānaṃ antare yakanamaṃsaṃ nissāya patiṭṭhite mahākosātakīkosakasadise pittakosake ṭhitaṃ, yamhi kupite sattā ummattakā honti, vipallatthacittā hirottappaṃ chaḍḍetvā akātabbaṃ karonti, abhāsitabbaṃ bhāsanti, acintitabbaṃ cintenti. Paricchedato pittabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Semhanti sarīrabbhantare ekapatthapūrappamāṇaṃ semhaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato setaṃ nāgabalāpaṇṇarasavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato udarapaṭale ṭhitaṃ. Yaṃ pānabhojanādiajjhoharaṇakāle seyyathāpi nāma udake sevālapaṇakaṃ kaṭṭhe vā kathale vā patante chijjitvā dvidhā hutvā puna ajjhottharitvā tiṭṭhati, evameva pānabhojanādimhi nipatante chijjitvā dvidhā hutvā puna ajjhottharitvā tiṭṭhati, yamhi ca mandībhūte pakkagaṇḍo viya pūtikukkuṭaṇḍamiva ca udaraṃ paramajegucchaṃ kuṇapagandhaṃ hoti, tato uggatena ca gandhena uddekopi mukhampi duggandhaṃ pūtikuṇapasadisaṃ hoti. So ca puriso apehi duggandhaṃ vāyasīti vattabbataṃ āpajjati, yañca vaḍḍhitvā bahalattamāpannaṃ pidhānaphalakamiva vaccakuṭiyaṃ udarapaṭalassa abbhantareyeva kuṇapagandhaṃ sannirumbhitvā tiṭṭhati. Paricchedato semhabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Pubboti pūtilohitavasena pavattapubbaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato paṇḍupalāsavaṇṇo. Matasarīre pana pūtibahalācāmavaṇṇo hoti. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhāno. Disato dvīsu disāsu hoti. Okāsato pana pubbassa okāso nāma nibaddho natthi, yattha so sannicito tiṭṭheyya, yatra yatra khāṇukaṇṭakapaharaṇaggijālādīhi abhihate sarīrappadese lohitaṃ saṇṭhahitvā paccati, gaṇḍapīḷakādayo vā uppajjanti, tatra tatra tiṭṭhati. Paricchedato pubbabhāgena paricchinno, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Lohitanti dve lohitāni sannicitalohitañca saṃsaraṇalohitañca. Tattha sannicitalohitaṃ vaṇṇato nipakkabahalalākhārasavaṇṇaṃ. Saṃsaraṇalohitaṃ acchalākhārasavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato ubhayampi okāsasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato sannicitalohitaṃ uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Itaraṃ dvisu disāsu jātaṃ. Okāsato saṃsaraṇalohitaṃ ṭhapetvā kesalomadantanakhānaṃ maṃsavinimuttaṭṭhānañceva thaddhasukkhacammañca dhamanijālānusārena sabbaṃ upādiṇṇasarīraṃ pharitvā ṭhitaṃ. Sannicitalohitaṃ yakanaṭṭhānassa heṭṭhābhāgaṃ pūretvā ekapatthapūramattaṃ hadayavakkapapphāsānaṃ upari thokaṃ thokaṃ paggharantaṃ vakkahadayayakanapapphāse temayamānaṃ ṭhitaṃ. Tasmiṃ hi vakkahadayādīni atemente sattā pipāsitā honti. Paricchedato lohitabhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Sedoti lomakūpādīhi paggharaṇakaāpodhātu. So vaṇṇato vippasannatilatelavaṇṇo. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhāno. Disato dvīsu disāsu jāto. Okāsato sedassokāso nāma nibaddho natthi, yattha so lohitaṃ viya sadā tiṭṭheyya. Yadā pana aggisantāpasūriyasantāpautuvikārādīhi sarīraṃ santapati, tadā udakato abbūḷhamattavisamacchinnabhisamuḷālakumudanāḷakalāpo viya sabbakesalomakūpavivarehi paggharati, tasmā tassa saṇṭhānampi kesalomakūpavivarānaññeva vasena veditabbaṃ. Sedapariggaṇhakena ca yoginā kesalomakūpavivare pūretvā ṭhitavaseneva sedo manasi kātabbo. Paricchedato sedabhāgena paricchinno, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Medoti thinasineho. So vaṇṇato phālitahaliddivaṇṇo. Saṇṭhānato thūlasarīrassa tāva cammamaṃsantare ṭhapitahaliddivaṇṇadukūlapilotikasaṇṭhāno hoti. Kisasarīrassa jaṅghamaṃsaṃ ūrumaṃsaṃ piṭṭhikaṇṭakanissitaṃ piṭṭhimaṃsaṃ udaravaṭṭimaṃsanti etāni nissāya diguṇatiguṇaṃ katvā ṭhapitahaliddivaṇṇadukūlapilotikasaṇṭhāno. Disato dvīsu disāsu jāto.
