Okāsato thūlassa sakalasarīraṃ pharitvā kisassa jaṅghamaṃsādīni nissāya ṭhito, yaṃ sinehasaṅkhaṃ gatampi paramajegucchattā neva muddhani telatthāya, na nāsatelādīnamatthāya gaṇhanti. Paricchedato heṭṭhā maṃsena, upari cammena, tiriyaṃ medabhāgena paricchinno, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Ru-Yi Translation

Bile: two kinds of bile, contained bile and uncontained bile. Herein, contained bile, by color, is the color of thick honey-oil. Uncontained bile is the color of a withered ākuli flower. By shape, both take the shape of their own location. By direction, contained bile arises in the upper direction; the other arises in both directions. By location, uncontained bile, apart from the places where the hair, body-hair, teeth, and nails are free of flesh, and the hard, dry skin, stands pervading the rest of the body like a drop of oil in water; when it is disturbed, the eyes become yellow, they roll about, the body trembles and itches. Contained bile stands in the bile-sac, resembling a large loofah-fruit's shell, situated between the heart and lungs, resting against the liver's flesh; when this is disturbed, beings become deranged, their minds overturned, and, casting aside moral shame and moral dread, they do what should not be done, say what should not be said, and think what should not be thought. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own bile-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Phlegm: phlegm within the body, amounting to a single bowlful. By color, it is white, the color of the leaves of the nāgabalā plant. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands on the stomach's membrane. Just as, at the time of swallowing drink, food, and the like, pond-scum or algae on water, when a piece of wood or a potsherd falls upon it, parts in two and then closes back over it, so too, when drink, food, and the like fall upon it, it parts in two and then closes back over it again; and when this diminishes, the stomach becomes extremely disgusting, with the smell of a corpse, like a ripe boil or a rotten hen's egg, and, from the smell rising up from it, even the belch and the mouth become foul-smelling, like a putrid corpse — such a person becomes one who must be told, "go away, you smell foul" — and this same substance, having grown and become thick, stands like a covering slab sealing off the corpse-like smell within the stomach's own membrane, as though it were a latrine-cover. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own phlegm-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Pus: pus that occurs by way of putrefying blood. By color, it is the color of a withered leaf; but in a dead body, it is the color of thick, rotten rice-gruel. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location. By direction, it occurs in both directions. By location, there is no fixed place for pus, no location where it stands accumulated; wherever, on some part of the body struck by a stake, a thorn, a blow, or a flame, blood collects and putrefies, or boils and swellings arise, there it stands. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own pus-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Blood: two kinds of blood, accumulated blood and circulating blood. Herein, accumulated blood, by color, is the color of cooked, thick lac-resin juice. Circulating blood is the color of clear lac-resin juice. By shape, both take the shape of their own location. By direction, accumulated blood arises in the upper direction; the other arises in both directions. By location, circulating blood, apart from the places where the hair, body-hair, teeth, and nails are free of flesh, and the hard, dry skin, stands pervading the whole of the sentient body, following the network of blood-vessels. Accumulated blood stands filling the lower portion of the liver's region, amounting to a single bowlful, oozing little by little over the heart, kidneys, and lungs, and keeping the kidneys, heart, and liver moist. For when the kidneys, heart, and the rest are not kept moist by it, beings become thirsty. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own blood-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Sweat: the water-element that oozes from the pores and the like. By color, it is the color of clear sesame oil. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location. By direction, it occurs in both directions. By location, there is no fixed place for sweat where it might stand constantly, as blood does. But when the body becomes heated through the heat of a fire, the heat of the sun, or a change in the season and the like, then, like a bundle of unevenly cut lotus-fibers, lotus-stalks, or water-lily stalks pulled up from the water, it oozes out through all the openings of the hair-pores and body-hair-pores; therefore its shape too should be understood by way of those very hair-pore and body-hair-pore openings. And a meditator investigating sweat should attend to it precisely as it stands filling the hair-pore and body-hair-pore openings. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own sweat-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Fat: a solidified unctuous substance. By color, it is the color of split turmeric. By shape: in a stout body, first, it is in the shape of a piece of turmeric-colored coarse cotton cloth placed between the skin and flesh. In a lean body, it is in the shape of a piece of turmeric-colored coarse cotton cloth, doubled or tripled and placed against the flesh of the calf, the flesh of the thigh, the flesh of the back resting on the spine, and the flesh of the belly-region. By direction, it occurs in both directions. By location, in a stout person, it stands pervading the entire body; in a lean person, it stands resting against the flesh of the calf and the rest — and this substance, though reckoned as an unctuous substance, is, on account of its extreme disgustingness, taken by no one for oiling the head, or for making nasal oil, or the like. By delimitation, it is delimited below by the flesh, above by the skin, and across by its own fat-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The distinction between "contained" and "uncontained" bile carries a real physiological-behavioral consequence in the text's own account: contained bile, held in its own sac, when disturbed produces derangement of judgment — doing, saying, and thinking what should not be — while uncontained bile, spread invisibly through the body, when disturbed produces purely physical symptoms (yellowed eyes, trembling, itching). The text is drawing a functional line between a fluid affecting mental clarity and one affecting only the body, based purely on where each is physically located.

The closing observation about fat — that despite being a genuinely useful unctuous substance, no one will use it for oiling the head or making nasal oil "on account of its extreme disgustingness" — is a quietly devastating piece of reasoning: an object's actual physical properties (its oiliness, its potential usefulness) turn out to be entirely overridden by simple repulsion once its source and context are known, exactly the same override the whole thirty-two-parts meditation is trying to induce toward the body as a whole.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
baddhapitta / abaddhapitta

contained bile / uncontained bile — baddha (bound, contained) and a-baddha (unbound); the twofold division whose different physical locations are said to produce entirely different disturbance-symptoms when each is agitated.

sannicitalohita / saṃsaraṇalohita

accumulated blood / circulating blood — the two forms of blood distinguished by location and function: one pooled near the internal organs to keep them moist, the other flowing throughout the body via the vascular network.

thinasineha

a solidified unctuous substance — thina (thickened, solidified) + sineha (oiliness, unctuousness); the technical description of fat, distinguishing it from the more fluid oils and greases described among the other bodily fluids.

Tears, Oil-of-the-Skin, Saliva, Nasal Mucus, Synovial Fluid, and Urine

The chapter completes the thirty-two parts with the six remaining fluids: tears, which pool only when triggered by joy, grief, unsuitable food, or irritation, never sitting constantly in the eyes; the oil that surfaces on the skin only when heated, exactly like the sheen on water into which an oiled hand has dipped; saliva, thin near the tip of the tongue and thick at its root, capable of moistening even dry food like water seeping into sand; nasal mucus, spoiled brain-matter that has descended through the palate; synovial fluid, whose scarcity in the body's one hundred and eighty joints makes bones creak audibly with every movement; and finally urine, which enters the bladder by a route no one can actually see, unlike its very visible exit.

Pali Source

Assūti akkhīhi paggharaṇakaāpodhātu. Taṃ vaṇṇato vippasannatilatelavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato uparimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato akkhikūpakesu ṭhitaṃ. Na cetaṃ pittakosake pittamiva akkhikūpakesu sadā sannicitaṃ tiṭṭhati. Yadā pana sattā somanassajātā mahāhasitaṃ hasanti, domanassajātā rodanti paridevanti, tathārūpaṃ vā visamāhāraṃ āhārenti, yadā ca nesaṃ akkhīni dhūmarajapaṃsukādīhi abhihaññanti. Tadā etehi somanassadomanassavisabhāgāhārautūhi samuṭṭhahitvā akkhikūpake pūretvā tiṭṭhati vā paggharati vā. Assupariggaṇhakena ca yoginā akkhikūpake pūretvā ṭhitavaseneva pariggaṇhitabbaṃ. Paricchedato assubhāgena paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Vasāti vilīnasineho. Sā vaṇṇato nāḷikeratelavaṇṇā. Ācāme āsittatelavaṇṇātipi vattuṃ vaṭṭati. Saṇṭhānato nhānakāle pasannaudakassa upari paribbhamantasinehabinduvisaṭasaṇṭhānā. Disato dvīsu disāsu jātā. Okāsato yebhuyyena hatthatalahatthapiṭṭhipādatalapādapiṭṭhināsapuṭanalāṭaaṃsakūṭesu ṭhitā. Na cesā etesu okāsesu sadā vilīnāva hutvā tiṭṭhati. Yadā pana aggisantāpasūriyasantāpautuvisabhāgadhātuvisabhāgehi te padesā usmājātā honti, tadā tattha nhānakāle pasannaudakūpari sinehabinduvisaṭo viya ito cito ca sañcarati. Paricchedato vasābhāgena paricchinnā, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisoyeva.

Kheḷoti antomukhe pheṇamissā āpodhātu. So vaṇṇato seto pheṇavaṇṇo. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhāno. Pheṇasaṇṭhānotipi vattuṃ vaṭṭati. Disato uparimāya disāya jāto. Okāsato ubhohi kapolapassehi oruyha jivhāya ṭhito. Na cesa ettha sadā sannicito hutvā tiṭṭhati. Yadā pana sattā tathārūpamāhāraṃ passanti vā saranti vā, uṇhatittakaṭukaloṇambilānaṃ vā kiñci mukhe ṭhapenti, yadā vā nesaṃ hadayaṃ āgilāyati, kismiñci deva vā jigucchā uppajjati, tadā kheḷo uppajjitvā ubhohi kapolapassehi oruyha jivhāya saṇṭhāti. Aggajivhāya cesa tanuko hoti, mūlajivhāya bahalo, mukhe pakkhittañca puthukaṃ vā taṇḍulaṃ vā aññaṃ vā kiñci khādanīyaṃ nadīpuline khatakūpakasalilaṃ viya parikkhayaṃ agacchantova temetuṃ samattho hoti. Paricchedato kheḷabhāgena paricchinno, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisova.

Siṅghāṇikāti matthaluṅgato paggharaṇakaasuci. Sā vaṇṇato taruṇatālaṭṭhimiñjavaṇṇā. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhānā. Disato uparimāya disāya jātā. Okāsato nāsāpuṭe pūretvā ṭhitā. Na cesā ettha sadā sannicitā hutvā tiṭṭhati, atha kho yathā nāma puriso paduminipatte dadhiṃ bandhitvā heṭṭhā kaṇṭakena vijjheyya, athānena chiddena dadhimuttaṃ gaḷitvā bahi pateyya, evameva yadā sattā rodanti, visabhāgāhārautuvasena vā sañjātadhātukhobhā honti, tadā anto sīsato pūtisemhabhāvamāpannaṃ matthaluṅgaṃ gaḷitvā tālumatthakavivarena otaritvā nāsāpuṭe pūretvā tiṭṭhati vā paggharati vā. Siṅghāṇikā pariggaṇhakena ca yoginā nāsāpuṭe pūretvā ṭhitavaseneva pariggaṇhitabbā. Paricchedato siṅghāṇikābhāgena paricchinnā, ayamassā sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisova.

Lasikāti sarīrasandhīnaṃ abbhantare picchilakuṇapaṃ. Sā vaṇṇato kaṇikāraniyyāsavaṇṇā. Saṇṭhānato okāsasaṇṭhānā. Disato dvīsu disāsu jātā. Okāsato aṭṭhisandhīnaṃ abbhañjanakiccaṃ sādhayamānā asītisatasandhīnaṃ abbhantare ṭhitā. Yassa cesā mandā hoti, tassa uṭṭhahantassa nisīdantassa abhikkamantassa paṭikkamantassa samiñjantassa pasārentassa aṭṭhikāni kaṭakaṭāyanti, accharāsaddaṃ karonto viya sañcarati. Ekayojanadviyojanamattaṃ addhānaṃ gatassa vāyodhātu kuppati, gattāni dukkhanti. Yassa pana bahukā honti, tassa uṭṭhānanisajjādīsu na aṭṭhīni kaṭakaṭāyanti, dīghampi addhānaṃ gatassa na vāyodhātu kuppati, na gattāni dukkhanti. Paricchedato lasikābhāgena paricchinnā, ayamassā sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisova.

Muttanti muttarasaṃ. Taṃ vaṇṇato māsakhārodakavaṇṇaṃ. Saṇṭhānato adhomukhaṭṭhapitaudakakumbhaabbhantaragataudakasaṇṭhānaṃ. Disato heṭṭhimāya disāya jātaṃ. Okāsato vatthissa abbhantare ṭhitaṃ. Vatthi nāma vatthi puṭo vuccati. Yattha seyyathāpi candanikāya pakkhitte amukhe ravaṇaghaṭe candanikāraso pavisati, na cassa pavisanamaggo paññāyati, evameva sarīrato muttaṃ pavisati, na cassa pavisanamaggo paññāyati, nikkhamanamaggo pana pākaṭo hoti. Yamhi ca muttassa bharite passāvaṃ karomāti sattānaṃ āyūhanaṃ hoti. Paricchedato vatthiabbhantarena ceva muttabhāgena ca paricchinnaṃ, ayamassa sabhāgaparicchedo. Visabhāgaparicchedo pana kesasadisova.

Ru-Yi Translation

Tears: the water-element that oozes from the eyes. By color, it is the color of clear sesame oil. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands in the eye-sockets. And it does not stand constantly accumulated in the eye-sockets, as bile does in its own sac. But when beings, filled with joy, laugh a great laugh; or, filled with sorrow, weep and lament; or partake of some unsuitable food; or when their eyes are struck by smoke, dust, or grit — then, arising from these conditions of joy, sorrow, unsuitable food, and season, it stands filling the eye-sockets, or oozes forth. And a meditator investigating tears should investigate it precisely as it stands filling the eye-sockets. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own tear-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Oil-of-the-skin: a dissolved unctuous substance. By color, it is the color of coconut oil; one might also say the color of oil sprinkled on rice-scrapings. By shape, it is in the shape of an oily drop spreading out on the surface of clear water during bathing. By direction, it occurs in both directions. By location, it stands for the most part on the palms of the hands, the backs of the hands, the soles of the feet, the tops of the feet, the bridge of the nose, the forehead, and the points of the shoulders. And it does not stand there always in a dissolved state at these locations; but when these areas become heated through the heat of a fire, the heat of the sun, a change of season, or a disturbance in the elements, then, at that place, like an oily drop spreading over clear water during bathing, it moves about here and there. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own oily portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Saliva: the water-element mixed with foam within the mouth. By color, it is white, the color of foam. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location; one might also say the shape of foam. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it descends from both cheeks and stands upon the tongue. And it does not stand there always accumulated; but when beings see or recall some kind of food, or place something hot, bitter, pungent, salty, or sour in the mouth, or when their heart feels queasy, or some disgust arises regarding something, then saliva arises, descends from both cheeks, and settles upon the tongue. It is thin at the tip of the tongue and thick at its root, and, placed in the mouth, is capable of moistening puffed rice, ordinary rice, or any other food, without ever running out — just as water seeps into a hollow dug in a riverbank's sand. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own saliva-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Nasal mucus: the impurity that oozes from the brain. By color, it is the color of the pith of a young palm-fruit stone. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location. By direction, it arises in the upper direction. By location, it stands filling the nostrils. And it does not stand there always accumulated; but just as a man, having tied curd inside a lotus-leaf, might pierce it from below with a thorn, whereupon the whey would trickle out through that hole and fall outside — just so, when beings weep, or when a disturbance of the elements arises through unsuitable food or season, then the brain within the head, having reached a state of putrid phlegm, trickles down, descends through the gap at the top of the palate, and stands filling the nostrils, or oozes forth. And a meditator investigating nasal mucus should investigate it precisely as it stands filling the nostrils. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Synovial fluid: a slippery, corpse-like substance within the joints of the body. By color, it is the color of kaṇikāra-tree resin. By shape, it takes the shape of its own location. By direction, it occurs in both directions. By location, it stands within the hundred and eighty joints, accomplishing the function of lubricating the bone-joints. For one in whom it is scarce, when he rises, sits, walks forward, walks back, bends, or stretches, his bones creak, making a sound as though snapping fingers, as he moves. For one who has walked a distance of a league or two, the wind-element becomes disturbed, and his limbs ache. But for one in whom it is abundant, his bones do not creak when he rises, sits, and so on, and, even after walking a long distance, the wind-element is not disturbed, nor do his limbs ache. By delimitation, it is delimited by its own portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Urine: the fluid of urine. By color, it is the color of an alkaline solution made from bean-ash. By shape, it is in the shape of water contained within a water-pot placed mouth-downward. By direction, it arises in the lower direction. By location, it stands within the bladder. The "bladder" is what is called the urinary receptacle. Just as, when placed in a cesspool, the liquid of the cesspool enters a jar with no visible mouth, and the path by which it enters is not apparent — just so, urine enters from the body, and the path by which it enters is not apparent, though the path by which it exits is evident. And when the bladder is full, beings feel the urge, thinking, "I must urinate." By delimitation, it is delimited by the bladder's interior and by its own urine-portion; this is its delimitation by similar parts. Its delimitation by dissimilar parts is exactly as with the hairs.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Across this final group of six fluids, a recurring pattern becomes explicit that has been quietly operating throughout the whole thirty-two-parts analysis: several of these substances (tears, oil-of-the-skin, saliva, nasal mucus) are described as not sitting constantly at their named location but arising only under specific triggering conditions — joy, grief, unsuitable food, heat, disturbance. The meditator is instructed, in each such case, to attend to the substance specifically "as it stands filling" its location when present, rather than imagining a fluid perpetually pooled there — a small but important instruction in accuracy, keeping the contemplation grounded in how the body actually behaves rather than in an oversimplified static picture.

The synovial-fluid section's closing detail — that its scarcity produces audible bone-creaking with ordinary movement, while its abundance produces silent, painless motion — is one of the few places in the entire thirty-two parts where a substance's function is treated as something that can vary for the better as well as being simply repulsive; the passage acknowledges, almost in passing, that this particular fluid is doing something genuinely necessary and beneficial, even while remaining formally classified among the body's thirty-two impurities.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
vasā

oil-of-the-skin, a dissolved unctuous substance — distinguished from medo (solid fat) as the more fluid, surface-level oiliness that appears on the skin only when the body becomes heated, unlike fat's constant presence beneath skin and flesh.

asītisatasandhi

the hundred and eighty joints — asīti (eighty) + sata (hundred) + sandhi (joint); the traditional total count of the body's joints, all lubricated (or, when synovial fluid is scarce, painfully under-lubricated) by this single substance.

vatthi

the bladder, the urinary receptacle — described with a memorable simile: urine's entry path into it is entirely invisible, unlike its very evident exit path, paralleling the cesspool-and-sealed-jar image used to illustrate the same point.

Completing the Contemplation: The Flower-Garland Simile

Having worked through all thirty-two parts individually, the chapter closes by describing what happens as the meditator's attention widens: just as a person with good eyesight, looking at a garland strung from thirty-two different flowers, sees every flower at once without needing to examine each one separately, so too the whole body becomes evident part after part — and, once attention extends even to other people and animals encountered in daily life, they too stop appearing as beings and start appearing simply as a heap of the same thirty-two parts. The chapter closes with the story of the Elder Mallaka, who attained thirty-two separate first-jhāna absorptions, one for each part, progressing at a rate the tradition still remembered precisely.

Pali Source

Evañhi kesādike koṭṭhāse vaṇṇasaṇṭhānadisokāsaparicchedavasena vavatthapetvā anupubbato nātisīghatotiādinā nayena vaṇṇasaṇṭhānagandhāsayokāsavasena pañcadhā paṭikkūlā paṭikkūlāti manasikaroto paṇṇattisamatikkamāvasāne seyyathāpi cakkhumato purisassa dvattiṃsavaṇṇānaṃ kusumānaṃ ekasuttakaganthitaṃ mālaṃ olokentassa sabbapupphāni apubbāpariyamiva pākaṭāni honti, evameva atthi imasmiṃ kāye kesāti imaṃ kāyaṃ olokentassa sabbe te dhammā apubbāpariyāva pākaṭā honti. Tena vuttaṃ manasikārakosallakathāyaṃ "ādikammikassa hi kesāti manasikaroto manasikāro gantvā muttanti imaṃ pariyosānakoṭṭhāsameva āhacca tiṭṭhatī"ti.

Sace pana bahiddhāpi manasikāraṃ upasaṃharati, athassa evaṃ sabbakoṭṭhāsesu pākaṭībhūtesu āhiṇḍantā manussatiracchānādayo sattākāraṃ vijahitvā koṭṭhāsarāsivaseneva upaṭṭhahanti, tehi ca ajjhohariyamānaṃ pānabhojanādi koṭṭhāsarāsimhi pakkhipamānamiva upaṭṭhāti.

Athassa anupubbamuñcanādivasena paṭikkūlā paṭikkūlāti punappunaṃ manasikaroto anukkamena appanā uppajjati. Tattha kesādīnaṃ vaṇṇasaṇṭhānadisokāsaparicchedavasena upaṭṭhānaṃ uggahanimittaṃ. Sabbākārato paṭikkūlavasena upaṭṭhānaṃ paṭibhāganimittaṃ. Taṃ āsevato bhāvayato vuttanayena asubhakammaṭṭhānesu viya paṭhamajjhānavaseneva appanā uppajjati.

Sā yassa ekova koṭṭhāso pākaṭo hoti, ekasmiṃ vā koṭṭhāse appanaṃ patvā puna aññasmiṃ yogaṃ na karoti, tassa ekāva uppajjati. Yassa pana aneke koṭṭhāsā pākaṭā honti, ekasmiṃ vā jhānaṃ patvā puna aññasmiṃpi yogaṃ karoti, tassa mallakattherassa viya koṭṭhāsagaṇanāya paṭhamajjhānāni nibbattanti.

So kirāyasmā dīghabhāṇakaabhayattheraṃ hatthe gahetvā "āvuso abhaya, imaṃ tāva pañhaṃ uggaṇhāhī"ti vatvā āha — "mallakatthero dvattiṃsakoṭṭhāsesu dvattiṃsāya paṭhamajjhānānaṃ lābhī. Sace rattiṃ ekaṃ, divā ekaṃ samāpajjati, atirekaddhamāsena puna sampajjati, sace pana devasikaṃ ekaṃ samāpajjati, atirekamāsena puna sampajjatī"ti.

Evaṃ paṭhamajjhānavasena ijjhamānampi cetaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ vaṇṇasaṇṭhānādīsu satibalena ijjhanato kāyagatāsatīti vuccati.

Imañca kāyagatāsatimanuyutto bhikkhu aratiratisaho hoti, na ca naṃ arati sahati, uppannaṃ aratiṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya viharati. Bhayabheravasaho hoti, na ca naṃ bhayabheravaṃ sahati, uppannaṃ bhayabheravaṃ abhibhuyya abhibhuyya viharati. Khamo hoti sītassa uṇhassa …pe… pāṇaharānaṃ adhivāsakajātiko hoti (ma. ni. 3.159). Kesādīnaṃ vaṇṇabhedaṃ nissāya catunnaṃ jhānānaṃ lābhī hoti. Cha abhiññā paṭivijjhati (ma. ni. 3.159).

Tasmā have appamatto, anuyuñjetha paṇḍito;
Evaṃ anekānisaṃsaṃ, imaṃ kāyagatāsatinti.

Idaṃ kāyagatāsatiyaṃ vitthārakathāmukhaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

For thus, having determined the parts beginning with hair by way of color, shape, direction, location, and delimitation, and attending, in order, not too quickly, not too slowly, and by the remaining methods already stated, to their fivefold repulsiveness by way of color, shape, smell, origin, and location — thus repeatedly attending, "repulsive, repulsive" — then, once the mere concept has been gone beyond, it is just as with a man of good sight looking at a garland strung on a single thread from thirty-two kinds of flowers: all the flowers become evident to him at once, not one after another in sequence. Just so, for one looking upon this body, thinking "there are, in this body, hairs," all these states become evident at once, not one after another in sequence. For this it was said, in the discussion of skill in attention, "for a beginner attending 'hair,' his attention, proceeding onward, comes to rest precisely on that final part, 'urine.'"

And if he extends his attention outward as well, then, once all the parts have become manifest in this way, human beings, animals, and the rest, as they move about, appear to him, having abandoned the mode of being a "being," simply as a heap of parts; and whatever drink, food, and the like are being swallowed by them appears as though being placed into that very heap of parts.

Then, for one attending again and again, "repulsive, repulsive," by way of releasing them in succession and the rest, absorption arises in due order. Herein, the manifestation of hair and the rest by way of color, shape, direction, location, and delimitation is the learning-sign; the manifestation, in every aspect, by way of repulsiveness, is the counterpart-sign. For one cultivating and developing that, by the method already stated, absorption arises by way of the first jhāna alone, just as in the foulness meditation subjects.

For one for whom only a single part becomes manifest, or who, having reached absorption in one part, makes no further effort regarding another, only a single absorption arises. But for one for whom many parts become manifest, or who, having reached jhāna in one, makes further effort regarding another as well, first jhānas are produced according to the number of parts, as happened for the Elder Mallaka.

It is told that this venerable one, taking the Elder Abhaya, the reciter of the Long Discourses, by the hand, said, "friend Abhaya, learn this matter well first," and then said: "the Elder Mallaka is one who gains thirty-two first jhānas in the thirty-two parts. If he attains one at night and one by day, he accomplishes them all again in a little more than half a month; but if he attains only one each day, he accomplishes them all again in a little more than a month."

Thus, even though this meditation subject succeeds by way of the first jhāna, it is called "mindfulness of the body" because it succeeds by the power of mindfulness applied to color, shape, and the rest.

And a monk devoted to this mindfulness of the body becomes one able to withstand discontent, and discontent does not overcome him — he dwells, overcoming and overcoming any discontent that arises. He becomes one able to withstand fear and terror, and fear and terror do not overcome him — he dwells, overcoming and overcoming any fear and terror that arise. He becomes patient of cold, of heat, ... of a nature to endure things that threaten life. In dependence on the differentiation of color and the rest of the parts, he becomes one who gains the four jhānas; he penetrates the six direct knowledges (M.N. 3.159).

Therefore let the wise one, heedful, / practice this mindfulness of the body, / of such manifold benefit.

This is the opening of the detailed discussion on mindfulness of the body.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The garland simile marks the precise transition-point this whole chapter has been building toward: the thirty-two separate, individually described parts, learned one at a time through recitation, groupings, similes, and painstaking sequential attention, are meant to eventually collapse into a single, simultaneous field of vision — not thirty-two things noticed in succession, but one unified perception of repulsiveness with thirty-two aspects, exactly as a garland's many flowers are seen all at once rather than counted one by one.

The Elder Mallaka's story is a remarkable demonstration of just how far the "releasing in succession" technique, introduced earlier as a beginner's triage method, can eventually be pushed: rather than settling for a single absorption once one part becomes clear, this elder methodically pursued and achieved a separate first-jhāna absorption in each of the thirty-two parts individually, cycling through the entire set on a schedule the tradition tracked with striking numerical precision — a testament to sustained, systematic practice applied to what might otherwise seem an already-completed task.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
ekasuttakaganthita mālā

a garland strung on a single thread — eka (one) + suttaka (thread) + ganthita (strung, bound); the closing image for how thirty-two individually contemplated parts eventually merge into one simultaneous perception.

koṭṭhāsagaṇanā

counting by parts — koṭṭhāsa (part, portion) + gaṇanā (counting); the method by which the Elder Mallaka's thirty-two separate first-jhāna attainments, one per body part, were tracked and remembered.

aratiratisaha

able to withstand discontent and delight — arati (discontent, dissatisfaction) + rati (delight) + saha (able to bear); one of the practical benefits of this meditation, an even-keeled resilience against both negative and positive reactive pulls.

The Canonical Formula in Full

The chapter turns to mindfulness of breathing — praised by the Buddha as peaceful, sublime, unadulterated, and pleasant, a meditation capable of instantly banishing whatever unwholesome states have arisen — and quotes, in full, the celebrated sixteen-step formula from the Ānāpānasati Sutta: four tetrads covering, in turn, the physical breath itself, the accompanying feeling, the state of mind, and finally direct insight, each step framed as something the meditator trains himself to do rather than something that merely happens.

Pali Source

Idāni yaṃ taṃ bhagavatā "ayampi kho, bhikkhave, ānāpānassatisamādhi bhāvito bahulīkato santo ceva paṇīto ca asecanako ca sukho ca vihāro, uppannuppanne ca pāpake akusale dhamme ṭhānaso antaradhāpeti vūpasametī"ti evaṃ pasaṃsitvā —

"Kathaṃ bhāvito ca, bhikkhave, ānāpānassatisamādhi kathaṃ bahulīkato santo ceva paṇīto ca asecanako ca sukho ca vihāro, uppannuppanne ca pāpake akusale dhamme ṭhānaso antaradhāpeti vūpasameti? Idha, bhikkhave, bhikkhu araññagato vā rukkhamūlagato vā suññāgāragato vā nisīdati pallaṅkaṃ ābhujitvā ujuṃ kāyaṃ paṇidhāya parimukhaṃ satiṃ upaṭṭhapetvā, so satova assasati sato passasati. Dīghaṃ vā assasanto dīghaṃ assasāmīti pajānāti. Dīghaṃ vā passasanto…pe… rassaṃ vā assasanto…pe… rassaṃ vā passasanto rassaṃ passasāmīti pajānāti. Sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī assasissāmīti sikkhati. Sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī passasissāmīti sikkhati. Passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ assasissāmīti sikkhati. Passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ passasissāmīti sikkhati. Pītipaṭisaṃvedī… sukhapaṭisaṃvedī… cittasaṅkhārapaṭisaṃvedī… passambhayaṃ cittasaṅkhāraṃ… cittapaṭisaṃvedī… abhippamodayaṃ cittaṃ… samādahaṃ cittaṃ… vimocayaṃ cittaṃ… aniccānupassī… virāgānupassī… nirodhānupassī. Paṭinissaggānupassī assasissāmīti sikkhati. Paṭinissaggānupassī passasissāmīti sikkhatī"ti —

Evaṃ soḷasavatthukaṃ ānāpānassatikammaṭṭhānaṃ niddiṭṭhaṃ. Tassa bhāvanānayo anuppatto. So pana yasmā pāḷivaṇṇanānusāreneva vuccamāno sabbākāraparipūro hoti. Tasmā ayamettha pāḷivaṇṇanāpubbaṅgamo niddeso.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now comes the exposition of that meditation subject which the Blessed One, having praised it thus — "this concentration through mindfulness of breathing too, monks, developed and made much of, is a peaceful and sublime dwelling, unadulterated and pleasant, and it instantly banishes and stills whatever evil, unwholesome states have arisen" —

"And how, monks, is this concentration through mindfulness of breathing developed, how, made much of, is it a peaceful and sublime dwelling, unadulterated and pleasant, and instantly banishes and stills whatever evil, unwholesome states have arisen? Here, monks, a monk, gone to the forest, or to the root of a tree, or to an empty place, sits down, having folded his legs crosswise, having set his body straight, having established mindfulness in front of him. Mindful, he breathes in; mindful, he breathes out. Breathing in long, he understands, 'I breathe in long'; breathing out long, he understands, 'I breathe out long'; breathing in short, he understands, 'I breathe in short'; breathing out short, he understands, 'I breathe out short.' 'Experiencing the whole body, I shall breathe in,' thus he trains; 'experiencing the whole body, I shall breathe out,' thus he trains. 'Calming the bodily formation, I shall breathe in,' thus he trains; 'calming the bodily formation, I shall breathe out,' thus he trains. 'Experiencing joy... experiencing happiness... experiencing the mental formation... calming the mental formation... experiencing the mind... gladdening the mind... concentrating the mind... liberating the mind... contemplating impermanence... contemplating fading away... contemplating cessation... contemplating relinquishment, I shall breathe in,' thus he trains; 'contemplating relinquishment, I shall breathe out,' thus he trains" —

Thus the meditation subject of mindfulness of breathing, with its sixteen bases, was set forth. The exposition of its development has now been reached. And since it becomes complete in every respect only when explained in accordance with a running commentary on the canonical text, the exposition here proceeds first by way of commenting on that text.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The formula's own grammatical shift, from "he understands" (pajānāti) in the first four steps to "thus he trains" (sikkhati) in the remaining twelve, is not a stylistic accident the chapter will pass over — it marks a genuine transition the commentary is about to unpack in detail: the first tetrad describes simply noticing what is already occurring (breath naturally long or short), while everything after it describes an active cultivation the meditator deliberately brings about, a distinction with real consequences for how each step is actually practiced.

The formula's overall architecture — four tetrads of four steps each, moving from the physical breath, to feeling, to the state of mind, and finally to insight into impermanence, fading, cessation, and relinquishment — quietly builds an entire path from the most basic physical anchor all the way to liberating insight within a single, continuous sixteen-step sequence, without ever changing the object of attention away from the breath itself.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
asecanaka

unadulterated, needing no addition — a (without) + secana (sprinkling, adding flavor); describing this concentration as already complete and satisfying in itself, without requiring anything further to make it palatable.

soḷasavatthuka

having sixteen bases — soḷasa (sixteen) + vatthuka (having as its basis); the standing technical name for this meditation subject's complete formula, organized into four tetrads of four steps each.

kāyasaṅkhāra / cittasaṅkhāra

the bodily formation / the mental formation — technical terms (identified later in the chapter as the in-and-out breath itself, and as perception-and-feeling, respectively) that the second and third tetrads instruct the meditator to calm.

Why a Forest Dwelling: The Calf and the Leopard

The chapter explains why the Buddha specifically prescribed a forest, the root of a tree, or an empty place for this practice: a mind long accustomed to running wild after sights and sounds will not willingly climb onto so subtle an object as the breath, and must first be tethered — like a wild calf bound to a post until it can no longer escape and finally settles down beside it. The chapter adds a second, more triumphant image: a monk pursuing this practice in the forest is like a leopard lying in wait to seize its prey, patiently working through the four paths and fruits exactly as a leopard captures buffalo, deer, and boar.

Pali Source

Kathaṃ bhāvito ca, bhikkhave, ānāpānassati samādhīti ettha tāva kathanti ānāpānassatisamādhibhāvanaṃ nānappakārato vitthāretukamyatāpucchā. Bhāvito ca bhikkhave ānāpānassatisamādhīti nānappakārato vitthāretukamyatāya puṭṭhadhammanidassanaṃ.

Araññagato vā…pe… suññāgāragato vāti idamassa ānāpānassatisamādhibhāvanānurūpasenāsanapariggahaparidīpanaṃ. Imassa hi bhikkhuno dīgharattaṃ rūpādīsu ārammaṇesu anuvisaṭaṃ cittaṃ ānāpānassatisamādhiārammaṇaṃ abhiruhituṃ na icchati, kūṭagoṇayuttaratho viya uppathameva dhāvati. Tasmā seyyathāpi nāma gopo kūṭadhenuyā sabbaṃ khīraṃ pivitvā vaḍḍhitaṃ kūṭavacchaṃ dametukāmo dhenuto apanetvā ekamante mahantaṃ thambhaṃ nikhaṇitvā tattha yottena bandheyya, athassa so vaccho ito cito ca vipphanditvā palāyituṃ asakkonto tameva thambhaṃ upanisīdeyya vā upanipajjeyya vā, evameva imināpi bhikkhunā dīgharattaṃ rūpārammaṇādirasapānavaḍḍhitaṃ duṭṭhacittaṃ dametukāmena rūpādiārammaṇato apanetvā araññaṃ vā…pe… suññāgāraṃ vā pavesetvā tattha assāsapassāsathambhe satiyottena bandhitabbaṃ. Evamassa taṃ cittaṃ ito cito ca vipphanditvāpi pubbe āciṇṇārammaṇaṃ alabhamānaṃ satiyottaṃ chinditvā palāyituṃ asakkontaṃ tamevārammaṇaṃ upacārappanāvasena upanisīdati ceva upanipajjati ca. Tenāhu porāṇā —

"Yathā thambhe nibandheyya, vacchaṃ damaṃ naro idha;
Bandheyyevaṃ sakaṃ cittaṃ, satiyārammaṇe daḷha"nti. (pārā. aṭṭha. 2.165; dī. ni. aṭṭha. 3.374; ma. ni. aṭṭha. 1.107)

Evamassetaṃ senāsanaṃ bhāvanānurūpaṃ hoti. Tena vuttaṃ "idamassa ānāpānassatisamādhibhāvanānurūpasenāsanapariggahaparidīpana"nti.

Atha vā yasmā idaṃ kammaṭṭhānappabhede muddhabhūtaṃ sabbaññubuddhapaccekabuddhabuddhasāvakānaṃ visesādhigamadiṭṭhadhammasukhavihārapadaṭṭhānaṃ ānāpānassatikammaṭṭhānaṃ itthipurisahatthiassādisaddasamākulaṃ gāmantaṃ apariccajitvā na sukaraṃ bhāvetuṃ, saddakaṇṭakattā jhānassa. Agāmake pana araññe sukaraṃ yogāvacarena idaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ pariggahetvā ānāpānacatutthajjhānaṃ nibbattetvā tadeva pādakaṃ katvā saṅkhāre sammasitvā aggaphalaṃ arahattaṃ sampāpuṇituṃ. Tasmāssa anurūpasenāsanaṃ dassento bhagavā "araññagato vā"tiādimāha.

Vatthuvijjācariyo viya hi bhagavā, so yathā vatthuvijjācariyo nagarabhūmiṃ passitvā suṭṭhu upaparikkhitvā "ettha nagaraṃ māpethā"ti upadisati, sotthinā ca nagare niṭṭhite rājakulato mahāsakkāraṃ labhati, evameva yogāvacarassa anurūpasenāsanaṃ upaparikkhitvā "ettha kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuñjitabba"nti upadisati, tato tattha kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuttena yoginā kamena arahatte patte "sammāsambuddho vata so bhagavā"ti mahantaṃ sakkāraṃ labhati.

Ayaṃ pana bhikkhu dīpisadisoti vuccati. Yathā hi mahādīpirājā araññe tiṇagahanaṃ vā vanagahanaṃ vā pabbatagahanaṃ vā nissāya nilīyitvā vanamahiṃsagokaṇṇasūkarādayo mige gaṇhāti, evameva ayaṃ araññādīsu kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuñjanto bhikkhu yathākkamena sotāpattisakadāgāmianāgāmiarahattamagge ceva ariyaphalañca gaṇhatīti veditabbo. Tenāhu porāṇā —

"Yathāpi dīpiko nāma, nilīyitvā gaṇhatī mige;
Tathevāyaṃ buddhaputto, yuttayogo vipassako;
Araññaṃ pavisitvāna, gaṇhāti phalamuttama"nti. (pārā. aṭṭha. 2.165; dī. ni. aṭṭha. 2.374; ma. ni. aṭṭha. 1.107)

Tenassa parakkamajavayoggabhūmiṃ araññasenāsanaṃ dassento bhagavā "araññagato vā"tiādimāha.

Tattha araññagatoti "araññanti nikkhamitvā bahi indakhīlā sabbametaṃ arañña"nti (vibha. 529) ca, "āraññakaṃ nāma senāsanaṃ pañcadhanusatikaṃ pacchima"nti (pārā. 654) ca evaṃ vuttalakkhaṇesu araññesu yaṃkiñci pavivekasukhaṃ araññaṃ gato. Rukkhamūlagatoti rukkhasamīpaṃ gato. Suññāgāragatoti suññaṃ vivittokāsaṃ gato. Ettha ca ṭhapetvā araññañca rukkhamūlañca avasesasattavidhasenāsanagatopi suññāgāragatoti vattuṃ vaṭṭati.

Ru-Yi Translation

As to "and how, monks, is this concentration through mindfulness of breathing developed" — herein, first, "how" is a question intended to elicit a detailed explanation, in various ways, of the development of this concentration; "developed, monks, is this concentration through mindfulness of breathing" is an indication of the state of affairs that has been asked about, for the purpose of that detailed explanation.

"Gone to the forest ... gone to an empty place": this indicates the choice of a dwelling suited to the development of this concentration through mindfulness of breathing. For this monk's mind, having long spread out among such objects as visible forms, does not wish to mount up onto the object of concentration through mindfulness of breathing — it runs off the road entirely, like a chariot yoked to an unbroken ox. Therefore, just as a cowherd, wishing to tame an unruly calf that has drunk all its unruly mother's milk and grown up, might take it away from its mother, dig a great post into the ground somewhere, and bind it there with a rope — whereupon that calf, struggling this way and that but unable to escape, would sit or lie down right beside that very post — just so, this monk too, wishing to tame his own vicious mind, long grown wild on the flavor of drinking in visible forms and other such objects, should remove it from those objects, lead it into the forest, or to the root of a tree, or to an empty place, and there bind it with the rope of mindfulness to the post of in-and-out breathing. Then, though his mind struggles this way and that, no longer obtaining the objects it was previously accustomed to, and unable to sever the rope of mindfulness and escape, it comes to sit and lie down, by way of access and absorption, right beside that very object. Hence the ancients said —

"Just as a man here, taming a calf, / would bind it to a post, / so should one bind one's own mind firmly / to its object, by mindfulness."

Thus this dwelling is suited to development. For this it was said, "this indicates the choice of a dwelling suited to the development of this concentration through mindfulness of breathing."

Or else, since this meditation subject of mindfulness of breathing, foremost among the divisions of meditation subjects, is the proximate cause of the distinguished attainments and the happy dwelling here and now of omniscient Buddhas, Paccekabuddhas, and the Buddha's own disciples, it is not easy to develop without giving up the outskirts of a village crowded with the sounds of women, men, elephants, horses, and the like — since sound is a thorn to jhāna. But in a village-free forest, it is easy for a meditator, having taken up this meditation subject, to produce the fourth jhāna of breathing, and, making that very jhāna the foundation, to contemplate the formations and attain the highest fruit, arahantship. Therefore, showing him a suitable dwelling, the Blessed One said, "gone to the forest," and so on.

For the Blessed One is like a master of building-sites: just as a master of building-sites, having seen a piece of ground for a city and examined it thoroughly, advises, "build the city here," and, once the city is safely completed, receives great honor from the royal family — just so, having examined a dwelling suited to the meditator, he advises, "the meditation subject should be pursued here"; and then, once the meditator devoted to the meditation subject there has, in due course, attained arahantship, he receives great honor, with the thought, "that Blessed One is indeed a perfectly self-enlightened one."

This monk, moreover, is said to resemble a leopard. For just as a great leopard-king, lying in wait in the forest, concealed by a thicket of grass, or a jungle-thicket, or a mountain-thicket, seizes such prey as forest buffalo, deer, and boar — just so this monk, pursuing his meditation subject in the forest and elsewhere, seizes, in due order, the paths of stream-entry, once-return, non-return, and arahantship, together with the noble fruits — so it should be understood. Hence the ancients said —

"Just as one called a leopard, / concealed, seizes deer, / just so this child of the Buddha, / devoted to the practice, an insight-seer, / having entered the forest, / seizes the highest fruit."

Therefore, showing him the forest dwelling that is the proper ground for the speed of his exertion, the Blessed One said, "gone to the forest," and so on.

Herein, "gone to the forest" means: gone to whatever forest offers the happiness of seclusion, among those forests defined thus: "'forest' means all that lies beyond the boundary-post, outside [a village]" (Vibh. 529), and "a forest dwelling, at the least, is five hundred bow-lengths distant" (Pārā. 654). "Gone to the root of a tree" means: gone near a tree. "Gone to an empty place" means: gone to an empty, secluded spot. And here, setting aside the forest and the root of a tree, one who has gone to any of the remaining seven kinds of dwelling may also be said to have "gone to an empty place."

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The wild-calf simile, memorialized in the ancients' own verse, makes explicit something the chapter has hinted at repeatedly across other meditation subjects: a mind is not gently invited onto a subtle object like the breath — it must be physically removed from its accustomed feeding grounds (sights, sounds) and forcibly tethered until struggling proves useless. The image is notably unsentimental about the process: the calf does not come to enjoy the post, it simply exhausts itself trying to escape and settles beside it only because escape has become impossible.

The leopard simile, introduced immediately after and cast in a far more triumphant register than the calf's story, reframes the same forest setting from a place of restraint into a place of predatory opportunity: the meditator is no longer the tethered animal but the patient hunter, and the four paths and fruits become prey seized in succession. Reading the two similes together, the chapter presents forest solitude as serving two simultaneous functions for the very same practice — a cage for an unruly mind, and a hunting ground for a skilled one — depending on which side of the meditator's own development is being described.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
saddakaṇṭaka

sound as a thorn — sadda (sound) + kaṇṭaka (a thorn); the specific reason a village setting, however otherwise pleasant, actively obstructs jhāna, since even a beneficial sound intrudes on concentration exactly as a thorn intrudes on skin.

vatthuvijjācariya

a master of building-sites — vatthu (a site, a plot of ground) + vijjā (knowledge, science) + ācariya (teacher); the simile for the Buddha's own role in identifying which dwelling suits which meditator, paralleling a professional site-surveyor's expertise.

indakhīla

a boundary-post, a city gate-post — the traditional marker defining where a village or town ends and genuine "forest" (in the technical sense relevant to this meditation subject) begins.

Sitting Down, and Establishing Mindfulness

The chapter turns to the formula's physical instructions: the cross-legged posture, arranged so precisely — vertebra fitted to vertebra — that skin, flesh, and sinews never buckle under their own weight, sparing the meditator the momentary discomforts that would otherwise scatter concentration; and "mindfulness established in front," a phrase the chapter shows, through the Paṭisambhidāmagga's own three-part gloss, to mean something more technical than merely "facing the object."

Pali Source

Evamassa ututtayānukūlaṃ dhātucariyānukūlañca ānāpānassatibhāvanānurūpaṃ senāsanaṃ upadisitvā alīnānuddhaccapakkhikaṃ santaṃ iriyāpathaṃ upadisanto nisīdatīti āha. Athassa nisajjāya daḷhabhāvaṃ assāsapassāsānaṃ pavattanasukhataṃ ārammaṇapariggahūpāyañca dassento pallaṅkaṃ ābhujitvātiādimāha. Tattha pallaṅkanti samantato ūrubaddhāsanaṃ. Ābhujitvāti bandhitvā. Ujuṃ kāyaṃ paṇidhāyāti uparimasarīraṃ ujukaṃ ṭhapetvā. Aṭṭhārasapiṭṭhikaṇṭake koṭiyā koṭiṃ paṭipādetvā. Evañhi nisīdantassa cammamaṃsanhārūni na paṇamanti. Athassa yā tesaṃ paṇamanappaccayā khaṇe khaṇe vedanā uppajjeyyuṃ, tā na uppajjanti. Tāsu anuppajjamānāsu cittaṃ ekaggaṃ hoti, kammaṭṭhānaṃ na paripatati, vuddhiṃ phātiṃ upagacchati. Parimukhaṃ satiṃ upaṭṭhapetvāti kammaṭṭhānābhimukhaṃ satiṃ ṭhapayitvā. Atha vā parīti pariggahaṭṭho. Mukhanti niyyānaṭṭho. Satīti upaṭṭhānaṭṭho. Tena vuccati "parimukhaṃ sati"nti evaṃ paṭisambhidāyaṃ (paṭi. ma. 1.164) vuttanayenapettha attho daṭṭhabbo. Tatrāyaṃ saṅkhepo, pariggahitaniyyānaṃ satiṃ katvāti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Having thus advised a dwelling suited to the three seasons and to a fitting elemental constitution, and appropriate for the development of mindfulness of breathing, the Blessed One, advising a peaceful posture inclined toward neither sloth nor restlessness, said "sits down." Then, showing him the firmness of a proper sitting posture, the ease with which the in-and-out breaths then proceed, and the method of grasping the object, he said "having folded his legs crosswise," and so on. Herein, "crosswise" means a posture in which the thighs are bound all around. "Having folded" means having bound. "Having set his body straight" means having placed the upper body upright, fitting vertebra-tip to vertebra-tip among the eighteen vertebrae of the spine. For one sitting thus, the skin, flesh, and tendons do not buckle; and, they not buckling, the sensations that would otherwise arise moment by moment from that buckling do not arise; and, these not arising, the mind becomes one-pointed, the meditation subject does not collapse, and it advances toward growth and increase. "Having established mindfulness in front of him" means: having placed mindfulness facing the meditation subject. Or else, "pari" has the sense of grasping; "mukha" has the sense of outlet; "sati" has the sense of establishment. By this it is said, "mindfulness in front" — and the meaning here should be seen in accordance with the method stated thus in the Paṭisambhidāmagga. This, herein, is the summary: having made mindfulness that which grasps the outlet.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The insistence on a precisely aligned spine — "fitting vertebra-tip to vertebra-tip" — is not presented as a matter of formal correctness for its own sake, but as directly causal for concentration: a misaligned spine produces buckling in the skin, flesh, and sinews, which produces recurring low-level physical discomfort, which in turn disrupts the very one-pointedness the meditation is trying to establish. The chapter traces a clean causal chain from posture, through physiology, to the meditation subject's own success or failure — sound and practical bodily advice embedded within an otherwise highly technical commentarial passage.

The threefold etymological gloss on "parimukhaṃ sati" — pari as grasping, mukha as outlet, sati as establishment — shows the chapter working to extract a more precise technical sense from a phrase that, taken at face value, might simply mean "mindfulness turned toward the front" or "toward the face." By grounding the reading in the Paṭisambhidāmagga's own analysis, the chapter arrives at something closer to "mindfulness that has grasped hold of the outlet" — a description of an active mental grip on the breath's point of entry and exit, rather than a merely spatial orientation.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
aṭṭhārasapiṭṭhikaṇṭaka

the eighteen vertebrae of the spine — aṭṭhārasa (eighteen) + piṭṭhikaṇṭaka (a spine-bone, vertebra); the precise anatomical detail underlying the instruction to sit with the spine correctly aligned, vertebra fitted to vertebra.

parimukhaṃ sati

mindfulness established in front / mindfulness that grasps the outlet — a phrase given a technical three-part etymology (pari = grasping, mukha = outlet, sati = establishment) rather than a simple spatial reading, drawn from the Paṭisambhidāmagga.

niyyānaṭṭha

the sense of an outlet, a way out — niyyāna (going forth, an exit); one of the three components of the technical gloss on "mukha" in this section's etymology, referring to the breath's point of passage.

Long and Short Breathing

The chapter explains what it actually means for a monk to be "mindful," and then works out, through careful comparison with elephants, snakes, dogs, and hares, exactly what "long" and "short" breathing mean — not fixed durations but relative ones, measured against how long a given creature's own natural breath-cycle takes to fill and empty a stretch of bodily space, whether that stretch is traversed slowly or quickly.

Pali Source

So satova assasati sato passasatīti so bhikkhu evaṃ nisīditvā evañca satiṃ upaṭṭhapetvā taṃ satiṃ avijahanto sato eva assasati sato passasati, satokārī hotīti vuttaṃ hoti. Idāni yehākārehi satokārī hoti, te dassetuṃ dīghaṃ vā assasantotiādimāha. Vuttañhetaṃ paṭisambhidāyaṃ "so satova assasati sato passasatī"ti etasseva vibhaṅge —

"Bāttiṃsāya ākārehi sato kārī hoti. Dīghaṃ assāsavasena cittassa ekaggataṃ avikkhepaṃ pajānato sati upaṭṭhitā hoti. Tāya satiyā tena ñāṇena sato kārī hoti. Dīghaṃ passāsavasena…pe… paṭinissaggānupassī assāsavasena. Paṭinissaggānupassī passāsavasena cittassa ekaggataṃ avikkhepaṃ pajānato sati upaṭṭhitā hoti. Tāya satiyā tena ñāṇena sato kārī hotī"ti. (paṭi. ma. 1.165)

Tattha dīghaṃ vā assasantoti dīghaṃ vā assāsaṃ pavattayanto. Assāsoti bahi nikkhamanavāto. Passāsoti anto pavisanavātoti vinayaṭṭhakathāyaṃ vuttaṃ. Suttantaṭṭhakathāsu pana uppaṭipāṭiyā āgataṃ. Tattha sabbesampi gabbhaseyyakānaṃ mātukucchito nikkhamanakāle paṭhamaṃ abbhantaravāto bahi nikkhamati. Pacchā bāhiravāto sukhumarajaṃ gahetvā abbhantaraṃ pavisanto tāluṃ āhacca nibbāyati. Evaṃ tāva assāsapassāsā veditabbā.

Yā pana tesaṃ dīgharassatā, sā addhānavasena veditabbā. Yathā hi okāsaddhānaṃ pharitvā ṭhitaṃ udakaṃ vā vālikā vā "dīghamudakaṃ dīghā vālikā, rassamudakaṃ rassā vālikā"ti vuccati, evaṃ cuṇṇavicuṇṇāpi assāsapassāsā hatthisarīre ca ahisarīre ca tesaṃ attabhāvasaṅkhātaṃ dīghaṃ addhānaṃ saṇikaṃ pūretvā saṇikameva nikkhamanti. Tasmā dīghāti vuccanti. Sunakhasasādīnaṃ attabhāvasaṅkhātaṃ rassaṃ addhānaṃ sīghaṃ pūretvā sīghameva nikkhamanti, tasmā rassāti vuccanti. Manussesu pana keci hatthiahiādayo viya kāladdhānavasena dīghaṃ assasanti ca passasanti ca. Keci sunakhasasādayo viya rassaṃ, tasmā tesaṃ kālavasena dīghamaddhānaṃ nikkhamantā ca pavisantā ca te "dīghā" ittaramaddhānaṃ nikkhamantā ca pavisantā ca "rassā"ti veditabbā.

Tatrāyaṃ bhikkhu navahākārehi dīghaṃ assasanto passasanto ca "dīghaṃ assasāmi, passasāmī"ti pajānāti. Evaṃ pajānato cassa ekenākārena kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānabhāvanā sampajjatīti veditabbā. Yathāha paṭisambhidāyaṃ (paṭi. ma. 1.166) —

"Kathaṃ dīghaṃ assasanto dīghaṃ assasāmīti pajānāti. Dīghaṃ passasanto dīghaṃ passasāmīti pajānāti. Dīghaṃ assāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasati. Dīghaṃ passāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte passasati. Dīghaṃ assāsapassāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasatipi passasatipi. Dīghaṃ assāsapassāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasatopi passasatopi chando uppajjati. Chandavasena tato sukhumataraṃ dīghaṃ assāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasati. Chandavasena tato sukhumataraṃ dīghaṃ passāsaṃ…pe… dīghaṃ assāsapassāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasatipi passasatipi. Chandavasena tato sukhumataraṃ dīghaṃ assāsapassāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasatopi passasatopi pāmojjaṃ uppajjati. Pāmojjavasena tato sukhumataraṃ dīghaṃ assāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasati. Pāmojjavasena tato sukhumataraṃ dīghaṃ passāsaṃ…pe… dīghaṃ assāsapassāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasatipi passasatipi. Pāmojjavasena tato sukhumataraṃ dīghaṃ assāsapassāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte assasatopi passasatopi dīghaṃ assāsapassāsā cittaṃ vivattati, upekkhā saṇṭhāti. Imehi navahi ākārehi dīghaṃ assāsapassāsā kāyo. Upaṭṭhānaṃ sati. Anupassanā ñāṇaṃ. Kāyo upaṭṭhānaṃ, no sati. Sati upaṭṭhānañceva sati ca. Tāya satiyā tena ñāṇena taṃ kāyaṃ anupassati. Tena vuccati kāye kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānabhāvanā"ti.

Esa nayo rassapadepi. Ayaṃ pana viseso, yathā ettha "dīghaṃ assāsaṃ addhānasaṅkhāte"ti vuttaṃ, evamidha "rassaṃ assāsaṃ ittarasaṅkhāte assasatī"ti āgataṃ. Tasmā rassavasena yāva "tena vuccati kāye kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānabhāvanā"ti, tāva yojetabbaṃ.

Evaṃ ayaṃ addhānavasena ittaravasena ca imehākārehi assāsapassāse pajānanto dīghaṃ vā assasanto dīghaṃ assasāmīti pajānāti…pe… rassaṃ vā passasanto rassaṃ passasāmīti pajānātīti veditabbo. Evaṃ pajānato cassa —

Dīgho rasso ca assāso,
Passāsopi ca tādiso;
Cattāro vaṇṇā vattanti,
Nāsikaggeva bhikkhunoti. (pārā. aṭṭha. 2.165)

Ru-Yi Translation

"Mindful, he breathes in; mindful, he breathes out" means: this monk, having sat down thus and having established mindfulness in this way, without abandoning that mindfulness, breathes in mindfully and breathes out mindfully — he is one who acts with mindfulness, this is what is meant. Now, to show the ways in which he acts with mindfulness, it was said, "breathing in long," and so on. For it is said in the Paṭisambhidāmagga, in its own analysis of "mindful, he breathes in; mindful, he breathes out" —

"He acts with mindfulness in thirty-two ways. For one understanding, by way of a long in-breath, the one-pointedness and non-distraction of the mind, mindfulness is established; by that mindfulness, by that knowledge, he acts with mindfulness. By way of a long out-breath ... by way of an in-breath contemplating relinquishment. For one understanding, by way of an out-breath contemplating relinquishment, the one-pointedness and non-distraction of the mind, mindfulness is established; by that mindfulness, by that knowledge, he acts with mindfulness." (Paṭis. 1.165)

Herein, "breathing in long" means: causing a long in-breath to occur. "In-breath" is the wind going out; "out-breath" is the wind going in — so it is said in the Vinaya commentary. But in the Discourse commentaries, it is given in the reverse order. Herein, it should be understood that, for all those born from a womb, at the time of emerging from the mother's womb, the internal wind first goes outward; afterward, the external wind, taking in fine dust, enters within, and, striking the palate, subsides. Thus, first, in-breath and out-breath should be understood in this way.

As for their length or shortness, that should be understood by way of extent. For just as water or sand, spread out and standing over an extent of space, is said to be "long water, long sand" or "short water, short sand," so too the in-breaths and out-breaths, however finely divided, in the body of an elephant or of a snake, slowly filling and slowly emerging through the extent of space reckoned as their own bodily frame, are for that reason called "long." Those of a dog, a hare, and the like, quickly filling and quickly emerging through the extent reckoned as their own bodily frame, are for that reason called "short." Among human beings, however, some, like the elephant, the snake, and the like, breathe in and out long by way of time; others, like the dog, the hare, and the like, breathe short. Therefore, it should be understood that those whose breath, by way of time, enters and emerges over a long extent are "long," and those whose breath enters and emerges over a brief extent are "short."

Herein, this monk, breathing in and out long by nine ways, understands, "I breathe in long, I breathe out long." For one understanding thus, it should be understood that the development of the establishment of mindfulness by way of contemplating the body is accomplished in one respect. As it is said in the Paṭisambhidāmagga —

"How does one, breathing in long, understand, 'I breathe in long'? Breathing out long, one understands, 'I breathe out long.' One breathes in a long in-breath reckoned by extent. One breathes out a long out-breath reckoned by extent. One breathes in, and also breathes out, a long in-and-out-breath reckoned by extent. For one breathing in, and also breathing out, a long in-and-out-breath reckoned by extent, zeal arises. By way of that zeal, one breathes in a long in-breath, finer than before, reckoned by extent. By way of that zeal, one breathes out a long out-breath, finer than before ... one breathes in, and also breathes out, a long in-and-out-breath, finer than before, reckoned by extent. For one, by way of that zeal, breathing in, and also breathing out, a long in-and-out-breath, finer than before, reckoned by extent, gladness arises. By way of that gladness, one breathes in a long in-breath, finer still, reckoned by extent. By way of that gladness, one breathes out a long out-breath, finer still ... one breathes in, and also breathes out, a long in-and-out-breath, finer still, reckoned by extent. For one, by way of that gladness, breathing in, and also breathing out, a long in-and-out-breath, finer still, reckoned by extent, the mind turns away from the long in-and-out-breath, and equanimity becomes established. By these nine ways, the long in-and-out-breath is the body; the establishment is mindfulness; the contemplation is knowledge. The body is the establishment, but it is not mindfulness; mindfulness is both the establishment and mindfulness. By that mindfulness, by that knowledge, one contemplates that body. Hence it is called 'the development of the establishment of mindfulness by way of contemplating the body in the body.'"

The same method applies to the "short" term as well. But this is the distinction: just as here it was said, "a long in-breath reckoned by extent," so here it comes down as, "one breathes in a short in-breath reckoned as brief." Therefore, it should be applied, by way of the short term, all the way up to "hence it is called the development of the establishment of mindfulness by way of contemplating the body in the body."

Thus, this monk, understanding the in-breaths and out-breaths by these ways, by way both of extent and of brevity, should be understood as, breathing in long, understanding, "I breathe in long" ... breathing out short, understanding, "I breathe out short." For one understanding thus —

Long and short in-breath, / and out-breath likewise such; / four kinds proceed / at the very tip of a monk's nose.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The definition of "long" and "short" breathing turns out to be entirely relative rather than absolute, measured not against any fixed unit of time but against how long it takes a given creature's own breath to traverse the extent of its own body — the same underlying principle (water or sand spread over a stretch of space being called "long" or "short" according to that stretch) applies whether the breather is an elephant with a naturally long breath-cycle or a hare with a naturally short one, and even among individual humans, some breathe more like an elephant and others more like a hare, without either being wrong.

The nine-step progression embedded in the Paṭisambhidāmagga citation — plain understanding, then zeal-refined understanding, then gladness-refined understanding, each producing an even finer breath — models something the whole ānāpānassati chapter keeps returning to: sustained, correctly-applied attention does not merely observe an unchanging object, it actively refines the breath itself into something progressively subtler, with the meditator's own developing zeal and gladness serving as the mechanism of that refinement rather than any deliberate manipulation of the breath.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
addhāna / ittara

extent, duration / brief, momentary — the two technical terms used to define "long" and "short" breathing relatively, by the extent of bodily space a breath fills and empties, rather than by any fixed unit of clock-time.

assāsa / passāsa

in-breath / out-breath — the chapter notes a genuine disagreement between the Vinaya commentaries and the Discourse commentaries over which term names the outward versus the inward breath, resolved here by simply presenting the Discourse-commentary order used throughout the rest of this exposition.

chanda / pāmojja

zeal / gladness — the two successive refining factors in the ninefold breath-analysis, each producing a progressively subtler breath as the meditator's engagement with the practice deepens.

The Whole Breath-Body, and Calming the Bodily Formation

The chapter takes up the tetrad's final two steps: training to know a breath's beginning, middle, and end in their entirety — a skill some meditators find easy and others find genuinely difficult, depending on which portion of the breath happens to stand out to them — and training to calm the "bodily formation," identified here as the in-and-out breath itself, illustrated by the palpable physical difference between a runner who has just stopped, gasping through the mouth, and that same person once rested, cooled, and breathing so faintly it can hardly be detected at all.

Pali Source

Sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī assasissāmi…pe… passasissāmīti sikkhatīti sakalassa assāsakāyassa ādimajjhapariyosānaṃ viditaṃ karonto pākaṭaṃ karonto assasissāmīti sikkhati. Sakalassa passāsakāyassa ādimajjhapariyosānaṃ viditaṃ karonto pākaṭaṃ karonto passasissāmīti sikkhati. Evaṃ viditaṃ karonto pākaṭaṃ karonto ñāṇasampayuttacittena assasati ceva passasati ca. Tasmā "assasissāmi passasissāmī"ti sikkhatīti vuccati. Ekassa hi bhikkhuno cuṇṇavicuṇṇavisaṭe assāsakāye passāsakāye vā ādi pākaṭo hoti, na majjhapariyosānaṃ. So ādimeva pariggahetuṃ sakkoti, majjhapariyosāne kilamati. Ekassa majjhaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti, na ādipariyosānaṃ. Ekassa pariyosānaṃ pākaṭaṃ hoti, na ādimajjhaṃ. So pariyosānaṃyeva pariggahetuṃ sakkoti, ādimajjhe kilamati. Ekassa sabbampi pākaṭaṃ hoti, so sabbampi pariggahetuṃ sakkoti, na katthaci kilamati, tādisena bhavitabbanti dassento āha — "sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī assasissāmīti…pe… passasissāmīti sikkhatī"ti.

Tattha sikkhatīti evaṃ ghaṭati vāyamati. Yo vā tathābhūtassa saṃvaro, ayamettha adhisīlasikkhā. Yo tathābhūtassa samādhi, ayaṃ adhicittasikkhā. Yā tathābhūtassa paññā, ayaṃ adhipaññāsikkhāti imā tisso sikkhāyo tasmiṃ ārammaṇe tāya satiyā tena manasikārena sikkhati āsevati bhāveti bahulīkarotīti evamettha attho daṭṭhabbo.

Tattha yasmā purimanaye kevalaṃ assasitabbaṃ passasitabbameva, na ca aññaṃ kiñci kātabbaṃ. Ito paṭṭhāya pana ñāṇuppādanādīsu yogo karaṇīyo. Tasmā tattha assasāmīti pajānāti passasāmīti pajānāticceva vattamānakālavasena pāḷiṃ vatvā ito paṭṭhāya kattabbassa ñāṇuppādanādino ākārassa dassanatthaṃ sabbakāyapaṭisaṃvedī assasissāmītiādinā nayena anāgatavacanavasena pāḷi āropitāti veditabbā.

Passambhayaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ assasissāmīti…pe… passasissāmīti sikkhatīti oḷārikaṃ kāyasaṅkhāraṃ passambhento paṭippassambhento nirodhento vūpasamento assasissāmi passasissāmīti sikkhati. Tatra evaṃ oḷārikasukhumatā ca passaddhi ca veditabbā. Imassa hi bhikkhuno pubbe apariggahitakāle kāyo ca cittañca sadarathā honti oḷārikā. Kāyacittānaṃ oḷārikatte avūpasante assāsapassāsāpi oḷārikā honti, balavatarā hutvā pavattanti, nāsikā nappahoti, mukhena assasantopi passasantopi tiṭṭhati. Yadā panassa kāyopi cittampi pariggahitā honti, tadā te santā honti vūpasantā. Tesu vūpasantesu assāsapassāsā sukhumā hutvā pavattanti, "atthi nu kho natthī"ti vicetabbatākārappattā honti.

Seyyathāpi purisassa dhāvitvā, pabbatā vā orohitvā, mahābhāraṃ vā sīsato oropetvā ṭhitassa oḷārikā assāsapassāsā honti, nāsikā nappahoti, mukhena assasantopi passasantopi tiṭṭhati. Yadā panesa taṃ parissamaṃ vinodetvā nhatvā ca pivitvā ca allasāṭakaṃ hadaye katvā sītāya chāyāya nipanno hoti, athassa te assāsapassāsā sukhumā honti "atthi nu kho natthī"ti vicetabbatākārappattā, evameva imassa bhikkhuno pubbe apariggahitakāle kāyo ca…pe… vicetabbatākārappattā honti. Taṃ kissa hetu? Tathā hissa pubbe apariggahitakāle "oḷārikoḷārike kāyasaṅkhāre passambhemī"ti ābhogasamannāhāramanasikārapaccavekkhaṇā natthi, pariggahitakāle pana atthi. Tenassa apariggahitakālato pariggahitakāle kāyasaṅkhāro sukhumo hoti. Tenāhu porāṇā —

"Sāraddhe kāye citte ca, adhimattaṃ pavattati;
Asāraddhamhi kāyamhi, sukhumaṃ sampavattatī"ti. (pārā. aṭṭha. 2.165)

Pariggahepi oḷāriko, paṭhamajjhānupacāre sukhumo. Tasmimpi oḷāriko, paṭhamajjhāne sukhumo. Paṭhamajjhāne ca dutiyajjhānupacāre ca oḷāriko, dutiyajjhāne sukhumo. Dutiyajjhāne ca tatiyajjhānupacāre ca oḷāriko, tatiyajjhāne sukhumo. Tatiyajjhāne ca catutthajjhānupacāre ca oḷāriko, catutthajjhāne atisukhumo appavattimeva pāpuṇātīti. Idaṃ tāva dīghabhāṇakasaṃyuttabhāṇakānaṃ mataṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

"'Experiencing the whole body, I shall breathe in ... I shall breathe out,' thus he trains": making known, making evident, the beginning, middle, and end of the entire body of the in-breath, he trains, "I shall breathe in." Making known, making evident, the beginning, middle, and end of the entire body of the out-breath, he trains, "I shall breathe out." Making it known and evident in this way, he breathes in and breathes out with a mind conjoined with knowledge. Therefore it is said, "he trains, 'I shall breathe in, I shall breathe out.'" For one monk, in the finely divided, spread-out body of the in-breath or the out-breath, the beginning is evident, but not the middle and end — he is able to grasp only the beginning, and grows weary at the middle and end. For another, the middle is evident, but not the beginning and end. For another, the end is evident, but not the beginning and middle — he is able to grasp only the end, and grows weary at the beginning and middle. For another, the whole is evident — he is able to grasp the whole, and grows weary nowhere. Showing that one should be like this last, it was said, "'experiencing the whole body, I shall breathe in ... I shall breathe out,' thus he trains."

Herein, "trains" means: he strives, he makes effort in this way. Or else: whatever restraint there is for one in that state — this, here, is the higher training in virtue; whatever concentration there is for one in that state — this is the higher training in mind; whatever understanding there is for one in that state — this is the higher training in wisdom. The meaning here should be seen thus: he trains in, cultivates, develops, and makes much of these three trainings, with regard to that object, by that mindfulness, by that attention.

Herein, since in the earlier method only breathing in and breathing out need be done, and nothing further need be done, but from this point onward, effort must be applied to such things as the production of knowledge — therefore, the canonical text there was stated by way of the present tense, "he understands, 'I breathe in'; he understands, 'I breathe out,'" but, from this point onward, in order to show the mode of what is to be done, such as the production of knowledge, the canonical text has been framed by way of the future tense, in the manner of "'experiencing the whole body, I shall breathe in,'" and so on — this should be understood.

"'Calming the bodily formation, I shall breathe in ... I shall breathe out,' thus he trains" means: calming, thoroughly calming, causing to cease, causing to subside, the gross bodily formation, he trains, "I shall breathe in, I shall breathe out." Herein, this is how grossness, fineness, and calming should be understood. For this monk, in the time before this practice was undertaken, body and mind are agitated and gross. As long as the grossness of body and mind remains unstilled, the in-breaths and out-breaths too are gross, occurring with greater force, so that the nostrils do not suffice, and he continues breathing in and out through the mouth as well. But when his body and mind have become the object of this undertaking, then they become peaceful and stilled. When they are stilled, the in-breaths and out-breaths occur subtly, reaching a state where one must consider, "do they exist, or do they not?"

Just as, for a man who has been running, or has come down from a mountain, or has just set down a heavy load from his head, the in-breaths and out-breaths are gross, so that the nostrils do not suffice, and he continues breathing in and out through the mouth as well — but when he has dispelled that fatigue, bathed, drunk, placed a wet cloth over his heart, and lain down in cool shade, then his in-breaths and out-breaths become subtle, reaching a state where one must consider, "do they exist, or do they not?" — just so, for this monk, in the time before this practice was undertaken, body and mind ... reach a state where one must consider, "do they exist, or do they not?" Why is this? Because, in the time before this practice was undertaken, there is no attention, no bringing-to-mind, no reflection such as, "I am calming the gross bodily formation" — but in the time after this practice has been undertaken, there is. Therefore, from the time before undertaking this practice to the time after undertaking it, the bodily formation becomes subtle. Hence the ancients said —

"When body and mind are agitated, / it occurs to an excessive degree; / when the body is unagitated, / it occurs subtly."

Even in the stage of grasping it, it is gross; in the access to the first jhāna, it is subtle. Even there, it is gross; in the first jhāna itself, it is subtle. In the first jhāna, and in the access to the second jhāna, it is gross; in the second jhāna, it is subtle. In the second jhāna, and in the access to the third jhāna, it is gross; in the third jhāna, it is subtle. In the third jhāna, and in the access to the fourth jhāna, it is gross; in the fourth jhāna, it is extremely subtle, reaching complete non-occurrence. This, first, is the opinion of the reciters of the Long Discourses and the Connected Discourses.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The chapter's careful distinction between the earlier steps (present tense, "he understands") and the later ones (future tense, "I shall breathe in") is a small grammatical observation put to real interpretive work: it marks the precise point in the sixteen-step formula where the practice shifts from simply noticing an already-occurring natural process to actively cultivating a deliberate refinement of it — the future tense signals intention and effort in a way the earlier present-tense observations do not.

The parallel between a runner's labored, mouth-open breathing settling into near-imperceptibility once rested, and the meditator's own coarse breath settling as body and mind calm, is more than illustrative — it establishes the actual physiological mechanism the "calming the bodily formation" step depends on: breath does not become subtle because the meditator forces it to slow down, but because it is a direct symptom of an underlying bodily and mental agitation that is being addressed at its source, exactly as the runner's own breath naturally quiets once the actual exertion causing it has ended.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
assāsakāya / passāsakāya

the body of the in-breath / the body of the out-breath — kāya (body, mass) here used not for the physical body but for the entire extended "body" of a single breath, from its beginning through its middle to its end.

adhisīlasikkhā / adhicittasikkhā / adhipaññāsikkhā

the higher training in virtue / in mind / in wisdom — the traditional threefold training scheme, here shown to be simultaneously present within this single meditation subject's own practice of restraint, concentration, and understanding.

oḷārika / sukhuma

gross / subtle — the recurring analytical pair tracking the bodily formation's progressive refinement across the stages from ordinary pre-practice breathing all the way through each successive jhāna, reaching complete non-occurrence at the fourth.

Learning the Practice: The Five Bonds, and the Eight-Step Method

Before actual practice can begin, the chapter specifies that a clansman seeking arahantship through this meditation subject must learn it from a teacher through five distinct stages — learning, questioning, establishing, absorption, and characteristic — each carefully defined, before the chapter introduces its own celebrated summarizing verse: counting, following, touching, fixing, observing, turning away, purification, and reviewing — the eight-step progression the remainder of the chapter will now unpack one term at a time.

Pali Source

Yasmā panettha idameva catukkaṃ ādikammikassa kammaṭṭhānavasena vuttaṃ. Itarāni pana tīṇi catukkāni ettha pattajjhānassa vedanācittadhammānupassanāvasena vuttāni. Tasmā idaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ bhāvetvā ānāpānacatutthajjhānapadaṭṭhānāya vipassanāya saha paṭisambhidāhi arahattaṃ pāpuṇitukāmena ādikammikena kulaputtena pubbe vuttanayeneva sīlaparisodhanādīni sabbakiccāni katvā vuttappakārassa ācariyassa santike pañcasandhikaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ uggahetabbaṃ.

Tatrime pañca sandhayo uggaho, paripucchā, upaṭṭhānaṃ, appanā, lakkhaṇanti. Tattha uggaho nāma kammaṭṭhānassa uggaṇhanaṃ. Paripucchā nāma kammaṭṭhānassa paripucchanā. Upaṭṭhānaṃ nāma kammaṭṭhānassa upaṭṭhānaṃ. Appanā nāma kammaṭṭhānassa appanā. Lakkhaṇaṃ nāma kammaṭṭhānassa lakkhaṇaṃ. "Evaṃlakkhaṇamidaṃ kammaṭṭhāna"nti kammaṭṭhānasabhāvūpadhāraṇanti vuttaṃ hoti.

Evaṃ pañcasandhikaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ uggaṇhanto attanāpi na kilamati, ācariyampi na viheseti. Tasmā thokaṃ uddisāpetvā bahukālaṃ sajjhāyitvā evaṃ pañcasandhikaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ uggahetvā ācariyassa santike vā aññatra vā pubbe vuttappakāre senāsane vasantena upacchinnakhuddakapalibodhena katabhattakiccena bhattasammadaṃ paṭivinodetvā sukhanisinnena ratanattayaguṇānussaraṇena cittaṃ sampahaṃsetvā ācariyuggahato ekapadampi asammuyhantena idaṃ ānāpānassatikammaṭṭhānaṃ manasi kātabbaṃ. Tatrāyaṃ manasikāravidhi —

Gaṇanā anubandhanā, phusanā ṭhapanā sallakkhaṇā;
Vivaṭṭanā pārisuddhi, tesañca paṭipassanāti.

Tattha gaṇanāti gaṇanāyeva. Anubandhanāti anuvahanā. Phusanāti phuṭṭhaṭṭhānaṃ. Ṭhapanāti appanā. Sallakkhaṇāti vipassanā. Vivaṭṭanāti maggo. Pārisuddhīti phalaṃ. Tesañca paṭipassanāti paccavekkhaṇā.

Tattha iminā ādikammikena kulaputtena paṭhamaṃ gaṇanāya idaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasi kātabbaṃ. Gaṇentena ca pañcannaṃ heṭṭhā na ṭhapetabbaṃ. Dasannaṃ upari na netabbaṃ. Antarā khaṇḍaṃ na dassetabbaṃ. Pañcannaṃ heṭṭhā ṭhapentassa hi sambādhe okāse cittuppādo vipphandati sambādhe vaje sanniruddhagogaṇo viya. Dasannampi upari nentassa gaṇananissitako cittuppādo hoti. Antarā khaṇḍaṃ dassentassa "sikhāppattaṃ nu kho me kammaṭṭhānaṃ, no"ti cittaṃ vikampati. Tasmā ete dose vajjetvā gaṇetabbaṃ.

Gaṇentena ca paṭhamaṃ dandhagaṇanāya dhaññamāpakagaṇanāya gaṇetabbaṃ. Dhaññamāpako hi nāḷiṃ pūretvā "eka"nti vatvā okirati. Puna pūrento kiñci kacavaraṃ disvā taṃ chaḍḍento "ekaṃ eka"nti vadati. Esa nayo dve dvetiādīsu. Evameva imināpi assāsapassāsesu yo upaṭṭhāti, taṃ gahetvā "ekaṃ eka"nti ādiṃ katvā yāva "dasa dasā"ti pavattamānaṃ pavattamānaṃ upalakkhetvāva gaṇetabbaṃ. Tassa evaṃ gaṇayato nikkhamantā ca pavisantā ca assāsapassāsā pākaṭā honti.

Athānena taṃ dandhagaṇanaṃ dhaññamāpakagaṇanaṃ pahāya sīghagaṇanāya gopālakagaṇanāya gaṇetabbaṃ.
