Cheko hi gopālako sakkharādayo ucchaṅgena gahetvā rajjudaṇḍahattho pātova vajaṃ gantvā gāvo piṭṭhiyaṃ paharitvā palighatthambhamatthake nisinno dvārappattaṃ dvārappattaṃyeva gāviṃ ekā dveti sakkharaṃ khipitvā gaṇeti. Tiyāmarattiṃ sambādhe okāse dukkhaṃ vutthagogaṇo nikkhamanto nikkhamanto aññamaññaṃ upanighaṃsanto vegena vegena puñjapuñjo hutvā nikkhamati. So vegena vegena "tīṇi cattāri pañca dasā"ti gaṇetiyeva, evamimassāpi purimanayena gaṇayato assāsapassāsā pākaṭā hutvā sīghaṃ sīghaṃ punappunaṃ sañcaranti. Tatonena "punappunaṃ sañcarantī"ti ñatvā anto ca bahi ca agahetvā dvārappattaṃ dvārappattaṃyeva gahetvā "eko dve tīṇi cattāri pañca cha. Eko dve tīṇi cattāri pañca cha satta…pe… aṭṭha, nava, dasā"ti sīghaṃ sīyaṃ gaṇetabbameva. Gaṇanapaṭibaddhe hi kammaṭṭhāne gaṇanabaleneva cittaṃ ekaggaṃ hoti, arittupatthambhanavasena caṇḍasote nāvāṭṭhapanamiva.

Tassevaṃ sīghaṃ sīghaṃ gaṇayato kammaṭṭhānaṃ nirantaraṃ pavattaṃ viya hutvā upaṭṭhāti. Atha nirantaraṃ pavattatīti ñatvā anto ca bahi ca vātaṃ apariggahetvā purimanayeneva vegena vegena gaṇetabbaṃ. Anto pavisanavātena hi saddhiṃ cittaṃ pavesayato abbhantaraṃ vātabbhāhataṃ medapūritaṃ viya hoti. Bahi nikkhamanavātena saddhiṃ cittaṃ nīharato bahiddhā puthuttārammaṇe cittaṃ vikkhipati. Phuṭṭhaphuṭṭhokāse pana satiṃ ṭhapetvā bhāventasseva bhāvanā sampajjati. Tena vuttaṃ "anto ca bahi ca vātaṃ apariggahetvā purimanayeneva vegena vegena gaṇetabba"nti.

Kīvaciraṃ panetaṃ gaṇetabbanti? Yāva vinā gaṇanāya assāsapassāsārammaṇe sati santiṭṭhati. Bahivisaṭavitakkavicchedaṃ katvā assāsapassāsārammaṇe satisaṇṭhāpanatthaṃyeva hi gaṇanāti.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, since it is only this first tetrad that is stated here as the meditation subject for a beginner, while the remaining three tetrads are stated by way of the contemplation of feeling, of mind, and of mental states, for one who has already attained that jhāna — therefore, a clansman just beginning, desiring, having developed this meditation subject, to attain arahantship through insight that has the fourth jhāna of breathing as its foundation, together with the discriminations, should, having performed all the preliminary duties such as the purification of virtue in the manner already stated, take up this meditation subject with its five bonds in the presence of a teacher of the kind already described.

Herein, these are the five bonds: learning, questioning, establishing, absorption, and characteristic. Herein, "learning" means the learning of the meditation subject. "Questioning" means the questioning about the meditation subject. "Establishing" means the establishing of the meditation subject. "Absorption" means the absorption of the meditation subject. "Characteristic" means the characteristic of the meditation subject — meaning, the ascertainment of the meditation subject's own nature, thinking, "such is the characteristic of this meditation subject."

One learning the meditation subject with these five bonds neither wearies himself nor troubles his teacher. Therefore, having had it explained a little at a time, and having recited it over a long period, having thus taken up the meditation subject with its five bonds, and dwelling, whether in the presence of the teacher or elsewhere, in a dwelling of the kind already described, with minor impediments cut off, his meal-duty performed, drowsiness from eating dispelled, sitting comfortably, having gladdened his mind by recollecting the qualities of the Triple Gem, and without confusion about even a single word learned from the teacher, this meditation subject of mindfulness of breathing should be attended to. Herein, this is the method of attention —

Counting, following, touching, fixing, observing, / turning away, purification, and reviewing of these.

Herein, "counting" is simply counting. "Following" is continuous accompaniment. "Touching" is the point of contact. "Fixing" is absorption. "Observing" is insight. "Turning away" is the path. "Purification" is the fruit. "Reviewing of these" is reflective review.

Herein, this beginning clansman should first attend to this meditation subject by way of counting. And, in counting, he should not stop below five, nor carry it beyond ten, nor show a break in the middle. For one who stops below five, the mental arising flutters about within a cramped space, like a herd of cattle penned in a narrow enclosure. For one who carries it beyond ten, the mental arising becomes dependent on the counting itself. For one who shows a break in the middle, the mind wavers, thinking, "has my meditation subject reached its peak, or not?" Therefore, avoiding these faults, one should count.

And, in counting, one should first count by the slow counting of a grain-measurer. For a grain-measurer, having filled the measure, says "one," and pours it out; then, filling it again, on seeing some piece of chaff, discards it, saying, "one, one." The same method applies to "two, two," and so on. Just so, this meditator too, having taken hold of whatever in-breath or out-breath becomes manifest, should count, beginning with "one, one," and noting each occurrence in turn, up to "ten, ten." For one counting in this way, the in-breaths and out-breaths, as they emerge and enter, become manifest.

He should then give up that slow, grain-measurer's counting, and count by the swift counting of a cowherd. For a skilled cowherd, taking up pebbles and the like in his lap, with a rope and stick in hand, goes to the pen at the very break of day, strikes the cows on the back, and, sitting on the top of a bar-post, counts, throwing down a pebble for each cow as it reaches the gate, saying "one, two." For, having spent an uncomfortable night in a cramped space, the herd of cattle, as it emerges, jostling against one another, emerges swiftly, in dense clusters — so he counts swiftly, "three, four, five, ten." Just so, this meditator too, counting by the earlier method, finds that the in-breaths and out-breaths, having become manifest, now move swiftly, again and again. Knowing then that they "move again and again," he should, without grasping at what is within or without, but only at what has reached the point of contact, count swiftly indeed: "one, two, three, four, five, six; one, two, three, four, five, six, seven ... eight, nine, ten." For, in a meditation subject bound up with counting, the mind becomes one-pointed by the very strength of the counting, like a boat held steady in a swift current by the support of its rudder.

For one counting thus, swiftly and swiftly, the meditation subject comes to appear as occurring without interruption. Knowing then that it occurs without interruption, one should, without grasping the wind within or without, count swiftly and swiftly by the earlier method still. For one who directs the mind, together with the wind entering within, becomes as though the interior were struck by wind and filled with fat; and one who leads the mind out together with the wind emerging without scatters the mind among a variety of external objects. But for one developing it while fixing mindfulness precisely at the point of contact, the development succeeds. For this it was said: "without grasping the wind within or without, one should count swiftly and swiftly by the earlier method still."

For how long should this counting be done? Until, without counting, mindfulness stands firm with the in-breath and out-breath as its object. For counting exists only for the purpose of cutting off thought scattered outward and establishing mindfulness on the object of the in-breath and out-breath.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The two counting-similes, like the monkey-and-almsman pair used earlier for the thirty-two parts, are chosen to model two genuinely different phases of the identical underlying skill: the grain-measurer's slow, deliberate, one-at-a-time counting is suited to a beginner still struggling to notice individual breaths at all, while the cowherd's swift, almost automatic counting of a jostling herd emerging all at once models what happens once the breaths, having become reliably manifest, arrive faster than can be tracked one by one — the counting method itself must accelerate to match the meditator's own growing familiarity with the object.

The boat-and-rudder simile for counting's function is worth comparing to the earlier chapter's identical image for applied thought in the foulness meditations: in both cases, an outside support is needed to keep the mind fixed on an object it would otherwise drift away from in turbulent conditions. But counting, unlike applied thought in the foulness meditations, is explicitly temporary scaffolding — the chapter is direct that counting should eventually be dropped entirely, once mindfulness can hold the breath as its object "without counting," a threshold the rest of the chapter's eight-step sequence moves steadily toward.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
pañca sandhi

the five bonds — the required stages (learning, questioning, establishing, absorption, characteristic) for properly taking up a meditation subject from a teacher, specific to this chapter's treatment of mindfulness of breathing.

gaṇanā anubandhanā phusanā ṭhapanā sallakkhaṇā vivaṭṭanā pārisuddhi paṭipassanā

counting, following, touching, fixing, observing, turning away, purification, and reviewing — the eight-term mnemonic verse structuring the entire remainder of the chapter's treatment of this meditation subject's practical development.

dhaññamāpakagaṇanā / gopālakagaṇanā

the grain-measurer's counting / the cowherd's counting — the two successive counting techniques, moving from slow and deliberate to swift and continuous, matching the meditator's growing ease with tracking the breath.

Following, Touching, and Fixing: Three Great Similes

Once counting has served its purpose, the chapter turns to what replaces it: not tracking the breath's beginning, middle, and end as it travels from navel to nose or nose to navel, but resting mindfulness lightly at the single point where the breath touches — the nostril-rim or upper lip — illustrated by three of this meditation subject's most celebrated images: a lame man who pushes a swing and simply watches it from where he sits, a gatekeeper who questions only those actually arriving at the gate, and a woodcutter's saw whose steady rhythm arises from mindfulness of where the teeth make contact, not from any expectation about which tooth is coming or going.

Pali Source

Evaṃ gaṇanāya manasi katvā anubandhanāya manasi kātabbaṃ. Anubandhanā nāma gaṇanaṃ paṭisaṃharitvā satiyā nirantaraṃ assāsapassāsānaṃ anugamanaṃ. Tañca kho na ādimajjhapariyosānānugamanavasena. Bahinikkhamanavātassa hi nābhi ādi, hadayaṃ majjhaṃ, nāsikaggaṃ pariyosānaṃ. Abbhantaraṃ pavisanavātassa nāsikaggaṃ ādi, hadayaṃ majjhaṃ nābhi pariyosānaṃ. Tañcassa anugacchato vikkhepagataṃ cittaṃ sāraddhāya ceva hoti iñjanāya ca. Yathāha —

"Assāsādimajjhapariyosānaṃ satiyā anugacchato ajjhattaṃ vikkhepagatena cittena kāyopi cittampi sāraddhā ca honti iñjitā ca phanditā ca. Passāsādimajjhapariyosānaṃ satiyā anugacchato bahiddhā vikkhepagatena cittena kāyopi cittampi sāraddhā ca honti iñjitā ca phanditā cā"ti. (paṭi. ma. 1.157)

Tasmā anubandhanāya manasikarontena ādimajjhapariyosānavasena na manasi kātabbaṃ. Apica kho phusanāvasena ca ṭhapanāvasena ca manasi kātabbaṃ. Gaṇanānubandhanāvasena viya hi phusanāṭhapanāvasena visuṃ manasikāro natthi. Phuṭṭhaphuṭṭhaṭṭhāneyeva pana gaṇento gaṇanāya ca phusanāya ca manasi karoti. Tattheva gaṇanaṃ paṭisaṃharitvā te satiyā anubandhanto, appanāvasena ca cittaṃ ṭhapento anubandhanāya ca phusanāya ca ṭhapanāya ca manasi karotīti vuccati. Svāyamattho aṭṭhakathāsu vuttapaṅguḷadovārikūpamāhi paṭisambhidāyaṃ vuttakakacūpamāya ca veditabbo.

Tatrāyaṃ paṅguḷopamā — seyyathāpi paṅguḷo dolāya kīḷataṃ mātāputtānaṃ dolaṃ khipitvā tattheva dolāthambhamūle nisinno kamena āgacchantassa ca gacchantassa ca dolāphalakassa ubho koṭiyo majjhañca passati, na ca ubhokoṭimajjhānaṃ dassanatthaṃ byāvaṭo hoti, evamevāyaṃ bhikkhu sativasena upanibandhanathambhamūle ṭhatvā assāsapassāsadolaṃ khipitvā tattheva nimitte satiyā nisīdanto kamena āgacchantānañca gacchantānañca phuṭṭhaṭṭhāne assāsapassāsānaṃ ādimajjhapariyosānaṃ satiyā anugacchanto tattha ca cittaṃ ṭhapento passati, na ca tesaṃ dassanatthaṃ byāvaṭo hoti, ayaṃ paṅguḷopamā.

Ayaṃ pana dovārikūpamā — seyyathāpi dovāriko nagarassa anto ca bahi ca purise "ko tvaṃ, kuto vā āgato, kuhiṃ vā gacchasi, kiṃ vā te hatthe"ti na vīmaṃsati. Na hi tassa te bhārā, dvārappattaṃ dvārappattaṃyeva pana vīmaṃsati, evameva imassa bhikkhuno antopaviṭṭhavātā ca bahinikkhantavātā ca na bhārā honti, dvārappattā dvārappattāyeva bhārāti ayaṃ dovārikūpamā.

Kakacūpamā pana ādito paṭṭhāya evaṃ veditabbā. Vuttañhetaṃ —

"Nimittaṃ assāsapassāsā, anārammaṇamekacittassa;
Ajānato ca tayo dhamme, bhāvanā nupalabbhati.
"Nimittaṃ assāsapassāsā, anārammaṇamekacittassa;
Jānato ca tayo dhamme, bhāvanā upalabbhatī"ti. (paṭi. ma. 1.159)

"Kathaṃ ime tayo dhammā ekacittassa ārammaṇā na honti, na cime tayo dhammā aviditā honti, na ca cittaṃ vikkhepaṃ gacchati, padhānañca paññāyati, payogañca sādheti, visesamadhi gacchati? Seyyathāpi rukkho same bhūmibhāge nikkhitto, tamenaṃ puriso kakacena chindeyya. Rukkhe phuṭṭhakakacadantānaṃ vasena purisassa sati upaṭṭhitā hoti, na āgate vā gate vā kakacadante manasi karoti, na āgatā vā gatā vā kakacadantā aviditā honti, padhānañca paññāyati, payogañca sādheti, visesamadhigacchati.

"Yathā rukkho same bhūmibhāge nikkhitto, evaṃ upanibandhanānimittaṃ. Yathā kakacadantā, evaṃ assāsapassāsā. Yathā rukkhe phuṭṭhakakacadantānaṃ vasena purisassa sati upaṭṭhitā hoti, na āgate vā gate vā kakacadante manasi karoti, na āgatā vā gatā vā kakacadantā aviditā honti, padhānañca paññāyati, payogañca sādheti, visesamadhigacchati, evameva bhikkhu nāsikagge vā mukhanimitte vā satiṃ upaṭṭhapetvā nisinno hoti, na āgate vā gate vā assāsapassāse manasi karoti, na ca āgatā vā gatā vā assāsapassāsā aviditā honti, padhānañca paññāyati, payogañca sādheti, visesamadhigacchati.

"Padhānanti katamaṃ padhānaṃ? Āraddhavīriyassa kāyopi cittampi kammaniyaṃ hoti, idaṃ padhānaṃ. Katamo payogo? Āraddhavīriyassa upakkilesā pahīyanti, vitakkā vūpasamanti, ayaṃ payogo. Katamo viseso? Āraddhavīriyassa saṃyojanā pahīyanti, anusayā byantī honti, ayaṃ viseso. Evaṃ ime tayo dhammā ekacittassa ārammaṇā na honti, na cime tayo dhammā aviditā honti, na ca cittaṃ vikkhepaṃ gacchati, padhānañca paññāyati, payogañca sādheti, visesamadhigacchati. (paṭi. ma. 1.159)

"Ānāpānassati yassa, paripuṇṇā subhāvitā;
Anupubbaṃ paricitā, yathā buddhena desitā;
So imaṃ lokaṃ pabhāseti, abbhā muttova candimā"ti. (paṭi. ma. 1.160)

Ayaṃ kakacūpamā. Idha panassa āgatāgatavasena amanasikāramattameva payojananti veditabbaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

Having thus attended by way of counting, attention should be given by way of following. "Following" means the continuous accompaniment, by mindfulness, of the in-breaths and out-breaths, having given up counting. But this is not by way of following their beginning, middle, and end. For, in the case of the outward-going wind, the navel is the beginning, the heart the middle, and the tip of the nose the end; and, in the case of the inward-going wind, the tip of the nose is the beginning, the heart the middle, and the navel the end. And, for one following it in this way, the mind, having gone to distraction, becomes both agitated and shaken. As it is said —

"For one following, by mindfulness, the beginning, middle, and end of the in-breath, with a mind that has gone to internal distraction, both body and mind become agitated, shaken, and trembling. For one following, by mindfulness, the beginning, middle, and end of the out-breath, with a mind that has gone to external distraction, both body and mind become agitated, shaken, and trembling." (Paṭis. 1.157)

Therefore, one attending by way of following should not attend by way of beginning, middle, and end. Rather, attention should be given by way of touching and by way of fixing. For there is no separate act of attention by way of touching and fixing, as there is by way of counting and following; rather, in the very act of counting at the point of contact again and again, one attends both by counting and by touching. It is precisely there, having given up counting, following them by mindfulness, and fixing the mind by way of absorption, that one is said to attend by way of following, touching, and fixing. This meaning should be understood by the similes of the lame man and the gatekeeper, as stated in the commentaries, and by the simile of the saw, as stated in the Paṭisambhidāmagga.

Herein, this is the simile of the lame man: just as a lame man, having set a swing in motion for a mother and child at play, sits right there at the foot of the swing-post and sees both ends and the middle of the swing-board as it comes and goes in turn, without being occupied with watching both ends and the middle — just so, this monk, standing, as it were, at the foot of the post of binding by way of mindfulness, having set the swing of the in-breath and out-breath in motion, sitting with mindfulness right there at the sign, following, by mindfulness, the beginning, middle, and end of the in-breaths and out-breaths as they come and go in turn at the point of contact, and fixing the mind there, sees them without being occupied with watching them — this is the simile of the lame man.

This, now, is the simile of the gatekeeper: just as a gatekeeper does not investigate the men inside and outside the city, asking, "who are you, where have you come from, where are you going, what is in your hands" — for these are not his concern — but investigates only those actually arriving at the gate — just so, for this monk, the wind that has entered within and the wind that has emerged without are not his concern; only what has arrived at the gate is his concern. This is the simile of the gatekeeper.

The simile of the saw, however, should be understood from the very beginning, as follows. For it is said —

"The sign, the in-breath, and the out-breath are not the object of a single consciousness; and, for one who does not know these three states, development is not obtained.
"The sign, the in-breath, and the out-breath are not the object of a single consciousness; and, for one who does know these three states, development is obtained." (Paṭis. 1.159)

"How is it that these three states are not the object of a single consciousness, yet these three states are not unknown, and the mind does not become distracted, and the endeavor becomes evident, and the undertaking is accomplished, and distinction is reached? Just as a log placed on level ground — a man might cut it with a saw. The man's mindfulness is established by way of the saw's teeth as they touch the log; he does not attend to the teeth of the saw as they come or go, yet the teeth of the saw, as they come and go, are not unknown to him, and the endeavor becomes evident, and the undertaking is accomplished, and distinction is reached.

"Just as the log placed on level ground is the sign of binding, so are the in-breath and out-breath like the teeth of the saw. Just as the man's mindfulness is established by way of the saw's teeth touching the log, and he does not attend to the teeth as they come or go, yet they are not unknown to him, and the endeavor becomes evident, and the undertaking is accomplished, and distinction is reached — just so, a monk, having established mindfulness at the tip of the nose or at the sign in the mouth, sits without attending to the in-breaths and out-breaths as they come or go, yet they are not unknown to him, and the endeavor becomes evident, and the undertaking is accomplished, and distinction is reached.

"What is the 'endeavor'? For one of aroused energy, both body and mind become workable — this is the endeavor. What is the 'undertaking'? For one of aroused energy, defilements are abandoned, thoughts are stilled — this is the undertaking. What is the 'distinction'? For one of aroused energy, the fetters are abandoned, the underlying tendencies come to an end — this is the distinction. Thus these three states are not the object of a single consciousness, yet these three states are not unknown, and the mind does not become distracted, and the endeavor becomes evident, and the undertaking is accomplished, and distinction is reached." (Paṭis. 1.159)

"He whose mindfulness of breathing / is complete and well developed, / gradually cultivated, / just as taught by the Buddha —
He illuminates this world, / like the moon freed from a cloud." (Paṭis. 1.160)

This is the simile of the saw. Here, the point intended is simply non-attention by way of the breath's coming and going.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The three similes, though converging on an identical practical instruction, each isolate a different specific risk of the wrong approach. The lame man's simile targets the danger of the mind actively chasing the breath's full path back and forth (agitation from over-involvement); the gatekeeper's simile targets the danger of caring about the breath's internal or external destination rather than only its point of arrival (misplaced attention to irrelevant detail); and the saw simile, the most philosophically precise of the three, addresses the subtlest point of all — that the meditation succeeds even though the sign, the in-breath, and the out-breath, taken together, are never literally one single object of a single moment of consciousness, since attention rests only on the touch-point while the actual breath-states are merely "not unknown," known indirectly rather than directly tracked.

The saw simile's threefold outcome — endeavor, undertaking, and distinction, each defined by a specific effect of aroused energy — quietly maps this single meditation subject's mechanics directly onto the path structure the whole treatise has been building toward: a workable body and mind, then abandoned defilements and stilled thought, then abandoned fetters and ended underlying tendencies, moving in one continuous sequence from ordinary meditative readiness to full liberation, all while the meditator's attention itself never leaves the simple point where breath touches skin.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
phuṭṭhaṭṭhāna

the point of contact — phuṭṭha (touched) + ṭhāna (place); the single, stationary location (nostril-rim or upper lip) where the breath's touch is felt, replacing the earlier practice of following the breath's full internal path.

padhāna / payoga / visesa

endeavor / undertaking / distinction — the three specific effects of aroused energy named in the saw-simile passage, tracing a path from basic workability of body and mind through to the ending of underlying tendencies.

upanibandhanānimitta

the sign of binding — upanibandhana (tying, binding) + nimitta (sign); the fixed reference point (compared to the swing-post, or the log beneath the saw) that anchors mindfulness while the breath itself moves past it.

The Sign Arises, and How to Recognize It Rightly

The chapter now explains why the meditator should never seek the sign in some place other than its natural point of contact — offering the image of a farmer who marks the entrance to his cattle-pen rather than following his oxen deep into the forest, catching them only when they return to the spot he knows they must pass. When the sign does finally appear, it takes on wildly different forms for different meditators — a star, a string of pearls, a rough cotton-seed, a coil of rope, a wisp of smoke, a spider's web, a cloud, a lotus, or the disc of the moon or sun itself — and the chapter closes by carefully distinguishing two schools of thought on how a teacher should respond when a student reports the sign's arrival.

Pali Source

Idaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ manasikaroto kassaci na cireneva nimittañca uppajjati, avasesajhānaṅgapaṭimaṇḍitā appanāsaṅkhātā ṭhapanā ca sampajjati. Kassaci pana gaṇanāvaseneva manasikārakālato pabhuti anukkamato oḷārikaassāsapassāsanirodhavasena kāyadarathe vūpasante kāyopi cittampi lahukaṃ hoti, sarīraṃ ākāse laṅghanākārappattaṃ viya hoti. Yathā sāraddhakāyassa mañce vā pīṭhe vā nisīdato mañcapīṭhaṃ onamati, vikūjati, paccattharaṇaṃ valiṃ gaṇhāti. Asāraddhakāyassa pana nisīdato neva mañcapīṭhaṃ onamati, na vikūjati, na paccattharaṇaṃ valiṃ gaṇhāti, tūlapicupūritaṃ viya mañcapīṭhaṃ hoti. Kasmā? Yasmā asāraddho kāyo lahuko hoti. Evameva gaṇanāvasena manasikārakālato pabhuti anukkamato oḷārikaassāsapassāsanirodhavasena kāyadarathe vūpasante kāyopi cittampi lahukaṃ hoti, sarīraṃ ākāse laṅghanākārappattaṃ viya hoti.

Tassa oḷārike assāsapassāse niruddhe sukhumassāsapassāsanimittārammaṇaṃ cittaṃ pavattati. Tasmimpi niruddhe aparāparaṃ tato sukhumataraṃ sukhumataraṃ nimittārammaṇaṃ pavattatiyeva. Kathaṃ? Yathā puriso mahatiyā lohasalākāya kaṃsathālaṃ ākoṭeyya, ekappahārena mahāsaddo uppajjeyya, tassa oḷārikasaddārammaṇaṃ cittaṃ pavatteyya. Niruddhe oḷārike sadde atha pacchā sukhumasaddanimittārammaṇaṃ, tasmimpi niruddhe aparāparaṃ tato sukhumataraṃ sukhumataraṃ saddanimittārammaṇaṃ pavattateva, evanti veditabbaṃ. Vuttampicetaṃ — "seyyathāpi kaṃse ākoṭite"ti (paṭi. ma. 1.171) vitthāro.

Yathā hi aññāni kammaṭṭhānāni uparūpari vibhūtāni honti, na tathā idaṃ. Idaṃ pana uparūpari bhāventassa sukhumattaṃ gacchati, upaṭṭhānampi na upagacchati, evaṃ anupaṭṭhahante pana tasmiṃ tena bhikkhunā uṭṭhāyāsanā cammakhaṇḍaṃ papphoṭetvā na gantabbaṃ. Kiṃ kātabbaṃ? "Ācariyaṃ pucchissāmī"ti vā, "naṭṭhaṃ dāni me kammaṭṭhāna"nti vā na vuṭṭhātabbaṃ. Iriyāpathaṃ vikopetvā gacchato hi kammaṭṭhānaṃ navanavameva hoti. Tasmā yathānisinneneva desato āharitabbaṃ.

Tatrāyaṃ āharaṇūpāyo, tena hi bhikkhunā kammaṭṭhānassa anupaṭṭhānabhāvaṃ ñatvā iti paṭisañcikkhitabbaṃ, ime assāsapassāsā nāma kattha atthi, kattha natthi. Kassa vā atthi, kassa vā natthīti. Athevaṃ paṭisañcikkhatā ime antomātukucchiyaṃ natthi, udake nimuggānaṃ natthi, tathā asaññībhūtānaṃ, matānaṃ, catutthajjhānasamāpannānaṃ, rūpārūpabhavasamaṅgīnaṃ, nirodhasamāpannānanti ñatvā evaṃ attanāva attā paṭicodetabbo "nanu tvaṃ, paṇḍita, neva mātukucchigato, na udake nimuggo, na asaññībhūto, na mato, na catutthajjhānasamāpanno, na rūpārūpabhavasamaṅgī, na nirodhasamāpanno. Atthiyeva te assāsapassāsā, mandapaññatāya pana pariggahetuṃ na sakkosī"ti. Athānena pakatiphuṭṭhavasena cittaṃ ṭhapetvā manasikāro pavattetabbo. Ime hi dīghanāsikassa nāsāpuṭaṃ ghaṭṭentā pavattanti. Rassanāsikassa uttaroṭṭhaṃ. Tasmānena imaṃ nāma ṭhānaṃ ghaṭṭentīti nimittaṃ ṭhapetabbaṃ. Imameva hi atthavasaṃ paṭicca vuttaṃ bhagavatā — "nāhaṃ, bhikkhave, muṭṭhassatissa asampajānassa ānāpānassatibhāvanaṃ vadāmī"ti (ma. ni. 3.149; saṃ. ni. 5.992).

Kiñcāpi hi yaṃkiñci kammaṭṭhānaṃ satassa sampajānasseva sampajjati. Ito aññaṃ pana manasikarontassa pākaṭaṃ hoti. Idaṃ pana ānāpānassatikammaṭṭhānaṃ garukaṃ garukabhāvanaṃ buddhapaccekabuddhabuddhaputtānaṃ mahāpurisānaṃyeva manasikārabhūmibhūtaṃ, na ceva ittaraṃ, na ca ittarasattasamāsevitaṃ. Yathā yathā manasi karīyati, tathā tathā santañceva hoti sukhumañca. Tasmā ettha balavatī sati ca paññā ca icchitabbā.

Yathā hi maṭṭhasāṭakassa tunnakaraṇakāle sūcipi sukhumā icchitabbā. Sūcipāsavedhanampi tato sukhumataraṃ, evameva maṭṭhasāṭakasadisassa imassa kammaṭṭhānassa bhāvanākāle sūcipaṭibhāgā satipi, sūcipāsavedhanapaṭibhāgā taṃsampayuttā paññāpi balavatī icchitabbā. Tāhi ca pana satipaññāhi samannāgatena bhikkhunā na te assāsapassāsā aññatra pakatiphuṭṭhokāsā pariyesitabbā.

Yathā pana kassako kasiṃ kasitvā balībadde muñcitvā gocaramukhe katvā chāyāya nisinno vissameyya, athassa te balībaddā vegena aṭaviṃ paviseyyuṃ. Yo hoti cheko kassako, so puna te gahetvā yojetukāmo na tesaṃ anupadaṃ gantvā aṭaviṃ āhiṇḍahi, atha kho rasmiñca patodañca gahetvā ujukameva tesaṃ nipātanatitthaṃ gantvā nisīdati vā nipajjati vā, atha te goṇe divasabhāgaṃ caritvā nipātanatitthaṃ otaritvā nhatvā ca pivitvā ca paccuttaritvā ṭhite disvā rasmiyā bandhitvā patodena vijjhanto ānetvā yojetvā puna kammaṃ karoti, evameva tena bhikkhunā na te assāsapassāsā aññatra pakatiphuṭṭhokāsā pariyesitabbā. Satirasmiṃ pana paññāpatodañca gahetvā pakatiphuṭṭhokāse cittaṃ ṭhapetvā manasikāro pavattetabbo. Evañhissa manasikaroto na cirasseva te upaṭṭhahanti nipātanatitthe viya goṇā. Tatonena satirasmiyā bandhitvā tasmiṃyeva ṭhāne yojetvā paññāpatodena vijjhantena punappunaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuñjitabbaṃ.

Tassevamanuyuñjato na cirasseva nimittaṃ upaṭṭhāti. Taṃ panetaṃ na sabbesaṃ ekasadisaṃ hoti. Apica kho kassaci sukhasamphassaṃ uppādayamāno tūlapicu viya kappāsapicu viya vātadhārā viya ca upaṭṭhātīti ekacce āhu.

Ayaṃ pana aṭṭhakathāsu vinicchayo, idañhi kassaci tārakarūpaṃ viya maṇiguḷikā viya muttāguḷikā viya ca, kassaci kharasamphassaṃ hutvā kappāsaṭṭhi viya dārusārasūci viya ca, kassaci dīghapāmaṅgasuttaṃ viya kusumadāmaṃ viya dhūmasikhā viya ca, kassaci vitthataṃ makkaṭakasuttaṃ viya valāhakapaṭalaṃ viya padumapupphaṃ viya rathacakkaṃ viya candamaṇḍalaṃ viya sūriyamaṇḍalaṃ viya ca upaṭṭhāti. Tañca panetaṃ yathā sambahulesu bhikkhūsu suttantaṃ sajjhāyitvā nisinnesu ekena bhikkhunā "tumhākaṃ kīdisaṃ hutvā idaṃ suttaṃ upaṭṭhātī"ti vutte eko "mayhaṃ mahatī pabbateyyā nadī viya hutvā upaṭṭhātī"ti āha. Aparo "mayhaṃ ekā vanarāji viya". Añño "mayhaṃ eko sītacchāyo sākhāsampanno phalabhārabharitarukkho viyā"ti. Tesaṃ hi taṃ ekameva suttaṃ saññānānatāya nānato upaṭṭhāti. Evaṃ ekameva kammaṭṭhānaṃ saññānānatāya nānato upaṭṭhāti. Saññajañhi etaṃ saññānidānaṃ saññāpabhavaṃ. Tasmā saññānānatāya nānato upaṭṭhātīti veditabbaṃ.

Ettha ca aññameva assāsārammaṇaṃ cittaṃ, aññaṃ passāsārammaṇaṃ, aññaṃ nimittārammaṇaṃ. Yassa hi ime tayo dhammā natthi, tassa kammaṭṭhānaṃ neva appanaṃ, na upacāraṃ pāpuṇāti. Yassa pana ime tayo dhammā atthi, tasseva kammaṭṭhānaṃ upacārañca appanañca pāpuṇāti. Vuttañhetaṃ —

"Nimittaṃ assāsapassāsā, anārammaṇamekacittassa;
Ajānato tayo dhamme, bhāvanā nupalabbhati.
"Nimittaṃ assāsapassāsā, anārammaṇamekacittassa;
Jānatova tayo dhamme, bhāvanā upalabbhatī"ti. (pārā. aṭṭha. 2.165)

Evaṃ upaṭṭhite pana nimitte tena bhikkhunā ācariyassa santikaṃ gantvā ārocetabbaṃ "mayhaṃ, bhante, evarūpaṃ nāma upaṭṭhātī"ti. Ācariyena pana etaṃ nimittanti vā na vā nimittanti na vattabbaṃ. "Evaṃ hoti, āvuso"ti vatvā punappunaṃ manasi karohīti vattabbo. Nimittanti hi vutte vosānaṃ āpajjeyya. Na nimittanti vutte nirāso visīdeyya. Tasmā tadubhayampi avatvā manasikāreyeva niyojetabboti. Evaṃ tāva dīghabhāṇakā.

Majjhimabhāṇakā panāhu "nimittamidaṃ, āvuso, kammaṭṭhānaṃ punappunaṃ manasi karohi sappurisāti vattabbo"ti. Athānena nimitteyeva cittaṃ ṭhapetabbaṃ. Evamassāyaṃ ito pabhuti ṭhapanāvasena bhāvanā hoti. Vuttañhetaṃ porāṇehi —

"Nimitte ṭhapayaṃ cittaṃ, nānākāraṃ vibhāvayaṃ;
Dhīro assāsapassāse, sakaṃ cittaṃ nibandhatī"ti. (pārā. aṭṭha. 2.165)

Tassevaṃ nimittupaṭṭhānato pabhuti nīvaraṇāni vikkhambhitāneva honti, kilesā sannisinnāva. Sati upaṭṭhitāyeva. Cittaṃ upacārasamādhinā samāhitameva. Athānena taṃ nimittaṃ neva vaṇṇato manasi kātabbaṃ, na lakkhaṇato paccavekkhitabbaṃ. Apica kho khattiyamahesiyā cakkavattigabbho viya kassakena sāliyavagabbho viya ca āvāsādīni satta asappāyāni vajjetvā tāneva satta sappāyāni sevantena sādhukaṃ rakkhitabbaṃ. Atha naṃ evaṃ rakkhitvā punappunaṃ manasikāravasena vuddhiṃ virūḷhiṃ gamayitvā dasavidhaṃ appanākosallaṃ sampādetabbaṃ, vīriyasamatā yojetabbā. Tassevaṃ ghaṭentassa pathavīkasiṇe vuttānukkameneva tasmiṃ nimitte catukkapañcakajjhānāni nibbattanti.

Ru-Yi Translation

For one attending to this meditation subject, in some the sign arises very quickly, and the fixing reckoned as absorption, adorned with the remaining jhāna factors, is accomplished. But for another, from the very time of attending by way of counting, gross in-breaths and out-breaths cease in due order, and, the disturbance of the body being stilled, both body and mind become light, and the body seems as though it has reached a condition of leaping up into the air. Just as, for one of agitated body sitting on a bed or a bench, the bed or bench sags and creaks, and the covering wrinkles; but for one of unagitated body sitting there, the bed or bench neither sags nor creaks, nor does the covering wrinkle, and the bed or bench becomes as though filled with cotton-down — why? Because an unagitated body is light. Just so, from the time of attending by way of counting, gross in-breaths and out-breaths cease in due order, and, the disturbance of the body being stilled, both body and mind become light, and the body seems as though it has reached a condition of leaping up into the air.

For him, once the gross in-breaths and out-breaths have ceased, the mind proceeds with the sign of the subtle in-breath and out-breath as its object. Once that too has ceased, a sign-object finer and finer still continues to occur, again and again. How? Just as, if a man were to strike a bronze bowl with a great iron rod, a loud sound would arise from that single blow, and his mind would proceed with that gross sound as its object; and, once that gross sound has ceased, afterward the mind proceeds with the sign of a subtle sound as its object; and, once that too has ceased, a sign of sound finer and finer still continues to occur — just so should this be understood. This too was said in the passage beginning, "just as, when a bronze bowl is struck" (Paṭis. 1.171), given in full elsewhere.

For, unlike other meditation subjects, which become clearer and clearer the more they are developed, this one is not so. Rather, the more it is developed, the more subtle it becomes, to the point of not even appearing at all. And, when it fails to appear in this way, that monk should not rise from his seat, brush off his leather mat, and go elsewhere. What should be done? He should not get up thinking, "I will ask my teacher," or "my meditation subject is now lost." For, upsetting one's posture and going elsewhere, the meditation subject only becomes ever newer. Therefore, it should be recovered from that very spot, just as one is sitting.

Herein, this is the method of recovery: that monk, knowing that the meditation subject has failed to appear, should reflect thus: "where do these in-breaths and out-breaths exist, and where do they not exist? For whom do they exist, and for whom do they not?" Reflecting thus, and knowing that they do not exist for one still in the mother's womb, nor for those submerged in water, nor likewise for the unconscious, for the dead, for one who has attained the fourth jhāna, for one possessed of the immaterial states of existence, and for one who has attained cessation — he should reprove himself thus: "surely, wise one, you are neither in the womb, nor submerged in water, nor unconscious, nor dead, nor one who has attained the fourth jhāna, nor possessed of the immaterial states of existence, nor one who has attained cessation. Your in-breaths and out-breaths do indeed exist — it is only through weakness of understanding that you are unable to grasp them." He should then fix his mind by way of the natural point of contact and let attention proceed. For these breaths, in one with a long nose, strike against the nostril's rim as they occur; in one with a short nose, against the upper lip. Therefore, the sign should be fixed thus: "they strike against this particular spot." For it was with reference to this very meaning that the Blessed One said: "I do not speak, monks, of the development of mindfulness of breathing for one of confused mindfulness, lacking full awareness" (M.N. 3.149; S.N. 5.992).

For although it is true that any meditation subject succeeds only for one who is mindful and fully aware, this particular one becomes evident to one attending to it more than any other does. This meditation subject of mindfulness of breathing is weighty, its development a weighty matter, the very domain of attention proper to great beings — Buddhas, Paccekabuddhas, and the Buddha's own disciples — and not something trivial, nor something practiced by trivial beings. The more it is attended to, the more peaceful and subtle it becomes. Therefore, powerful mindfulness and understanding are required here.

For just as, in stitching a fine garment, a fine needle is required, and an even finer instrument still is needed to thread that needle — just so, in the development of this meditation subject, comparable to a fine garment, powerful mindfulness, comparable to the needle, is required, and understanding conjoined with it, comparable to the instrument for threading the needle, is required as well. And a monk possessed of such mindfulness and understanding should not seek out those in-breaths and out-breaths anywhere other than at their natural point of contact.

Just as a farmer, having plowed his field, might release his oxen, direct them toward pasture, and, sitting in the shade, rest — whereupon those oxen might swiftly enter a forest — a skilled farmer, wishing to catch and yoke them again, would not go tracking them step by step through the forest, but, taking a rope and a goad, would go straight to the ford where they must come down to drink, and sit or lie down there; and then, seeing those oxen, having grazed for the day's portion, come down to the ford, bathe, drink, and climb back up to stand there, he would bind them with the rope, drive them on with the goad, bring them back, yoke them, and resume his work — just so, that monk should not seek out those in-breaths and out-breaths anywhere other than at their natural point of contact. Rather, taking up the rope of mindfulness and the goad of understanding, he should fix his mind at the natural point of contact and let attention proceed. For one attending thus, they soon become manifest to him, like the oxen at the ford. He should then, having bound them with the rope of mindfulness, and yoked them right at that very spot, pursue the meditation subject again and again, driving it on with the goad of understanding.

For one pursuing it thus, the sign soon becomes manifest. But this is not the same for everyone. Some say that, for some, producing a pleasant tactile sensation, it appears like a tuft of cotton-down, like a wisp of raw cotton, or like a current of air.

But this is the judgment given in the commentaries: for some, it appears like a star, or a ball of gems, or a string of pearls; for some, having a rough touch, it appears like a cotton-seed, or a peg of heartwood; for some, it appears like a long, twisted thread, or a garland of flowers, or a wisp of smoke; for some, it appears spread out like a spider's web, or a layer of cloud, or a lotus flower, or a chariot-wheel, or the disc of the moon, or the disc of the sun. And this is so in the same way that, when several monks, having recited a discourse together, are sitting, and one monk asks, "in what form does this discourse appear to you," one says, "to me it appears like a great mountain river"; another, "to me, like a single line of forest"; another, "to me, like a single tree, cool-shaded, richly branched, laden with fruit" — for that same single discourse appears differently to them, owing to the difference in their own perception. Just so, this same single meditation subject appears differently, owing to the difference in perception. For this sign is born of perception, has perception as its source, has perception as its origin. Therefore it should be understood that it appears differently owing to the difference in perception.

And here, the consciousness having the in-breath as its object is one thing; that having the out-breath as its object is another; and that having the sign as its object is yet another. For one in whom these three states are not present, the meditation subject reaches neither absorption nor access. But for one in whom these three states are present, that very meditation subject reaches both access and absorption. For it is said —

"The sign, the in-breath, and the out-breath are not the object of a single consciousness; and, for one who does not know these three states, development is not obtained.
"The sign, the in-breath, and the out-breath are not the object of a single consciousness; and, for one who does indeed know these three states, development is obtained." (Pārājika Commentary 2.165)

Now, once the sign has appeared in this way, that monk should go to his teacher and report it, saying, "venerable sir, such and such appears to me." But the teacher should say neither "this is the sign" nor "this is not the sign." He should say, "it is so, friend; attend to it again and again." For, were he to say "this is the sign," the monk might come to a premature stop; and were he to say "this is not the sign," he might become discouraged and dejected. Therefore, without saying either of these, he should simply direct him to continue his attention. This, first, is the view of the reciters of the Long Discourses.

The reciters of the Middle Discourses, however, say that he should be told, "this, friend, is the sign; attend to this meditation subject again and again, good man." Then that monk should fix his mind precisely on the sign. In this way, from this point on, his development proceeds by way of fixing. For this the ancients said —

"Fixing the mind upon the sign, / discerning its various modes, / the wise one binds his own mind / to the in-breaths and out-breaths."

For one thus manifesting the sign, the hindrances are already suppressed, the defilements already settled, mindfulness already established, and the mind already concentrated by access concentration. He should then attend to that sign neither by way of its color, nor should he review it by way of its characteristic. Rather, just as a chief queen guards a would-be universal monarch still in the womb, or as a farmer guards a rice crop about to ripen, avoiding the seven unsuitable conditions concerning the dwelling and the rest, and cultivating the seven suitable ones, he should carefully guard it. Having thus guarded it, and, by way of repeated attention, brought it to growth and increase, he should bring the tenfold skill in absorption to completion, and apply the balance of energy. For one making effort thus, in that very sign, the fourfold and fivefold jhānas are produced, exactly in the order already stated for the earth kasiṇa.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The self-reproach method for a vanished sign is a striking piece of applied logic turned into direct address: rather than simply reassuring the meditator that the breath is still there, the passage instructs him to run through the specific, exhaustive list of conditions under which breath genuinely does not occur — in the womb, submerged, unconscious, dead, in fourth jhāna, in the immaterial attainments, or in cessation — and, having systematically ruled out every one of these as his own actual condition, conclude by elimination that the only remaining explanation is his own insufficient skill, not any real absence of breath.

The catalogue of ways the sign can appear — a star, gems, pearls, a cotton-seed, smoke, a spider's web, a lotus, a chariot-wheel, the sun or moon — closes with the "same discourse, different descriptions" simile, which makes a genuinely important epistemological point for this entire meditation tradition: the fact that different meditators report wildly different appearances for what is functionally the identical meditative sign is not evidence that some of them are practicing incorrectly, but simply reflects that the sign, "born of perception," is inevitably filtered through each individual's own perceptual habits, exactly as one discourse can honestly remind different listeners of a river, a forest line, or a single fruit-laden tree.

The two schools' disagreement over exactly how a teacher should respond when a student first reports the sign — never confirming or denying it (the Dīgha-reciters' caution against either premature satisfaction or discouragement) versus confirming it directly and telling the student to fix upon it (the Majjhima-reciters' more direct approach) — is presented without the chapter declaring a winner, a rare moment of genuinely unresolved methodological disagreement preserved openly within the text itself.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
saññaja / saññānidāna / saññāpabhava

born of perception / having perception as its source / having perception as its origin — three near-synonymous phrases explaining why the counterpart-sign appears so differently to different meditators, since it is fundamentally shaped by each individual's own perceptual habits.

dīghabhāṇaka / majjhimabhāṇaka

the reciters of the Long Discourses (Dīgha Nikāya) / the reciters of the Middle Discourses (Majjhima Nikāya) — two traditional lineages of canonical transmission, cited here as holding genuinely different views on how directly a teacher should confirm a student's reported meditative sign.

satirasmi / paññāpatoda

the rope of mindfulness / the goad of understanding — the two tools in the farmer-and-oxen simile, mindfulness binding attention to the natural point of contact and understanding driving the practice forward once the breath becomes manifest there.

From Absorption to Insight: The First Tetrad Completed

Having reached the fourfold and fivefold jhānas through mindfulness of breathing alone, the chapter shows how a meditator turns that very attainment into the foundation for insight — observing that the in-breaths and out-breaths themselves arise in dependence on the physical body and the mind, exactly as the sound of a blacksmith's bellows depends on both the bag and a man's effort — and then tracing, through direct experience of impermanence and the fading away of formations, the path all the way from the first stirrings of insight to full arahantship and the final review-knowledge that closes out an entire spiritual career.

Pali Source

Evaṃ nibbattacatukkapañcakajjhāno panettha bhikkhu sallakkhaṇāvivaṭṭanāvasena kammaṭṭhānaṃ vaḍḍhetvā pārisuddhiṃ pattukāmo tadeva jhānaṃ pañcahākārehi vasippattaṃ paguṇaṃ katvā nāmarūpaṃ vavatthapetvā vipassanaṃ paṭṭhapeti. Kathaṃ? So hi samāpattito vuṭṭhāya assāsapassāsānaṃ samudayo karajakāyo ca cittañcāti passati. Yathā hi kammāragaggariyā dhamamānāya bhastañca purisassa ca tajjaṃ vāyāmaṃ paṭicca vāto sañcarati, evameva kāyañca cittañca paṭicca assāsapassāsāti. Tato assāsapassāse ca kāyañca rūpanti cittañca taṃsampayuttadhamme ca arūpanti vavatthapeti. Ayamettha saṅkhepo. Vitthārato pana nāmarūpavavatthānaṃ parato āvibhavissati.

Evaṃ nāmarūpaṃ vavatthapetvā tassa paccayaṃ pariyesati. Pariyesanto ca naṃ disvā tīsupi addhāsu nāmarūpassa pavattiṃ ārabbha kaṅkhaṃ vitarati. Vitiṇṇakaṅkho kalāpasammasanavasena tilakkhaṇaṃ āropetvā udayabbayānupassanāya pubbabhāge uppanne obhāsādayo dasa vipassanupakkilese pahāya upakkilesavimuttaṃ paṭipadāñāṇaṃ maggoti vavatthapetvā udayaṃ pahāya bhaṅgānupassanaṃ patvā nirantaraṃ bhaṅgānupassanena vayato upaṭṭhitesu sabbasaṅkhāresu nibbindanto virajjanto vimuccanto yathākkamaṃ cattāro ariyamagge pāpuṇitvā arahattaphale patiṭṭhāya ekūnavīsatibhedassa paccavekkhaṇāñāṇassa pariyantaṃ patto sadevakassa lokassa aggadakkhiṇeyyo hoti.

Ettāvatā cassa gaṇanaṃ ādiṃ katvā vipassanāpariyosānā ānāpānassatisamādhibhāvanā samattā hotīti ayaṃ sabbākārato paṭhamacatukkavaṇṇanā.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, a monk who has thus produced the fourfold and fivefold jhānas, desiring, by way of observing and turning away, to develop this meditation subject further and reach purity, having made that very jhāna mastered and thoroughly familiar by the five kinds of mastery, determines mentality-materiality, and sets insight in motion. How? For, emerging from the attainment, he sees that the arising of the in-breaths and out-breaths depends on the physical body and the mind. Just as, when a blacksmith's bellows is being blown, wind moves in dependence on both the bag and a man's corresponding effort, so too the in-breaths and out-breaths occur in dependence on the body and the mind. Then he determines that the in-breaths and out-breaths, together with the body, are materiality, and that the mind, together with the states associated with it, are mentality. This, here, is the summary; in detail, however, the determination of mentality-materiality will become evident later.

Having thus determined mentality-materiality, he searches out its condition. And, searching it out and seeing it, he crosses beyond doubt concerning the occurrence of mentality-materiality across all three time-periods. Having crossed beyond doubt, applying the three characteristics by way of comprehending it as a group, and, in the earlier stage of the contemplation of rise and fall, having abandoned the ten corruptions of insight — such as illumination and the rest — he determines the knowledge of the path free from those corruptions as "the path"; then, abandoning attention to arising, and reaching the contemplation of dissolution, and, through the uninterrupted contemplation of dissolution, growing disenchanted, dispassionate, and liberated with regard to all formations that appear as passing away — he attains, in due order, the four noble paths, and, established in the fruit of arahantship, reaches the culmination of the nineteenfold knowledge of reviewing, becoming the foremost recipient of offerings for the world together with its gods.

By this much — taking counting as its beginning and insight as its conclusion — the development of concentration through mindfulness of breathing is complete. This is the commentary on the first tetrad in every respect.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The bellows simile does real conceptual work at the exact hinge-point of this meditation subject: it is the pivot from serenity practice (developing jhāna through the breath) to insight practice (seeing the breath's own dependent, conditioned nature). Just as the sound of a bellows requires both a physical bag and a person's deliberate effort — neither alone is sufficient — the breath itself is shown to require both a physical body and a mind, meaning that even the most refined, jhāna-supporting object a meditator has been cultivating this entire chapter is itself revealed, at this very moment, to be a compounded, conditioned phenomenon rather than anything ultimately stable.

The chapter's compressed trajectory here — from determining mentality-materiality, through searching out conditions, through the three characteristics, past ten named corruptions of insight, through the contemplation of rise and fall and then of pure dissolution, to the four paths, arahantship, and the nineteen kinds of reviewing knowledge — packs the entirety of the classical progress-of-insight scheme into a single paragraph, explicitly promising fuller treatment "later," in the treatise's dedicated chapters on insight still to come. This is the chapter's own internal cross-referencing discipline again: rather than repeating material that belongs elsewhere, it gives just enough to show how this specific meditation subject connects to that larger structure.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
kalāpasammasana

comprehension by way of groups — kalāpa (a bundle, a group) + sammasana (comprehending, examining); the technique of applying the three characteristics (impermanence, suffering, non-self) to compounded groups of phenomena rather than to isolated single moments.

dasa vipassanupakkilesa

the ten corruptions of insight — obhāsa (illumination) and nine other named experiences that can arise during early insight practice and, if mistaken for the path itself, actually derail genuine progress; named here but treated fully in the treatise's later insight chapters.

ekūnavīsatibheda paccavekkhaṇāñāṇa

the nineteenfold knowledge of reviewing — the final reflective survey (of the path, fruit, defilements abandoned, defilements remaining, and Nibbāna, across four stages) that an arahant undertakes upon completing the entire path, closing out the whole progression this section has just traced.

The Remaining Three Tetrads: Feeling, Mind, and Pure Insight

The chapter closes its treatment of the sixteen-step formula by working through the three tetrads not intended for a beginner but for one who has already attained jhāna: experiencing and calming joy and happiness as manifestations of feeling, experiencing, gladdening, concentrating, and liberating the mind itself, and finally the fourth tetrad's four pure insight contemplations — impermanence, fading away, cessation, and relinquishment — each shown to operate through both a direct, momentary experience gained in meditation and a subsequent examination of that same experience once it has passed.

Pali Source

Itaresu pana tīsu catukkesu yasmā visuṃ kammaṭṭhānabhāvanānayo nāma natthi. Tasmā anupadavaṇṇanānayeneva tesaṃ evaṃ attho veditabbo.

Pītipaṭisaṃvedīti pītiṃ paṭisaṃviditaṃ karonto pākaṭaṃ karonto assasissāmi passasissāmīti sikkhati. Tattha dvīhākārehi pīti paṭisaṃviditā hoti ārammaṇato ca asammohato ca. Kathaṃ ārammaṇato pīti paṭisaṃviditā hoti? Sappītike dve jhāne samāpajjati. Tassa samāpattikkhaṇe jhānapaṭilābhena ārammaṇato pīti paṭisaṃviditā hoti, ārammaṇassa paṭisaṃviditattā. Kathaṃ asammohato? Sappītike dve jhāne samāpajjitvā vuṭṭhāya jhānasampayuttaṃ pītiṃ khayato vayato sammasati. Tassa vipassanākkhaṇe lakkhaṇapaṭivedhena asammohato pīti paṭisaṃviditā hoti.

Idampanettha visesamattaṃ, tiṇṇaṃ jhānānaṃ vasena sukhapaṭisaṃveditā, catunnampi vasena cittasaṅkhārapaṭisaṃveditā veditabbā. Cittasaṅkhāroti vedanādayo dve khandhā. Passambhayaṃ cittasaṅkhāranti oḷārikaṃ oḷārikaṃ cittasaṅkhāraṃ passambhento, nirodhentoti attho.

Apicettha pītipade pītisīsena vedanā vuttā. Sukhapade sarūpeneva vedanā. Dvīsu cittasaṅkhārapadesu "saññā ca vedanā ca cetasikā ete dhammā cittapaṭibaddhā cittasaṅkhārā"ti (paṭi. ma. 1.174; ma. ni. 1.463) vacanato saññāsampayuttā vedanāti evaṃ vedanānupassanānayena idaṃ catukkaṃ bhāsitanti veditabbaṃ.

Tatiyacatukkepi catunnaṃ jhānānaṃ vasena cittapaṭisaṃveditā veditabbā. Abhippamodayaṃ cittanti cittaṃ modento pamodento hāsento pahāsento assasissāmi passasissāmīti sikkhati. Tattha dvīhākārehi abhippamodo hoti samādhivasena ca vipassanāvasena ca. Kathaṃ samādhivasena? Sappītike dve jhāne samāpajjati. So samāpattikkhaṇe sampayuttapītiyā cittaṃ āmodeti pamodeti. Kathaṃ vipassanāvasena? Sappītike dve jhāne samāpajjitvā vuṭṭhāya jhānasampayuttapītiṃ khayato vayato sammasati. Evaṃ vipassanākkhaṇe jhānasampayuttaṃ pītiṃ ārammaṇaṃ katvā cittaṃ āmodeti pamodeti.

Samādahaṃ cittanti paṭhamajjhānādivasena ārammaṇe cittaṃ samaṃ ādahanto samaṃ ṭhapento. Tāni vā pana jhānāni samāpajjitvā vuṭṭhāya jhānasampayuttaṃ cittaṃ khayato vayato sampassato vipassanākkhaṇe lakkhaṇapaṭivedhena uppajjati khaṇikacittekaggatā.

Vimocayaṃ cittanti paṭhamajjhānena nīvaraṇehi cittaṃ mocento vimocento, dutiyena vitakkavicārehi, tatiyena pītiyā, catutthena sukhadukkhehi cittaṃ mocento vimocento. Tāni vā pana jhānāni samāpajjitvā vuṭṭhāya jhānasampayuttaṃ cittaṃ khayato vayato sammasati. So vipassanākkhaṇe aniccānupassanāya niccasaññāto cittaṃ mocento, dukkhānupassanāya sukhasaññāto, anattānupassanāya attasaññāto, nibbidānupassanāya nandito, virāgānupassanāya rāgato, nirodhānupassanāya samudayato, paṭinissaggānupassanāya ādānato cittaṃ mocento assasati ceva passasati ca. Evaṃ cittānupassanāvasena idaṃ catukkaṃ bhāsitanti veditabbaṃ.

Catutthacatukke pana aniccānupassīti ettha tāva aniccaṃ veditabbaṃ. Aniccatā veditabbā. Aniccānupassanā veditabbā. Aniccānupassī veditabbo. Tattha aniccanti pañcakkhandhā. Kasmā? Uppādavayaññathattabhāvā. Aniccatāti tesaṃyeva uppādavayaññathattaṃ, hutvā abhāvo vā, nibbattānaṃ tenevākārena aṭṭhatvā khaṇabhaṅgena bhedoti attho. Aniccānupassanāti tassā aniccatāya vasena rūpādīsu aniccanti anupassanā. Aniccānupassīti tāya anupassanāya samannāgato.

Virāgānupassīti ettha pana dve virāgā khayavirāgo ca accantavirāgo ca. Tattha khayavirāgoti saṅkhārānaṃ khaṇabhaṅgo. Accantavirāgoti nibbānaṃ. Virāgānupassanāti tadubhayadassanavasena pavattā vipassanā ca maggo ca. Nirodhānupassīpadepi eseva nayo.

Paṭinissaggānupassīti etthāpi dve paṭinissaggā pariccāgapaṭinissaggo ca pakkhandanapaṭinissaggo ca. Vipassanā hi tadaṅgavasena saddhiṃ khandhābhisaṅkhārehi kilese pariccajati, saṅkhatadosadassanena ca tabbiparīte nibbāne tanninnatāya pakkhandatīti pariccāgapaṭinissaggo ceva pakkhandanapaṭinissaggoti ca vuccati. Maggo samucchedavasena saddhiṃ khandhābhisaṅkhārehi kilese pariccajati, ārammaṇakaraṇena ca nibbāne pakkhandatīti pariccāgapaṭinissaggo ceva pakkhandanapaṭinissaggoti ca vuccati.

Idaṃ catutthacatukkaṃ suddhavipassanāvaseneva vuttaṃ. Purimāni pana tīṇi samathavipassanāvasena. Evaṃ catunnaṃ catukkānaṃ vasena soḷasavatthukāya ānāpānassatiyā bhāvanā veditabbā. Evaṃ soḷasavatthuvasena ca pana ayaṃ ānāpānassati mahapphalā hoti mahānisaṃsā.

Ru-Yi Translation

Now, as for the remaining three tetrads, since there is no distinct meditation-subject method for their development, their meaning should be understood simply by way of a running commentary on each term.

"'Experiencing joy, I shall breathe in ... breathe out,' thus he trains" means: making joy experienced, making it evident, he trains thus. Herein, joy is experienced in two ways: by way of the object, and by way of non-confusion. How is joy experienced by way of the object? One attains the two jhānas that have joy. At the moment of that attainment, through the obtaining of jhāna, joy is experienced by way of the object, since the object is experienced. How, by way of non-confusion? Having attained the two jhānas that have joy, and emerged, one comprehends the joy conjoined with that jhāna as subject to destruction and to falling away. At the moment of that insight, through the penetration of its characteristic, joy is experienced without confusion.

This, herein, is the distinctive point: happiness is experienced in the same way, by way of the three jhānas; and the mental formation is experienced by way of all four jhānas. The "mental formation" is the two aggregates of feeling and perception. "'Calming the mental formation, I shall breathe in ... breathe out'" means: calming, causing to cease, the mental formation, gross by gross degrees.

Moreover, herein, in the term "joy," feeling is spoken of under the heading of joy; in the term "happiness," feeling is spoken of directly, in its own form. In the two terms concerning the mental formation, since it is said, "perception and feeling, being mental, are mental states bound up with the mind, and are mental formations" (Paṭis. 1.174; M.N. 1.463), it is feeling conjoined with perception that is meant. Thus it should be understood that this tetrad is spoken by way of the method of contemplating feeling.

In the third tetrad too, the mind is experienced by way of the four jhānas. "'Gladdening the mind, I shall breathe in ... breathe out,' thus he trains" means: gladdening, delighting, cheering, rejoicing the mind, he trains thus. Herein, gladdening occurs in two ways: by way of concentration, and by way of insight. How, by way of concentration? One attains the two jhānas that have joy. At the moment of that attainment, by means of the conjoined joy, one gladdens and delights the mind. How, by way of insight? Having attained the two jhānas that have joy, and emerged, one comprehends the joy conjoined with that jhāna as subject to destruction and to falling away. Thus, at the moment of insight, taking the jhāna-conjoined joy as its object, one gladdens and delights the mind.

"'Concentrating the mind'" means: by way of the first jhāna and the rest, evenly settling and evenly fixing the mind on its object. Or else: having attained those jhānas and emerged, and, contemplating the jhāna-conjoined mind as subject to destruction and to falling away, at the moment of insight, through the penetration of its characteristic, momentary one-pointedness of mind arises.

"'Liberating the mind'" means: by the first jhāna, freeing and liberating the mind from the hindrances; by the second, from applied and sustained thought; by the third, from joy; by the fourth, freeing and liberating the mind from pleasure and pain. Or else: having attained those jhānas and emerged, one comprehends the jhāna-conjoined mind as subject to destruction and to falling away. At the moment of insight, by the contemplation of impermanence, freeing the mind from the perception of permanence; by the contemplation of suffering, from the perception of pleasure; by the contemplation of non-self, from the perception of self; by the contemplation of disenchantment, from delight; by the contemplation of fading away, from lust; by the contemplation of cessation, from arising; by the contemplation of relinquishment, from grasping — freeing the mind in this way, one breathes in and breathes out. Thus it should be understood that this tetrad is spoken by way of the method of contemplating mind.

In the fourth tetrad, as to "contemplating impermanence" — herein, first, "the impermanent" should be understood; "impermanence" should be understood; "the contemplation of impermanence" should be understood; "one who contemplates impermanence" should be understood. Herein, "the impermanent" means the five aggregates. Why? Because of their nature of arising, passing away, and change. "Impermanence" means precisely that nature of arising, passing away, and change in them — that is, their having been and then ceasing to be, or their breaking up, moment by moment, without remaining even in that same mode once produced. "The contemplation of impermanence" means the contemplation, by way of that impermanence, of visible form and the rest as impermanent. "One who contemplates impermanence" means one possessed of that contemplation.

As for "contemplating fading away" — herein, there are two kinds of fading away: the fading away of destruction, and the absolute fading away. Herein, "the fading away of destruction" is the momentary dissolution of formations. "Absolute fading away" is Nibbāna. "The contemplation of fading away" is the insight and the path that occur by way of seeing both of these. The same method applies to "contemplating cessation" as well.

As for "contemplating relinquishment" — here too there are two kinds of relinquishment: relinquishment as giving up, and relinquishment as entering into. For insight, by way of the substitution of opposites, gives up the defilements together with the aggregates and their formations, and, through seeing the fault in the conditioned, enters into its opposite, Nibbāna, by inclining toward it — hence it is called both "relinquishment as giving up" and "relinquishment as entering into." The path, by way of complete cutting off, gives up the defilements together with the aggregates and their formations, and, by making Nibbāna its object, enters into it — hence it too is called both "relinquishment as giving up" and "relinquishment as entering into."

This fourth tetrad is spoken purely by way of insight alone. The preceding three, however, are by way of both serenity and insight. Thus, by way of these four tetrads, the development of mindfulness of breathing, with its sixteen bases, should be understood. And thus, by way of these sixteen bases, this mindfulness of breathing is of great fruit and great benefit.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

Each of the tetrads after the first is given an explicit double reading — "by way of concentration" and "by way of insight," or "by way of the object" and "by way of non-confusion" — showing that the same sixteen-step formula can be walked through twice, once as a description of what happens while actually absorbed in jhāna, and once as a description of what happens afterward, upon emerging and examining that very jhāna's own joy, happiness, and mental states as impermanent. The formula's words do not change between these two readings; only the sense in which "experiencing" and "calming" are meant does.

The final tetrad's twofold "relinquishment" — giving up defilements on one side, entering into Nibbāna on the other — makes explicit that these are not two separate acts but two descriptions of a single movement, the way releasing a burden and stepping forward unburdened are simply two ways of describing what happens in the same moment. The chapter applies this same double-description exactly, and identically, to both insight (which does this "by way of substitution," temporarily) and the path (which does it "by way of complete cutting off," permanently) — the difference between insight and the path, on this account, is one of thoroughness and permanence, not of kind.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
khayavirāga / accantavirāga

the fading away of destruction / absolute fading away — the twofold sense of "virāga" applied to the third tetrad's insight contemplations: one referring to formations' own momentary dissolution, the other to Nibbāna itself as the utterly unconditioned.

pariccāgapaṭinissagga / pakkhandanapaṭinissagga

relinquishment as giving up / relinquishment as entering into — the two aspects of a single act of "relinquishment," describing both what is released (defilements) and what is thereby entered (Nibbāna), applicable to both insight and the path.

khaṇikacittekaggatā

momentary one-pointedness of mind — khaṇika (momentary) + citta-ekaggatā (unification of mind); the specific, brief concentration that arises at the moment of insight itself, distinct from jhānic absorption but performing an analogous unifying function.

The Benefits, and an Elder Who Chose His Own Moment of Death

The chapter closes its treatment of mindfulness of breathing by naming three specific benefits beyond the merely general: its power to cut off distracting thought, its role as the very foundation from which the four establishments of mindfulness, the seven enlightenment factors, and finally liberating knowledge itself are all said to grow, and — most remarkably — its capacity to make even the final moments of life, the breaths immediately preceding death, "known" rather than slipping past unnoticed. The chapter closes with a vivid account of an elder who, having mastered this practice so completely that he could calculate his own remaining lifespan with precision, calmly announced the exact moment of his death to the assembled Saṅgha and then walked to that mark and died exactly as foretold.

Pali Source

Tatrassa "ayampi kho, bhikkhave, ānāpānassatisamādhi bhāvito bahulīkato santo ceva paṇīto cā"tiādivacanato santabhāvādivasenāpi mahānisaṃsatā veditabbā, vitakkupacchedasamatthatāyapi. Ayañhi santapaṇītaasecanakasukhavihārattā samādhiantarāyakarānaṃ vitakkānaṃ vasena ito cito ca cittassa vidhāvanaṃ vicchinditvā ānāpānārammaṇābhimukhameva cittaṃ karoti. Teneva vuttaṃ "ānāpānassati bhāvetabbā vitakkupacchedāyā"ti (a. ni. 9.1).

Vijjāvimuttipāripūriyā mūlabhāvenāpi cassā mahānisaṃsatā veditabbā. Vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā — "ānāpānassati, bhikkhave, bhāvitā bahulīkatā cattāro satipaṭṭhāne paripūreti, cattāro satipaṭṭhānā bhāvitā bahulīkatā satta bojjhaṅge paripūrenti, satta bojjhaṅgā bhāvitā bahulīkatā vijjāvimuttiṃ paripūrentī"ti (ma. ni. 3.147).

Apica carimakānaṃ assāsapassāsānaṃ viditabhāvakaraṇatopissā mahānisaṃsatā veditabbā. Vuttañhetaṃ bhagavatā — "evaṃ bhāvitāya kho, rāhula, ānāpānassatiyā evaṃ bahulīkatāya yepi te carimakā assāsapassāsā, tepi viditāva nirujjhanti, no aviditā"ti (ma. ni. 2.121).

Tattha nirodhavasena tayo carimakā bhavacarimakā, jhānacarimakā, cuticarimakāti. Bhavesu hi kāmabhave assāsapassāsā pavattanti, rūpārūpabhavesu nappavattanti, tasmā te bhavacarimakā. Jhānesu purime jhānattaye pavattanti, catutthe nappavattanti, tasmā te jhānacarimakā. Ye pana cuticittassa purato soḷasamena cittena saddhiṃ uppajjitvā cuticittena saha nirujjhanti, ime cuticarimakā nāma. Ime idha "carimakā"ti adhippetā.

Imaṃ kira kammaṭṭhānaṃ anuyuttassa bhikkhuno ānāpānārammaṇassa suṭṭhu pariggahitattā cuticittassa purato soḷasamassa cittassa uppādakkhaṇe uppādaṃ āvajjayato uppādopi nesaṃ pākaṭo hoti. Ṭhitiṃ āvajjayato ṭhitipi nesaṃ pākaṭā hoti. Bhaṅgaṃ āvajjayato ca bhaṅgo nesaṃ pākaṭo hoti.

Ito aññaṃ kammaṭṭhānaṃ bhāvetvā arahattaṃ pattassa bhikkhuno hi āyuantaraṃ paricchinnaṃ vā hoti aparicchinnaṃ vā. Idaṃ pana soḷasavatthukaṃ ānāpānassatiṃ bhāvetvā arahattaṃ pattassa āyuantaraṃ paricchinnameva hoti. So "ettakaṃ dāni me āyusaṅkhārā pavattissanti, na ito para"nti ñatvā attano dhammatāya eva sarīrapaṭijaggananivāsanapārupanādīni sabbakiccāni katvā akkhīni nimīleti koṭapabbatavihāravāsītissatthero viya mahākarañjiyavihāravāsīmahātissatthero viya devaputtamahāraṭṭhe piṇḍapātikatissatthero viya cittalapabbatavihāravāsino dve bhātiyattherā viya ca.

Tatridaṃ ekavatthuparidīpanaṃ. Dvebhātiyattherānaṃ kireko puṇṇamuposathadivase pātimokkhaṃ osāretvā bhikkhusaṅghaparivuto attano vasanaṭṭhānaṃ gantvā caṅkame ṭhito candālokaṃ oloketvā attano āyusaṅkhāre upadhāretvā bhikkhusaṅghamāha — "tumhehi kathaṃ parinibbāyantā bhikkhū diṭṭhapubbā"ti. Tatra keci āhaṃsu "amhehi āsane nisinnakāva parinibbāyantā diṭṭhapubbā"ti. Keci "amhehi ākāse pallaṅkamābhujitvā nisinnakā"ti. Thero āha — "ahaṃ dāni vo caṅkamantameva parinibbāyamānaṃ dassessāmī"ti tato caṅkame lekhaṃ katvā "ahaṃ ito caṅkamakoṭito parakoṭiṃ gantvā nivattamāno imaṃ lekhaṃ patvāva parinibbāyissāmī"ti vatvā caṅkamaṃ oruyha parabhāgaṃ gantvā nivattamāno ekena pādena lekhaṃ akkantakkhaṇeyeva parinibbāyi.

Tasmā have appamatto, anuyuñjetha paṇḍito;
Evaṃ anekānisaṃsaṃ, ānāpānassatiṃ sadāti.

Idaṃ ānāpānassatiyaṃ vitthārakathāmukhaṃ.

Ru-Yi Translation

Herein, from the statement, "this concentration through mindfulness of breathing too, monks, developed and made much of, is peaceful and sublime," its great benefit should be understood by way of its peaceful nature and the rest, and also by way of its capacity to cut off thought. For this, on account of its being a peaceful, sublime, unadulterated, pleasant dwelling, cuts off the mind's running about here and there under the sway of thoughts that create obstacles to concentration, and turns the mind exclusively toward the object of breathing. For this very reason it was said, "mindfulness of breathing should be developed for the cutting off of thought" (A.N. 9.1).

Its great benefit should also be understood by way of its being the root cause for the fulfillment of true knowledge and deliverance. For it was said by the Blessed One: "mindfulness of breathing, monks, developed and made much of, fulfills the four establishments of mindfulness; the four establishments of mindfulness, developed and made much of, fulfill the seven enlightenment factors; the seven enlightenment factors, developed and made much of, fulfill true knowledge and deliverance" (M.N. 3.147).

Moreover, its great benefit should be understood by way of its making even the final breaths known. For it was said by the Blessed One: "Rāhula, for one who has thus developed and thus made much of mindfulness of breathing, even those final in-breaths and out-breaths cease known, not unknown" (M.N. 2.121).

Herein, by way of cessation, there are three kinds of "final": final with respect to existence, final with respect to jhāna, and final with respect to the death-consciousness. For, among the realms of existence, in-breaths and out-breaths occur in the sense-sphere existence, but do not occur in the fine-material or immaterial existences — hence these are "final with respect to existence." Among the jhānas, they occur in the first three jhānas, but do not occur in the fourth — hence these are "final with respect to jhāna." And those which, arising together with the sixteenth consciousness before the death-consciousness, cease together with the death-consciousness — these are called "final with respect to the death-consciousness." It is these that are intended here as "the final ones."

It is told that, for a monk devoted to this meditation subject, because the object of breathing has been so thoroughly grasped, at the moment of the arising of that sixteenth consciousness before the death-consciousness, the arising itself becomes evident to him as he attends to it; the persistence, too, becomes evident as he attends to it; and the dissolution, too, becomes evident as he attends to it.

For a monk who has developed some other meditation subject and attained arahantship, the remaining span of his life may be either determined or undetermined. But for one who has developed this sixteen-based mindfulness of breathing and attained arahantship, the remaining span of his life is always determined. Knowing, "my vital formations will now continue only this much longer, no further," he performs, of his own natural accord, all his final duties — tending to his body, putting on and arranging his robes, and the like — and closes his eyes, as did the Elder Tissa, a dweller at Koṭapabbata monastery; the Elder Mahātissa, a dweller at Mahākarañjiya monastery; the Elder Piṇḍapātika Tissa, of Devaputta in the great province; and the two brother-elders, dwellers at Cittalapabbata monastery.

Here is an account illustrating one such matter. It is told that one of the two brother-elders, having recited the Pātimokkha on the full-moon uposatha day, went, surrounded by the community of monks, to his own dwelling-place, and, standing on the walking-path, looked at the moonlight, and, having ascertained his own vital formations, said to the community of monks: "how have you seen monks passing into final Nibbāna before?" Some among them said, "we have seen monks passing into final Nibbāna seated in their seats." Others said, "we have seen them seated cross-legged in the air." The elder said, "I shall now show you one passing into final Nibbāna in the very act of walking." He then made a mark on the walking-path and said, "having gone from this end of the walking-path to the far end, and turning back, I shall pass into final Nibbāna exactly upon reaching this mark." Descending onto the walking-path, he went to the far side, and, turning back, at the very moment his foot touched the mark, he passed into final Nibbāna.

Therefore let the wise one, heedful, / practice this mindfulness of breathing, / of such manifold benefit.

This is the opening of the detailed discussion on mindfulness of breathing.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The claim that this specific meditation subject, uniquely among all others, guarantees a "determined" remaining lifespan for an arahant who attains liberation through it is a striking assertion: elsewhere, even an arahant does not necessarily know in advance exactly how long he has left to live, but a monk who has thoroughly mastered mindfulness of breathing is said to gain this knowledge as a direct consequence of having so completely tracked the arising, persistence, and dissolution of every mental moment leading up to the final breath — the very skill developed for meditative purposes turns out to double as a form of precise self-knowledge about death's timing.

The elder's story is a quietly remarkable demonstration of this claim put into practice: rather than treating his own death as something to be feared or resisted, he treats it as a teaching opportunity, first surveying how his fellow monks have previously witnessed others' final moments (seated, or cross-legged in the air) before calmly announcing that he will demonstrate a third mode entirely — dying in the very act of ordinary walking, at a precisely predicted spot. The story's quiet, almost businesslike tone throughout — no drama, no final words beyond a simple prediction fulfilled exactly — embodies the very equanimity this whole meditation subject is meant to cultivate.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
bhavacarimaka / jhānacarimaka / cuticarimaka

final with respect to existence / to jhāna / to the death-consciousness — the threefold classification of what "final breaths" can mean, with only the third sense (the breaths ceasing together with the very last moment of consciousness) being the one intended in the canonical passage this section explains.

āyusaṅkhāra

the vital formations — already introduced in the earlier recollection-of-death section as one of the conditions sustaining life; here, an arahant perfected in mindfulness of breathing is said to be able to directly perceive their exact remaining duration.

caṅkama

a walking-path, a place for walking meditation — the specific setting of the closing story, chosen by the elder precisely to demonstrate a third possible manner of dying (in mid-motion) beyond the two his community had already witnessed.

Recollection of Peace: Nibbāna's Many Names

The chapter's tenth and final recollection turns to Nibbāna itself, called simply "the stilling of all suffering" — unpacked through a cluster of names each pointing to a different facet of the same single reality: the crushing of pride, the removal of thirst, the uprooting of every lingering attachment, the cutting-off of the round, and the ending of craving, alongside a further list of epithets — the unconditioned, the truth, the far shore, the deathless, the island, the shelter, the refuge — drawn from elsewhere in the canon. Because Nibbāna's qualities are only ever recollected, never directly experienced by an ordinary meditator, this recollection, like several others in the chapter, can be undertaken even by one who has not yet reached any noble attainment, since mere report of such qualities is already enough to settle and clarify the mind.

Pali Source

Ānāpānassatiyā anantaraṃ uddiṭṭhaṃ pana upasamānussatiṃ bhāvetukāmena rahogatena paṭisallīnena — "yāvatā, bhikkhave, dhammā saṅkhatā vā asaṅkhatā vā virāgo tesaṃ dhammānaṃ aggamakkhāyati, yadidaṃ madanimmadano pipāsavinayo ālayasamugghāto vaṭṭupacchedo taṇhakkhayo virāgo nirodho nibbāna"nti (a. ni. 4.34; itivu. 90) evaṃ sabbadukkhūpasamasaṅkhātassa nibbānassa guṇā anussaritabbā.

Tattha yāvatāti yattakā. Dhammāti sabhāvā. Saṅkhatā vā asaṅkhatā vāti saṅgamma samāgamma paccayehi katā vā akatā vā. Virāgo tesaṃ dhammānaṃ aggamakkhāyatīti tesaṃ saṅkhatāsaṅkhatadhammānaṃ virāgo aggamakkhāyati seṭṭho uttamoti vuccati. Tattha virāgoti na rāgābhāvamattameva, atha kho yadidaṃ madanimmadano…pe… nibbānanti yo so madanimmadanotiādīni nāmāni asaṅkhatadhammo labhati, so virāgoti paccetabbo. So hi yasmā tamāgamma sabbepi mānamadapurisamadādayo madā nimmadā amadā honti vinassanti, tasmā madanimmadanoti vuccati. Yasmā ca tamāgamma sabbāpi kāmapipāsā vinayaṃ abbhatthaṃ yāti, tasmā pipāsavinayoti vuccati. Yasmā pana tamāgamma pañcakāmaguṇālayā samugghātaṃ gacchanti, tasmā ālayasamugghātoti vuccati. Yasmā ca tamāgamma tebhūmakaṃ vaṭṭaṃ upacchijjati, tasmā vaṭṭupacchedoti vuccati. Yasmā pana tamāgamma sabbaso taṇhā khayaṃ gacchati virajjati nirujjhati ca, tasmā taṇhakkhayo virāgo nirodhoti vuccati. Yasmā panesa catasso yoniyo pañca gatiyo satta viññāṇaṭṭhitiyo nava ca sattāvāse aparāparabhāvāya vinanato ābandhanato saṃsibbanato vānanti laddhavohārāya taṇhāya nikkhanto nissaṭo visaṃyutto, tasmā nibbānanti vuccatīti.

Evametesaṃ madanimmadanatādīnaṃ guṇānaṃ vasena nibbānasaṅkhāto upasamo anussaritabbo. Ye vā panaññepi bhagavatā — "asaṅkhatañca vo, bhikkhave, desessāmi… saccañca… pārañca… sududdasañca… ajarañca… dhuvañca… nippapañcañca… amatañca… sivañca… khemañca… abbhutañca… anītikañca… abyābajjhañca… visuddhiñca… dīpañca… tāṇañca… leṇañca vo, bhikkhave, desessāmī"tiādīsu (saṃ. ni. 4.366) suttesu upasamaguṇā vuttā, tesampi vasena anussaritabboyeva.

Tassevaṃ madanimmadanatādiguṇavasena upasamaṃ anussarato neva tasmiṃ samaye rāgapariyuṭṭhitaṃ cittaṃ hoti, na dosa… na mohapariyuṭṭhitaṃ cittaṃ hoti. Ujugatamevassa tasmiṃ samaye cittaṃ hoti upasamaṃ ārabbhāti buddhānussatiādīsu vuttanayeneva vikkhambhitanīvaraṇassa ekakkhaṇe jhānaṅgāni uppajjanti. Upasamaguṇānaṃ pana gambhīratāya nānappakāraguṇānussaraṇādhimuttatāya vā appanaṃ appatvā upacārappattameva jhānaṃ hoti. Tadetamupasamaguṇānussaraṇavasena upasamānussaticceva saṅkhyaṃ gacchati.

Cha anussatiyo viya ca ayampi ariyasāvakasseva ijjhati, evaṃ santepi upasamagarukena puthujjanenāpi manasi kātabbā. Sutavasenāpi hi upasame cittaṃ pasīdati.
