Imañca pana upasamānussatiṃ anuyutto bhikkhu sukhaṃ supati, sukhaṃ paṭibujjhati, santindriyo hoti santamānaso hirottappasamannāgato pāsādiko paṇītādhimuttiko sabrahmacārīnaṃ garu ca bhāvanīyo ca. Uttari appaṭivijjhanto pana sugatiparāyano hoti.

Tasmā have appamatto, bhāvayetha vicakkhaṇo;
Evaṃ anekānisaṃsaṃ, ariye upasame satinti.

Idaṃ upasamānussatiyaṃ vitthārakathāmukhaṃ.

Iti sādhujanapāmojjatthāya kate visuddhimagge
Samādhibhāvanādhikāre
Anussatikammaṭṭhānaniddeso nāma
Aṭṭhamo paricchedo.

Ru-Yi Translation

One desiring to develop recollection of peace, taught next after mindfulness of breathing, alone in a secluded place, withdrawn, should recollect the qualities of Nibbāna, reckoned as the stilling of all suffering, thus: "whatever states there are, monks, conditioned or unconditioned, fading away is declared to be the foremost of those states — that is to say, the crushing of pride, the removal of thirst, the uprooting of attachment, the cutting-off of the round, the destruction of craving, fading away, cessation, Nibbāna" (A.N. 4.34; Iti. 90).

Herein, "whatever" means: as many as there are. "States" means: their own natures. "Conditioned or unconditioned" means: made or unmade, through the coming together and convergence of conditions. "Fading away is declared to be the foremost of those states" means: fading away is declared, spoken of, as the foremost, the best, the supreme, among those conditioned and unconditioned states. Herein, "fading away" does not mean the mere absence of lust; rather, it should be understood that the very unconditioned state that receives such names as "the crushing of pride" and the rest — that same state, that is to say, Nibbāna — is to be identified as "fading away." For, because, in dependence on it, all forms of pride — the pride of conceit, the pride of virility, and the rest — become without pride, pride-free, and are destroyed, it is called "the crushing of pride." And because, in dependence on it, all thirst for sensual pleasure goes to its removal, is utterly done away with, it is called "the removal of thirst." And because, in dependence on it, attachment to the five strands of sensual pleasure is uprooted, it is called "the uprooting of attachment." And because, in dependence on it, the round belonging to the three planes of existence is cut off, it is called "the cutting-off of the round." And because, in dependence on it, craving is utterly destroyed, fades away, and ceases, it is called "the destruction of craving, fading away, cessation." And because this state has departed from, gone forth from, and become disjoined from craving — craving which, by weaving, binding, and stitching together the four modes of generation, the five destinies, the seven stations of consciousness, and the nine abodes of beings toward further and further becoming, has received the name "vāna" (weaving) — it is therefore called "Nibbāna" (the departure from weaving).

Thus, by way of these qualities such as "the crushing of pride" and the rest, peace, reckoned as Nibbāna, should be recollected. Or else, whatever other qualities of peace are stated by the Blessed One, in such discourses as — "I shall teach you, monks, the unconditioned ... and the truth ... and the far shore ... and the extremely hard to see ... and the unaging ... and the stable ... and the undiversified ... and the deathless ... and the auspicious ... and the secure ... and the marvelous ... and the unafflicted ... and that which is not oppressive ... and purity ... and the island ... and the shelter ... and the refuge, monks, shall I teach you" (S.N. 4.366) — by way of these too, it should indeed be recollected.

For one recollecting peace in this way, by way of such qualities as "the crushing of pride" and the rest, his mind, at that time, is possessed neither by greed, nor by hatred, nor by delusion. His mind at that time is simply straight, with peace as its object — and, by the same method already stated in the sections on recollection of the Buddha and the rest, the jhāna factors arise within a single moment, his hindrances suppressed. But, owing to the profundity of the qualities of peace, or owing to his mind's being devoted to recollecting their many different kinds, the jhāna that results does not reach absorption, but only reaches access. And this, because it has arisen by way of recollecting the qualities of peace, is reckoned simply as recollection of peace.

And, just as the six recollections already discussed, this one too succeeds only for a noble disciple; nevertheless, even an ordinary person who holds peace in high regard should attend to it. For even by way of mere report, the mind becomes settled and clear with regard to peace. And a monk devoted to this recollection of peace sleeps well and wakes well; he becomes one of peaceful faculties and a peaceful mind, possessed of moral shame and moral dread, endearing, of sublime inclination, respected and esteemed by his fellows in the holy life. And, even if he penetrates no further, he becomes one bound for a happy destination.

Therefore let the discerning one, heedful, / develop this recollection, / of such manifold benefit, / of the noble peace.

This is the opening of the detailed discussion on recollection of peace.

Thus, in the Visuddhimagga, composed for the gladdening of good people, in the section on the development of concentration, this is the eighth chapter, called the Description of the Meditation Subjects Consisting in Recollection.

Reading note — Ru-Yi

The etymology of "Nibbāna" itself, closing this section's opening analysis, is the chapter's own culminating wordplay: craving is personified as a weaver, its threads (vāna) binding beings again and again into the four modes of birth, five destinies, seven stations of consciousness, and nine abodes — an intricate, self-perpetuating fabric of further existence. "Nibbāna" is then read as the precise negation of this weaving: departure from it, disjunction from it, an unravelling rather than a further binding. This single etymology compresses an entire cosmological picture of continued rebirth into one grammatical move.

That this recollection, like several others in the chapter, is explicitly said to work "only for a noble disciple" and yet is immediately recommended to "even an ordinary person who holds peace in high regard" reprises a pattern already established for recollection of the Buddha: technical precision about who can attain the full, jhāna-approaching version of a practice does not stop the chapter from insisting that partial, hearsay-based benefit remains genuinely available to anyone, on the grounds that "even by way of mere report, the mind becomes settled and clear" — closing this exceptionally long and technical chapter on a notably inclusive, encouraging note.

Key vocabulary — for Pāli learners
vāna / nibbāna

weaving / the departure from weaving — the etymology at the heart of this section: craving personified as a weaver binding beings to further existence, with Nibbāna read literally as escape from that binding.

ālayasamugghāta

the uprooting of attachment — ālaya (a resting place, attachment) + samugghāta (uprooting, eradication); one of the five named epithets for Nibbāna in the core citation, specifically referring to attachment to the five strands of sensual pleasure.

dīpa / tāṇa / leṇa

the island / the shelter / the refuge — three of the many further epithets for Nibbāna drawn from the Saṃyutta Nikāya, each evoking a different image of safety (an island amid a flood, a shelter from danger, a place of refuge) for the same single unconditioned reality.

Anussatikammaṭṭhānaniddesa Complete

This closes the translation of the Visuddhimagga's eighth chapter, Anussatikammaṭṭhānaniddesa ("The Description of the Meditation Subjects Consisting in Recollection"), from the Pāli — by a wide margin the longest chapter completed in this project so far. In full: recollection of death, through its full eightfold analysis (the executioner standing ready, the ruin of success, comparison with figures of great fame, merit, strength, psychic power, and wisdom, a body shared by eighty families of worms, the fragility of a life bound to five conditions, death's total unpredictability, the brevity of a hundred-year span, and the momentariness of a single conscious instant); mindfulness of the body, with its sevenfold and tenfold skills in learning and attention (the hunted monkey, the almsround monk), and the complete thirty-two-part contemplation of the body's repulsiveness, part by part, in full anatomical and analytical detail, closing with the flower-garland simile and the Elder Mallaka's thirty-two separate absorptions; mindfulness of breathing, the treatise's most extensive single meditation-subject exposition after the earth kasiṇa itself, covering the full sixteen-step canonical formula, the wild-calf and leopard similes for forest dwelling, the precise technical account of long and short breathing, the five bonds and eight-step method of counting, following, touching, and fixing (with the lame-man, gatekeeper, and saw similes), the sign's many forms and the farmer-and-oxen simile, the full path from access concentration through the four jhānas to insight and arahantship, and the story of the elder who predicted the exact moment of his own death while walking; and finally recollection of peace, unpacking Nibbāna's many names through the etymology of "vāna" itself — closing, like the seven chapters before it, with the traditional colophon. The Visuddhimagga's ninth chapter, treating the divine abidings of loving-kindness, compassion, sympathetic joy, and equanimity, continues from here — the next undertaking for this translation.
